# C A N N E U R A L N E T W O R K S AC H I E V E O P T I - M A L C O M P U TAT I O N A L - S TAT I S T I C A L T R A D E - O F F ? A N A N A LY S I S O N S I N G L E - I N D E X M O D E L

Siyu Chen∗<sup>1</sup> , Beining Wu∗<sup>2</sup> , Miao Lu<sup>3</sup> , Zhuoran Yang<sup>1</sup> , Tianhao Wang<sup>4</sup>

<sup>1</sup>Yale University, <sup>2</sup>University of Chicago, <sup>3</sup>Stanford University, <sup>4</sup>Toyota Technological Institute at Chicago {siyu.chen.sc3226, zhuoran.yang}@yale.edu, beiningw@uchicago.edu miaolu@stanford.edu, tianhao.wang@ttic.edu

## A B S T R A C T

In this work, we tackle the following question: Can neural networks trained with gradient-based methods achieve the optimal statistical-computational tradeoff in learning Gaussian single-index models? Prior research has shown that any polynomial-time algorithm under the statistical query (SQ) framework requires Ω(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup>∨d) samples, where s ⋆ is the generative exponent representing the intrinsic difficulty of learning the underlying model. However, it remains unknown whether neural networks can achieve this sample complexity. Inspired by prior techniques such as label transformation and landscape smoothing for learning single-index models, we propose a unified gradient-based algorithm for training a two-layer neural network in polynomial time. Our method is adaptable to a variety of loss and activation functions, covering a broad class of existing approaches. We show that our algorithm learns a feature representation that strongly aligns with the unknown signal θ ⋆ , with sample complexity Or(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> ∨ d), matching the SQ lower bound up to a polylogarithmic factor for all generative exponents s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1. Furthermore, we extend our approach to the setting where θ ⋆ is k-sparse for k = o( √ d) by introducing a novel weight perturbation technique that leverages the sparsity structure. We derive a corresponding SQ lower bound of order Ω( <sup>r</sup> k s ⋆ ), matched by our method up to a polylogarithmic factor. Our framework, especially the weight perturbation technique, is of independent interest, and suggests potential gradient-based solutions to other problems such as sparse tensor PCA.

## 1 I N T R O D U C T I O N

The success of neural networks is largely attributed to their remarkable ability to learn rich and useful features from data during gradient-based training [\(Girshick et al.,](#page-11-0) [2014\)](#page-11-0). This feature-learning capability allows them to outperform traditional methods like kernel-based approaches, which rely on predefined features [\(Allen-Zhu & Li,](#page-10-0) [2019;](#page-10-0) [Ghorbani et al.,](#page-11-1) [2019;](#page-11-1) [Refinetti et al.,](#page-12-0) [2021\)](#page-12-0). However, when trained using (stochastic) gradient descent, neural networks can sometimes fall into a "kernel regime", where their behavior resembles that of a fixed kernel method, constrained by their random initialization [\(Jacot et al.,](#page-12-1) [2018;](#page-12-1) [Chizat et al.,](#page-10-1) [2019\)](#page-10-1). In this regime, the ability of the network to learn complex representations is severely limited, undermining the primary advantage of deep learning. Therefore, it is crucial to understand when and how neural networks trained with gradient-based method can perform effective feature learning to unlock their full potential, particularly in scenarios where a balance between computational efficiency and statistical performance is essential.

In this work, we approach this question in the context of Gaussian single-index models, a canonical class of problems in statistics and learning [\(MacCullagh & Nelder,](#page-12-2) [1989;](#page-12-2) [Ichimura,](#page-12-3) [1993;](#page-12-3) [Hristache](#page-11-2) [et al.,](#page-11-2) [2001;](#page-11-2) [Hardle et al.](#page-11-3) ¨ , [2004\)](#page-11-3). The model is defined as follows: for covariates z ∼ N (0, Id), the output y depends on the inner product ⟨θ ⋆ , z⟩ with an unknown signal θ <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>R</sup> d through a link distribution p, i.e., y ∼ p(· | ⟨θ ⋆ , z⟩). The goal is to recover θ <sup>⋆</sup> using i.i.d. samples (z1, y1), . . . ,(zn, yn) generated by the underlying model. While n = Ω(d) samples suffice to recover θ ⋆ information-theoretically

![](_page_1_Figure_1.jpeg)

Figure 1: (a) The contour plots of (log d, log n, acc(d, n)) for [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) under model y = ⟨z, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ 2 exp(−⟨z, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ 2 ), which has generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> = 4 [\(Example 2.2\)](#page-2-0). Here acc(d, n) is the average of the largest 8 values of the alignment between the neuron weights and the unknown signal θ ⋆ . The slopes of these contour lines are all close to 2, indicating a sample complexity n ≈ d 2 for s <sup>⋆</sup> = 4. (b) The paradigm of sample complexity achieved by our algorithm for different generative exponent s ⋆ and sparsity level k, illustrating the success of achiving computational-statistical tradeoff.

[\(Bach,](#page-10-2) [2017;](#page-10-2) [Damian et al.,](#page-11-4) [2024\)](#page-11-4), achieving this efficiently is difficult for polynomial-time algorithms, where the required sample size also depends on properties of the link distribution p, creating a computational-statistical gap. For example, when y is a polynomial of ⟨θ ⋆ , z⟩, it has been shown that two-layer neural networks with square loss need d Θ(q ⋆ samples [\(Arous et al.,](#page-10-3) [2021;](#page-10-3) [Bietti et al.,](#page-10-4) [2022;](#page-10-4) [Damian et al.,](#page-11-5) [2023\)](#page-11-5), where q ⋆ is the information exponent of the polynomial link function [\(Arous et al.,](#page-10-3) [2021;](#page-10-3) [Dudeja & Hsu,](#page-11-6) [2018\)](#page-11-6). Such sample complexity is indeed inevitable under the correlational statistical query (CSQ) framework, leading to a computational-statistical gap for q <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2.

However, the CSQ framework does not capture the fundamental limits of all gradient-based algorithms. Recent works have shown that by leveraging higher-order terms in the gradient, neural networks can learn polynomials with as few as Or(d) samples [\(Lee et al.,](#page-12-4) [2024;](#page-12-4) [Arnaboldi et al.,](#page-10-5) [2024\)](#page-10-5). It turns out that the intrinsic learning difficulty is captured by another quantity called the *generative exponent* s ⋆ , which is at most 2 for polynomial link functions, and the corresponding SQ lower bound on the sample complexity is n = Ω(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) [1](#page-1-0) [\(Damian et al.,](#page-11-4) [2024\)](#page-11-4). Thus, there is no computational-statistical gap up to poly-log factors for learning polynomial single-index models. However, for general single-index models with s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 3, no gradient-based algorithm for neural networks has been shown to match the SQ lower bound, leaving it an open problem [\(Arnaboldi et al.,](#page-10-5) [2024;](#page-10-5) [Lee et al.,](#page-12-4) [2024\)](#page-12-4).

Furthermore, learning the Gaussian single-index model can benefit from additional structures in the signal θ ⋆ , such as sparsity, which can significantly reduce the sample complexity compared to those depending on the ambient dimension d [\(Candes et al.](#page-10-6) ` , [2006;](#page-10-6) [Donoho et al.,](#page-11-7) [2009;](#page-11-7) [Raskutti et al.,](#page-12-5) [2012\)](#page-12-5). Recent work by [Vural & Erdogdu](#page-12-6) [\(2024\)](#page-12-6) examines the effectiveness of pruning in learning sparse features, demonstrating that it matches the correlated statistical query (CSQ) lower bound d q ⋆ for k ≪ √ d. However, the method fails to achieve the CSQ lower bound in non-sparse settings. For sparse single-index models with information exponent q <sup>⋆</sup> = 1, gradient descent on diagonal linear networks nearly achieves the information-theoretic lower bound thanks to its implicit regularization effect [\(Fan et al.,](#page-11-8) [2023\)](#page-11-8). Nonetheless, how to achieve the optimal sample complexity for general s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1 is also unknown under the sparse setting.

Contributions. Towards characterizing the fundamental feature learning capability of neural networks in the Gaussian single-index model, our main contributions are as follows:

- 1. We propose a unified recipe of gradient-based algorithms for training a two-layer neural network to learn the Gaussian single-index model. Our method integrates a general gradient oracle with a weight perturbation technique, carefully designed to exploit the underlying structure of the

<sup>1</sup>This Ω(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) sample complexity lower bound is essentially for the detection problem. [Dudeja & Hsu](#page-11-9) [\(2021\)](#page-11-9) shows that there is an estimation-detection gap for tensor PCA under the SQ framework, though it is unclear whether such gap exists universally. Throughout the paper, we always refer to the SQ lower bound as the detection lower bound, since detection in general is assumed to be easier than estimation.

Gaussian single-index model. This allows the neural network to perform feature learning of the unknown signal θ ⋆ in a computationally efficient manner. Our framework encompasses many existing approaches as special cases, such as batch reusing [\(Dandi et al.,](#page-11-10) [2024;](#page-11-10) [Lee et al.,](#page-12-4) [2024\)](#page-12-4), label transformation [\(Chen & Meka,](#page-10-7) [2020\)](#page-10-7), and landscape smoothing [\(Damian et al.,](#page-11-5) [2023\)](#page-11-5).

- 2. We show that for an *unknown link distribution* p with *any* generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1, the weights of the neural network achieve strong recovery of the true signal θ ⋆ after training by our algorithm using Or(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> ∨d) samples and polynomial running time. Our method achieves the SQ lower bound up to a polylogarithmic factor, and is the first gradient-based algorithm for training two-layer neural networks that attains the nearly optimal computational-statistical tradeoff for Gaussian single-index models with any s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1. [Figure 1](#page-1-1) (a) illustrates an example for s <sup>⋆</sup> = 4.
- 3. Furthermore, our method is able to take advantage of additional structural information of the true signal θ ⋆ . Specifically, we consider the case where θ ⋆ is k-sparse for k = o( √ d), and develop a *novel weight perturbation procedure* tailored to the sparsity of θ ⋆ . Equipped with this, we show that the weights of the neural network can achieve strong recovery of the sparse signal θ ⋆ after training with Or(k s ⋆ ) samples in polynomial time for any generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1. This sample complexity is also nearly optimal according to the sample complexity lower bound we establish for SQ algorithms, which might be of independent interest. Also, our method suggests a new approach to achieve the computational-statistical tradeoff for sparse tensor PCA.

In summary, our work provides a unified framework for training neural networks that can achieve the nearly optimal computational-statistical tradeoff for the Gaussian single-index model with any generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1. Our method not only tackles the intrinsic difficulty of learning the underlying model posed by the link distribution p, but also leverages the additional structural information of the true singal θ ⋆ that benefits the learning process. Integrating these results, our method attains nearly optimal balance between computational efficienty and statistical performance across almost all regimes of sparsity levels and generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1, as illustrated in [Figure 1](#page-1-1) (b).

#### 2 P R O B L E M S E T U P

We begin by introducing the notation used in the paper, and then describe the problem setup. For a probability distribution P, we denote by L 2 (P) the space of square-integrable functions with respect to <sup>P</sup>, and <sup>L</sup> (P) = means equality in L 2 (P). We denote the normalized probabilist's Hermite polynomials by {hs(·)}s≥0, where each hs(x):= (−1)<sup>s</sup> √ s! · e x <sup>2</sup>/2 · s <sup>d</sup>x<sup>s</sup> e −x <sup>2</sup>/2 . These polynomials form an orthonormal basis for L 2 (N (0, 1)), i.e., the space of square-integrable functions under the Gaussian measure.

Gaussian single-index model. We study the following Gaussian single-index model: The environment first samples an unobservable signal θ <sup>⋆</sup> ∼ π from some known prior π ∈ P(<sup>S</sup> d−1 ). Then i.i.d. data (z1, y1), . . . ,(zn, yn) ∈ <sup>R</sup> <sup>d</sup> × <sup>R</sup> are generated according to the following distribution <sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> given θ ⋆ :

$$\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*} : z \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I_d), \quad y \sim p(\cdot | \langle \theta^*, z \rangle). \quad (2.1)$$

Here p(· | ·) : <sup>R</sup> 7→ P(R) is referred to as the *link distribution*. A canonical example is the additive model where y = ϕ(⟨θ ⋆ , z⟩) + ϵ for some deterministic link function ϕ : <sup>R</sup> → <sup>R</sup> and random noise ϵ. See [Damian et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4) for more complicated examples.

Generative exponent. The following discussion on the generative exponent is based on the work of [Damian et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4). We aim to learn [\(2.1\)](#page-2-1) where the link distribution p has *generative exponent* s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1 , a measure of the computational-statistical gap for learning single-index models. We let x = ⟨θ ⋆ , z⟩. Notice that <sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y, z) = <sup>P</sup>(y, x) · N (z <sup>⊥</sup>; 0, Id−1) where we use <sup>P</sup> to denote the joint distribution of (x, y) as this joint distribution is independent of θ ⋆ . As the marginal distribution of y is also independent of θ ⋆ , we define the *null distribution* Q(y, z):= N (z; 0, Id) ⊗ Q(y) and denote Q(y, x):= N (x; 0, 1) ⊗ Q(y) where Q(y) = R <sup>P</sup>(y, x)dx. Under a square-integrable condition under Q, the likelihood ratio admits a Hermite expansion with coefficient functions {ζs(y)}s≥1, i.e.,

$$\frac{\mathbb{P}_{(y,x)}^{(y,x)}}{\mathbb{P}(y,x)} = \frac{\mathbb{P}(y,x)}{\mathbb{P}(y,x)} \stackrel{L=Q}{=} \sum_{s=0}^{\infty} \zeta_s(y) \cdot h_s(x), \quad \text{where } \zeta_s(y) = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h_s(x)|y], \quad (2.2)$$

and <sup>E</sup>Q[ζs(y) 2 ] ≤ 1 for all s ≥ 1. Note that [\(2.2\)](#page-2-2) makes sense only when we are working with the inner product of <sup>P</sup>/Q and a square-integrable function under the null distribution Q.

Definition 2.1 (Generative exponent). *For the Gaussian single-index model defined in* [\(2.1\)](#page-2-1)*, the generative exponent* s <sup>⋆</sup> *of the link distribution* p *is defined as* s ⋆ (p):= min{s ≥ 1 : <sup>E</sup>Q[ζs(y) 2 ] > 0}*.*

Example 2.2 (Example 2.7, [Damian et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4)). *Consider the special case of the Gaussian singleindex model* [\(2.1\)](#page-2-1) *where* y = ϕ(⟨θ ⋆ , z⟩) *for a deterministic link function* ϕ : <sup>R</sup> → <sup>R</sup>*. When* ϕ *is a polynomial function, it holds that* s ⋆ (ϕ) ≤ 2*, and the equality holds if and only if* f *is an even polynomial. In particular,* s ⋆ (hs) = 1 *for odd* s *and* s ⋆ (hs) = 2 *for even* s*. While for the example of* ϕ(x) = x 2 exp(−x 2 )*, which is not a polynomial, it has generative exponent* s ⋆ (ϕ) = 4*.*

Two-layer neural networks. We consider using a two-layer neural network with M hidden neurons to learn the single-index model [\(2.1\)](#page-2-1). The weight vector for each neuron m ∈ [M] is θ<sup>m</sup> ∈ <sup>R</sup> d , and the weights of the second layer are a1, . . . , a<sup>M</sup> ∈ <sup>R</sup>. We collect all the weights and denote θ = (θ1, . . . , θM) ∈ <sup>R</sup> <sup>d</sup>×<sup>M</sup>, a = (a1, . . . , aM) <sup>⊤</sup> ∈ <sup>R</sup><sup>M</sup>. Now for any input z ∈ <sup>R</sup> d , the output of the network is given by f(z; θ, a):= P<sup>M</sup> <sup>m</sup>=1 a<sup>m</sup> · σ(⟨z, θm⟩), where σ : <sup>R</sup> → <sup>R</sup> is the activation.

## 3 OV E RV I E W O F T E C H N I Q U E S

In this work, we apply gradient-based methods to learn Gaussian single-index models, with a focus on feature learning in neural networks and the corresponding computational-statistical tradeoff. To motivate the techniques involved, we begin by discussing an illustrative example that highlights such tradeoffs. For this overview, we focus on s <sup>⋆</sup> > 2 and the uniform prior π = Unif(<sup>S</sup> d−1 ). It has been shown that a gap exists between the information-theoretic lower bound Ω(d) and the SQ lower bound Ω(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) under this setting when s <sup>⋆</sup> > 2 [\(Bach,](#page-10-2) [2017;](#page-10-2) [Damian et al.,](#page-11-4) [2024\)](#page-11-4).

For illustration, let us consider training a two-layer network with a single neuron under the population square loss. When the weight of the second layer is small, the rescaled negative gradient g satisfies

$$g = -(2a)^{-1}\nabla_{\theta}(f(z;\theta,a)-y)^2 = -(a \cdot \sigma(\langle z, \theta \rangle) - y) \cdot \sigma'(\langle z, \theta \rangle) \cdot z = (y\sigma'(\langle z, \theta \rangle) + \text{err}) \cdot z,$$

where we take yσ′ (⟨z, θ⟩) as the signal term and treat −aσ(⟨z, θ⟩)σ ′ (⟨z, θ⟩) as the error term since it scales with a. [<sup>2</sup>](#page-3-0) We ignore the error term in the following discussion. Taking expectation over (z, y) ∼ <sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> and using the likelihood ratio decomposition in [\(2.2\)](#page-2-2), we have

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [g] \approx \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [y] \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\sigma'(\langle z, \theta \rangle) \cdot z]}_{\text{bias}} + \sum_{s \geq s^*} \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [y\zeta_s(y)] \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle z, \theta \rangle) \cdot z]}_{\text{informative queries}}, \quad (3.1)$$

where we use the fact that y and z are independent under the null distribution Q. Note that the *bias* term does not contain any information about θ ⋆ , and it can be easily removed by a debiasing procedure, so we assume for simplicity that <sup>E</sup>[y] = 0.

Failure of vanilla online minibatch SGD. We first consider the vanilla online minibatch SGD, which updates the weight vector θ by θ ← θ − η P<sup>n</sup> <sup>i</sup>=1 g<sup>i</sup> for a minibatch of size n. The sample complexity of gradient-based methods is determined by the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of the onesample gradient, which in our case is defined as SNR := <sup>E</sup>[⟨g, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩] <sup>2</sup>/E[∥g∥ 2 2 ]. This is the square of the alignment between g and θ ⋆ , governed primarily by the informative query corresponding to the lowest degree s ⋆ in [\(3.1\)](#page-3-1) assuming that <sup>E</sup>Q[yζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y)] ̸= 0. It can be shown that the inner product between the lowest-degree informative query in [\(3.1\)](#page-3-1) and the signal θ ⋆ satisfies (see [Lemma H.1\)](#page-66-0)

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [h_{s^*}(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle z, \theta \rangle) \cdot \langle z, \theta^* \rangle] \approx s^* \cdot \hat{\sigma}_{s^*} \cdot \langle \theta^*, \theta \rangle^{s^*-1} = \hat{\sigma}_{s^*} \cdot O(d^{-(s^*-1)/2}), \quad (3.2)$$

where σp<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> is the s ⋆ -th coefficient in the Hermite expansion of σ. While for ∥g∥2, we have

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\|g\|_2^2] \approx d \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [y^2 \sigma'(\langle z, \theta \rangle)^2] = \Omega(d),$$

where the high-order terms in the likelihood ratio decomposition are ignored and we come back to this point later. Now we can argue why vanilla online minibatch SGD has difficulty achieving the SQ lower bound for generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> > 2: Suppose <sup>E</sup>Q[yζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y)] and σp<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> are both nonzero constants. Then the one-sample SNR is O(d −s ⋆ ). For a minibatch with n samples, the SNR of the gradient averaged over the minibatch is roughly n times the one-sample SNR[<sup>3</sup>](#page-3-2) , i.g., nd−<sup>s</sup> ⋆ . To ensure

<sup>2</sup>A rigorous derivation of the error term with multiple neurons and general loss function ℓ can be found in the proof of [Example 4.6](#page-7-0) in [Appendix C.2.2.](#page-27-0)

<sup>3</sup>This argument is not fully rigorous because <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [∥g∥ <sup>2</sup>] also includes "bias" ∥<sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g]∥ <sup>2</sup> besides the fluctuations, but it remains valid as long as ∥g∥ <sup>2</sup> is dominated by fluctuations from all d directions at initialization.

one update step achieves alignment, i.e., the square root of the <sup>n</sup>-sample SNR, √ nd−<sup>s</sup> ⋆ , exceeding the trivial d −1/2 threshold attained by a random vector, it requires at least d s <sup>⋆</sup>−1 samples. Note that the sample complexity would become even worse if s <sup>⋆</sup> < argmins≥<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> {s : <sup>E</sup>Q[yζs(y)] ̸= 0}. This contrasts with the sample complexity O(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) suggested by the SQ lower bound.

The above failure of vanilla online minibatch SGD exposes three key challenges:

- (i) (Non-polynomial) How to handle the infinite sum of high-order terms in the likelihood ratio?
- (ii) (Low SNR) How to enhance the SNR to achieve the SQ lower bound?
- (iii) (Zero correlation) How to ensure that the algorithm still works if <sup>E</sup>Q[yζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y)] = 0?

Below we discuss our techniques for addressing these challenges.

Label transformation via general gradient oracle. The idea to fix the zero correlation problem is to apply a nonlinear transformation T : <sup>R</sup> → <sup>R</sup> to y such that T (y) has nonzero correlation with ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y). This label transformation technique has been widely used in the literature [\(Lu & Li,](#page-12-7) [2020;](#page-12-7) [Mondelli](#page-12-8) [& Montanari,](#page-12-8) [2018;](#page-12-8) [Dudeja & Hsu,](#page-11-6) [2018;](#page-11-6) [Chen & Meka,](#page-10-7) [2020;](#page-10-7) [Damian et al.,](#page-11-4) [2024\)](#page-11-4). In particular, [Lee et al.](#page-12-4) [\(2024\)](#page-12-4) show that the label transformation can be automatically realized by running two gradient steps on the same batch, a technique termed as *batch-reusing* [\(Dandi et al.,](#page-11-10) [2024;](#page-11-10) [Arnaboldi](#page-10-5) [et al.,](#page-10-5) [2024\)](#page-10-5). In this work, we study a more *general class of gradient-based methods* with gradient of form g = ψ(y,⟨θ, z⟩)z, which is an abstract form of the transformed gradient T (y)σ ′ (⟨z, θ⟩)z. The desired condition becomes <sup>E</sup>Q[ψp<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup>−1(y)ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y)] ̸= 0, where <sup>ψ</sup>ps(y) is the <sup>s</sup>-th Hermite coefficient function of ψ(y, x) in the Hermite basis of x. One particular way to obtain such a gradient is to use a modified loss function, similar to the approach in [Joshi et al.](#page-12-9) [\(2024\)](#page-12-9), while in our case the specific choice of ψ is also related to the other two challenges addressed as follows.

Exploration by weight perturbation with high-pass activation. The low-SNR challenge corresponds to the fact that points on the equator of S <sup>d</sup>−<sup>1</sup> orthogonal to θ ⋆ are all saddle points in terms of |⟨θ, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩|, and random initialization typically lies near this equator. To efficiently escape from such saddle points, we perform random weight perturbation, akin to the approach in [Jin et al.](#page-12-10) [\(2017\)](#page-12-10) for non-convex optimization. To understand the effectiveness of weight perturbation, we still stick to the squared loss and the two-layer neural network for the following second-moment calculation.[<sup>4</sup>](#page-4-0) Specifically, suppose the activation σ is high-pass and has the lowest degree s ⋆ , i.e., σ(x) = P s≥s <sup>⋆</sup> σpshs(x), and consider for simplicity the case of odd s ⋆ . In the extreme case where θ is perturbed into i.i.d. pure noise θ1, . . . , θ<sup>L</sup> ∼ Unif(<sup>S</sup> d−1 ), we compute the gradient for each θ<sup>l</sup> and aggregate them into g = L −1 (g<sup>1</sup> + · · · + gL). Using the properties of the Gaussian noise operator (see [Appendix B](#page-16-0) for details), the second moment of this aggregated gradient satisfies

$$\mathbb{E} \left[ \|g\|_2^2 \right] \approx \frac{d}{L^2} \sum_{l,l'=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[y^2] \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\sigma'(\langle z, \theta_l \rangle) \sigma'(\langle z, \theta_{l'} \rangle)] \approx d \sum_{s \geq s^*} s \cdot \hat{\sigma}_s^2 \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\theta, \theta'}[\langle \theta, \theta' \rangle^{s-1}],$$

where θ, θ′ are drown independently from Unif(S d−1 ). Since ⟨θ, θ′ ⟩ ≈ d −1/2 , we have <sup>E</sup>[∥g∥ 2 ] ≈ O(d −(s <sup>⋆</sup>−3)/2 ), yielding a higher one-sample SNR as the first moment remains unchanged and pushing the sample complexity towards the SQ lower bound. Moreover, we also see from the above calculation that the weight perturbation resolves the non-polynomial issue thanks to the nearorthogonality of the perturbed weights. The above heuristics can be made rigorous for polynomially large L, thereby handling non-polynomial link and activation functions.

Our approach also draws inspiration from the landscape smoothing method in [Damian et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4), but in constrast to their problem setup, we do not require full knowledge of the link distribution in advance. Instead, it suffices to know the generative exponent s ⋆ to construct a high-pass activation function as well as the gradient oracle ψ. See [Example 4.6](#page-7-0) for a detailed discussion on this.

## 4 G R A D I E N T- B A S E D A L G O R I T H M F O R U N I F O R M P R I O R

We first present our method and results for the case of θ <sup>⋆</sup> ∼ Unif(<sup>S</sup> d−1 ), or equivalently, when there is no structural information on θ ⋆ . Motivated by the discussion in [Section 3,](#page-3-3) we propose a gradient-based meta algorithm [\(Algorithm 1\)](#page-5-0) that can train a two-layer neural network to learn the unknown signal θ <sup>⋆</sup> with Or(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> ∨ d) sample complexity, nearly matching the corresponding SQ lower bound. This

meta-algorithm [\(Algorithm 1\)](#page-5-0) covers an extensive family of gradient-based methods including the batch-reusing and gradient descent with label transformation or modified loss.

#### 4 . 1 G R A D I E N T- B A S E D T R A I N I N G A L G O R I T H M ( A L G O R I T H M [1](#page-5-0) )

We initialize each neuron m with θ (0) <sup>m</sup> ∼ Unif(S d−1 ), and we set a (t) <sup>m</sup> ≡ a for some sufficiently small a > 0 throughout the training. In each iteration t ∈ [T], we sample a new data batch of size n.

Weight perturbation. Before calculating the gradients, we first perturb the weights of each neuron to get L noisy replica, by injecting uniform noise from the sphere S d−1 as in Line [4.](#page-5-1) There is a simple rule for choosing the polarization level γ. In the previous section, we discussed how <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [∥g∥ 2 2 ] in the one-sample SNR depends on the following quantity:

$$\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{m,l}^{(t)}, \xi_{m,l'}^{(t)}} \langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, w_{m,l'}^{(t)} \rangle^{s^*-1} \lesssim (\gamma^2 \|\theta_m^{(t)}\|_2^2)^{s^*-1} + \mathbb{E}_{\xi_{m,l}, \xi_{m,l'}^{(t)}} \langle \xi_{m,l}^{(t)}, \xi_{m,l'}^{(t)} \rangle^{s^*-1} \approx (\gamma^2 \vee d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1}.$$

In this context, γ 2 represents the bias from the *exploitation* of the learned search direction, and d −1/2 accounts for the variance from the *exploration* for the unknown signal. In fact, γ should be set as large as possible to maximize exploitation while still ensuring that the exploration noise dominates. This balance is necessary to fully gain the SNR enhancement from weight perturbation. This gives rise to the choice γ = Θ( <sup>r</sup> d −1/4 ). Moreover, it suffices to set L = Ω( <sup>r</sup> n √ d) as stated in [Theorem 4.2.](#page-6-0)

Gradient aggregation and debiasing. Then for each neuron m and its perturbed weights w (t) m,l, we calculate the gradient g (t) m,l,i for every sample (Line [5\)](#page-5-2). Here, we express the gradient as a sum of an oracle term ψ(y (t) i ,⟨z (t) i , w (t) m,l⟩) and an error term err (t) m,l,i, which we will discuss in more detail in the next paragraph. Next, we aggregate the gradients for each neuron m by averaging over the n samples and L perturbations to get g (t) <sup>m</sup> as shown in Line [6.](#page-5-3) Here we additionally subtract a term <sup>ψ</sup>p1(<sup>y</sup> (t) i )w (t) m,l to debias the gradient, where <sup>ψ</sup>p1(y) is the first coefficient function in the Hermite expansion of the oracle function ψ(y, x) with respect to x. Finally, we update θ (t) <sup>m</sup> according to Line [7](#page-5-4) and Line [8.](#page-5-5)

Implementation for a general loss function. The gradient step in Line [5](#page-5-2) is an implicit decomposition of the actual implementation, and we present the meta-algorithm in this way for the purpose of theoretical analysis. Specifically, for a general loss function ℓ(y, f(z; θ, a)) evaluated on the twolayer neural network, the actual g (t) m,l,i is the negative gradient of the loss rescaled by a −1 <sup>m</sup> , i.e.,

$$g_{m,l,i}^{(t)} = -a_m^{-1} \nabla_{\theta_m} \ell(y_i^{(t)}, f(z_i^{(t)}; \{w_{m,l}^{(t)}, \mathbf{a}\})) = -\ell'(y_i^{(t)}, f(z_i^{(t)}; \{w_{m,l}^{(t)}, \mathbf{a}\})) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i^{(t)} \rangle) \cdot z_i^{(t)},$$

where we denote by ℓ ′ (y, f) the partial derivative of the loss function with respect to the output f. Correspondingly, the oracle term and the error term are given by

$$\begin{aligned} \psi(y_i^{(t)}, \langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i^{(t)} \rangle) &= -\ell'(y_i^{(t)}, 0) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i^{(t)} \rangle), \\ \text{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)} &= \left( \ell'(y_i^{(t)}, 0) - \ell'(y_i^{(t)}, f(z_i^{(t)}; \{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m=1}^M, \mathbf{a})) \right) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i^{(t)} \rangle). \end{aligned}$$

Note that err (t) m,l,i can be made small by setting a to be small. Again, we emphasize that these two terms are not calculated explicitly, but rather from an implicit decomposition of the actual gradient.

Beyond the above example, the meta-algorithm encompasses more general gradient-based methods, and the theoretical results presented later are valid as long as the actual gradient can be decomposed in the same way such that the assumptions of our main theorem are satisfied.

#### 4 . 2 F E AT U R E A L I G N M E N T A N D S TAT I S T I C A L C O M P L E X I T Y

For any ψ ∈ L 2 (Q), we write its Hermite expansion as ψ(y, x) = P<sup>∞</sup> <sup>s</sup>=0 <sup>ψ</sup>ps(y) · <sup>h</sup>s(y) in the <sup>L</sup> 2 (Q) sense, where <sup>ψ</sup>ps(y) is the <sup>s</sup>-th coefficient function and P<sup>∞</sup> <sup>s</sup>=0 <sup>E</sup>Q[ψps(y) 2 ] < ∞.

Assumption 4.1. *For the Gaussian single-index model in* [\(2.1\)](#page-2-1) *with generative exponent* s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1*, the oracle* ψ : R × R → R *satisfies the following conditions:*

- *(a) (Quadruple-integrable under* Q*). Both* ψ(y, x) · x *and* ψ(y, x) *belong to* L 4 (Q)*.*
- *(b) (High-pass under* <sup>Q</sup>*). For all* <sup>s</sup> = 1, . . . , s<sup>⋆</sup>−2*, the* <sup>s</sup>*-th coefficient function is zero, i.e.,* <sup>ψ</sup>ps(y) ≡ <sup>0</sup>*. In addition, there exists a constant* C > 0 *such that* |<sup>E</sup>Q[ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y) · <sup>ψ</sup>p<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup>−1(y)]| ≥ C*.*
- *(c) (Polynomial-like tail under* P *and* Q*). There exists a constant* C > 0 *such that for all* r ≥ 1*,* max{<sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup> [|ψ(y, x)| r ] , <sup>E</sup><sup>Q</sup> [|ψ(y, x)| r ]} ≤ C · r Cr *.*

The quadruple-integrability condition [\(Assumption 4.1\(a\)\)](#page-6-1) ensures that the decomposition of the likelihood ratio in [\(2.2\)](#page-2-2) is well defined for calculations involving the second moment, i.e.,

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}} \left[ \psi(y, x)^2 x^2 \right] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ \psi(y, x)^2 x^2 \cdot \frac{\mathbb{P}(y, x)}{\mathbb{Q}(y, x)} \right] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ \psi(y, x)^2 x^2 \cdot \sum_{s=0}^{\infty} \zeta_s(y) h_s(x) \right].$$

The high-pass condition [\(Assumption 4.1\(b\)\)](#page-6-3) has been motivated in [Section 3](#page-3-3) and guarantees noise reduction for the second moment. The polynomial-like tail condition [\(Assumption 4.1\(c\)\)](#page-6-4) is used for concentration arguments in the proof. Note that this condition is analogue to the Gaussian hypercontractivity property, where <sup>E</sup>x∼N(0,1)[|f(x)| r ] ≲ r Dr/2 if f(x) is a polynomial of degree at most D. In particular, ψ can be constructed as ψ(y, x) = ℓ ′ (y)σ ′ (x), where ℓ is the loss function and σ is the activation in the two-layer network. It suffices to use a loss ℓ with bounded derivative and a polynomial activation σ for the polynomial-like tail condition (see [Section 4.2.1](#page-7-1) and [4.2.2\)](#page-7-2).

Now we are ready to state our first main result on the sample complexity of [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) for uniform prior. See [Appendix D](#page-30-0) for a proof sketch for even s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2 and [Appendix E](#page-31-0) for a detailed proof.

Theorem 4.2 (Sample complexity for uniform prior). *Under [Assumption 4.1,](#page-6-2) set the initialization of the weights as* θ (0) 1 , . . . , θ(0) M <sup>i</sup>.i.d. ∼ *Unif*(<sup>S</sup> d−1 )*. Suppose that the event* E = {|err (t) m,l,i| ≤ d −10s ⋆ , ∀(m, l, i, t) ∈ [M] × [L] × [n] × [T]} *holds with probability at least* 1 − O(d <sup>−</sup>c<sup>0</sup> ) *for some constant* c<sup>0</sup> > 0 *with* (M, L, n, T) *specified as follows. Set the learning rate* η > 2*, the polarization level* γ = d −1/4 (log d) 1/4 *, and the number of neurons* M = Θ(1)*. Suppose*

$$n = \Theta((d^{s^*/2}(\log d)^{1+s^*/2}) \vee d(\log d)^2), \quad L = \Theta(d^{(s^*+1)/2} \log d).$$

*Define* τ = η <sup>−</sup><sup>2</sup>/(1 − η −1 ) *, and let* ∆ = (log d) −1/2 *if* s <sup>⋆</sup> ≤ 2 *and* ∆ = d −1/4 (log d) 1/4 *if* s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 3*. Then with probability at least* 1 − O(d −c ) *for some constant* c > 0*, after running [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) for* T = O(log d + log(∆<sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> )/ log(τ −1 )) *steps, there are at least* Ω(M) *neurons having alignment* |⟨θ (T) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| ≥ 1 − O( √ ∆).

[Theorem 4.2](#page-6-0) shows that the sample complexity of [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) is nT = Θ( <sup>r</sup> d s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> ∨ d), matching the SQ sample complexity lower bound for all s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1 established by [Damian et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4). Compared to the partial trace method in [Damian et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4), our algorithm does not require special warm-start initialization. Meanwhile, the computational complexity of [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) is MLnT = Θ( <sup>r</sup> d s <sup>⋆</sup>+1/<sup>2</sup>∨d 2 ). Indeed, one is allowed to choose η = +∞, which resembles tensor power iteration. So far the gradient

oracle ψ is still an abstract object, and next we will instantiate the above general theorem with concrete examples of ψ that yield implementable algorithms. We consider the special case of polynomial link functions with s <sup>⋆</sup> ≤ 2 in [Section 4.2.1,](#page-7-1) and then the general case for any s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1 in [Section 4.2.2.](#page-7-2)

Remark 4.3 (Benefit of overparametrization). *[Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) trains a two-layer neural network with constant width* M*, involving* L *times of perturbation for every neuron in each step. Indeed, this is equivalent to train a two-layer neural network with width* LM = Θ d (s <sup>⋆</sup>−1)/<sup>2</sup> ∨(d log d) 1/2 *, where we divide the neurons into* L *groups, each having* M *neurons. In each iteration we perturb the weights and compute the gradients, and then aggregate the gradients within each group of* M *neurons. This combination of weight perturbation and* gradient sharing *exploits the* benefit of overparametrization*.*

#### 4 . 2 . 1 O N L I N E S G D W I T H B AT C H RE U S I N G

The oracle function ψ can be specialized to two consecutive gradient descent steps on the same batch under square loss to handle polynomial link functions corresponding to s <sup>⋆</sup> ≤ 2 [\(Lee et al.,](#page-12-4) [2024\)](#page-12-4).

Example 4.4 (Batch-reusing: ψ for polynomial link functions). *Suppose that the link distribution is a polynomial of degree* q*. We consider* ψ *induced by batch-reusing on single neuron, i.e.,* ψ(y, x) = yσ′ (x) + yσ′ (x + yσ′ (x)) *(see Section 4.2 of [Lee et al.](#page-12-4) [\(2024\)](#page-12-4) for deduction) and choose* σ ′ (x) = P<sup>C</sup><sup>q</sup> <sup>i</sup>=0 cihi(x) *where* C<sup>q</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup> *is a constant depending only on* q *and each* c<sup>i</sup> ∼ Unif([0, 1])*.*

Corollary 4.5 (Batch-reusing for polynomial link function). *Suppose that the link distribution is given by a polynomial link function. Under the same setups in [Theorem 4.2](#page-6-0) with the oracle* ψ *given by [Example 4.4,](#page-7-3) the sample complexity of [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) is* Θ( <sup>r</sup> d)*, recovering the result of [Lee et al.](#page-12-4) [\(2024\)](#page-12-4).*

The proof is deferred to [Appendix C.2.1.](#page-24-0) However, batch-reusing may not be optimal for s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 3 due to violation of the high-pass condition, necessitating a more general approach to construct ψ.

#### 4 . 2 . 2 L A B E L T R A N S F O R M AT I O N V I A M O D I F I E D L O S S

We discuss another approach to construct ψ by modifying the loss function, a universal method for arbitrary generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1. Additional details and proofs are postponed to [Appendix C.2.2.](#page-27-0)

Example 4.6 (ψ based on modified loss). *Let* ψ(y, x) = ℓ ′ (y)σ ′ (x) *with* ℓ(y) *being certain loss function and* σ(x) *being some activation function. Such a form corresponds to the gradient of the loss* ℓ(y − f(z; θ)) *(assuming that* a *is fixed and has small entries), since*

$$a_m^{-1} \nabla_{\theta_m} \ell(y - f(z; \boldsymbol{\theta})) = -\ell'(y - f(z; \boldsymbol{\theta})) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta_m, z \rangle) \cdot z = -\underbrace{\ell'(y) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta_m, z \rangle)}_{:=\psi(y, \langle \theta_m, z \rangle)} \cdot z + \text{err}_m \cdot z,$$

*where* err<sup>m</sup> :=[ℓ ′ (y) − ℓ ′ (y − f(z; θ, a))] · σ ′ (⟨θm, z⟩) = O(f(z; θ, a))· σ ′ (⟨θm, z⟩) *denotes small error for sufficiently small* a*. In [Appendix C.2.2,](#page-27-0) we provide specific choice of the activation function* σ(x) *(order-*s <sup>⋆</sup> *Hermite polynomial) and the loss function* ℓ(y) *(a carefully designed random loss function), satisfying all the conditions in [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2)*

Corollary 4.7 (Modified loss for general s ⋆ ). *The oracle* ψ *given by [Example 4.6](#page-7-0) satisfies all the assumptions of [Theorem 4.2,](#page-6-0) thus the results of [Theorem 4.2](#page-6-0) hold for [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) using this* ψ*.*

To satisfy the high-pass condition with high probability, we construct a random loss function based on the spectral properties of ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> in the likelihood ratio decomposition, as detailed in [Appendix C.2.2.](#page-27-0) Furthermore, we show an equivalence between direct label transformation and modified loss functions (see [Appendix C.1.1](#page-19-0) for details). Therefore, using a random label transformation with the standard squared loss can also achieve a similar effect. (See [Appendix C.4](#page-30-1) for a discussion on the limitations.)

## 5 E X P L O I T I N G T H E S T R U C T U R E : A L G O R I T H M F O R S PA R S E P R I O R

We have seen that when the prior on θ ⋆ is uninformative, our method nearly achieves the SQ lower bound that scales with the ambient dimension d. It is natural to ask whether our method can benefit from extra structural information on θ ⋆ , one classic example being sparsity. To this end, we consider an extension of the framework in the previous section to the setting where θ ⋆ is a k-sparse vector. We first introduce the algorithm for extreme sparsity k = o( √ d) and then discuss the general sparse case. Gaussian single-index model with sparse signal. Given sparsity level k = o( √ d), we consider the Gaussian single-index model in [\(2.1\)](#page-2-1) with θ <sup>⋆</sup> drawn from a k-sparse prior:

$$\pi_k : \theta^* \mid \phi^* \sim \text{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{k-1}(\phi^*)), \quad \phi^* \sim \text{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_k), \quad (5.1)$$

where S<sup>k</sup> :={ϕ ⊂ [d] : |ϕ| = k} is the collection of all k-sparse support sets, and <sup>S</sup> k−1 (ϕ):={x ∈ R d : P i∈ϕ x 2 <sup>i</sup> = 1, x<sup>j</sup> = 0, ∀j /∈ ϕ} is the associated k-dimensional unit sphere for any ϕ ∈ Sk.

We show that [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) for the uniform prior can be easily adapted to leverage the sparsity of θ ⋆ , striking a nearly optimal computational-statistical balance with Or(k s ⋆ ) sample complexity.

#### 5 . 1 A L G O R I T H M D E S I G N : H O W T O L E V E R A G E S PA R S I T Y ?

Note that [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) can also learn the k-sparse Gaussian single-index model, albeit with Or(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> ∨ d) samples, which is apparently suboptimal in light of the classic example of sparse linear regression. Here the key challenge is *support identification* of ϕ ⋆ , and the issue of [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) lies in the weight perturbation using noise ξ ∼ Unif(S d−1 ), thus unaware of the sparsity of θ ⋆ . Below we discuss how to calibrate the weight perturbation with the sparse prior.

Perturbation by replicating the prior is not enough. An intuitive first-cut attempt is to use perturbation noise from the same distribution as the sparse prior π<sup>k</sup> in [\(5.1\)](#page-8-0), which turns out to be suboptimal as well. To illustrate this, we assume for simplicity a balanced θ <sup>⋆</sup> where every nonzero entry of θ ⋆ is equal to k −1/2 , and consider i.i.d. ξ1, . . . , ξ<sup>L</sup> ∼ πk. For each j ∈ ϕ ⋆ , consider the j-th entry of the lowest-degree informative query (analogous to [\(3.2\)](#page-3-4)), whose first moment satisfies

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}, \theta^*} [g_j] \approx \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[y\zeta_{\delta}(y)] \cdot s^* \hat{\sigma}_{s^*} \cdot \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle \theta^*, \theta_l \rangle^{s^*-1} \theta_j \approx \frac{\mathbb{E}_{\theta \sim \pi_{\mathbb{K}}}[\langle \theta^*, \theta \rangle^{s^*-1}]}{\sqrt{k}} \simeq \frac{k^2}{d} \cdot \frac{k^{-(s^*-1)}}{\sqrt{k}},$$

where the last step follows from direct calculation for θ ∼ πk. Similarly, the second moment satisfies

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta,*}}[g_j^2] \approx \sum_{s \geq s^*} s \cdot (\hat{\sigma}_s)^2 \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\theta, \theta' \sim \pi_k}[(\theta, \theta')^{s-1}] \simeq \frac{k^2}{d} \cdot k^{-(s^*-1)},$$

where θ and θ ′ are drawn independently from the prior πk. This calculation implies that the fluctuation of each entry of the aggregated gradient is of order p k <sup>2</sup>/d · k −(s <sup>⋆</sup>−1)/2 · n −1/2 for batch size n. To successfully identify the true support ϕ ⋆ , the signal must be larger than the fluctuation for entries in ϕ ⋆ , resulting in a sample complexity of n = Or(k s ⋆ · d/k<sup>2</sup> ). In comparison to the SQ lower bound in [Theorem 5.4,](#page-9-0) this is suboptimal by a factor d/k<sup>2</sup> . However, for s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1, we observe that <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g<sup>j</sup> ] = Ω(k −1 ) for j ∈ ϕ ⋆ and <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g 2 j ] = O(1), indicating that the support ϕ ⋆ can still be identified using Or(k) samples. The form of the perturbation does not matter here since both ⟨θ ⋆ , θ⟩ and ⟨θ, θ′ ⟩ are degree zero in terms of k −1 . Therefore, we conjecture that our algorithm succeeds for s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1 even without weight perturbation as outlined in [Conjecture 5.3.](#page-9-1)

Perturbation by groups that cover the prior. The suboptimality of the previous stragety originates from the fact that ϕ ⋆ is sampled from a uniform distribution over d k different k-sparse support sets, making it unlikely for two independent sets to overlap (only with k <sup>2</sup>/d probability). Then how to perturb the weights in a way that guarantees a significant overlap with ϕ ⋆ ? The solution is to construct *a polynomial-size cover* for the prior πk. Specifically, we divide S<sup>k</sup> into d subsets, where the j-th subset is define as Sk,j := {ϕ ∈ S<sup>k</sup> | j ∈ ϕ}, which contains all k-sparse support sets that include the j-th coordinate. Now suppose θ <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ Sk,j , then for any perturbed weight θ<sup>l</sup> with support from the same subset Sk,j , its support overlaps with ϕ ⋆ almost surely, thereby eliminating the d/k<sup>2</sup> factor.

In particular, considering a two-layer neural network with width d, the above strategy can be carried out by perturbing each neuron m using θm,1, . . . , θm,L whose support sets are sampled from the same group Sk,m. As a result, at least k neurons will have one or more overlapping coordinates with the true signal θ ⋆ . For these neurons, the signal in the aggregated gradient would be strong enough for simple thresholding methods to correctly identify the true support ϕ <sup>⋆</sup> with Or(k ⋆ ) samples. We further refine this by first projecting the aggregated gradient for each neuron onto its top-k support, and then selecting the *strongest* projected gradient to update the weights.

Combining these yields [Algorithm 2](#page-9-2) for the sparse case, where we define the support projection matrix P<sup>ϕ</sup> := P i∈ϕ eie ⊤ i and the top-k operator Top<sup>k</sup> (v):= argmaxϕ⊂S<sup>k</sup> ∥Pϕ(v)∥1, which extracts the k-sparse support ϕ corresponding to the largest (in absolute value) k entries of v. We set the polarization level γ = k −1/2 , following the same balance between exploitation and exploration as in the uniform case, since the exploration noise is now of order k −1 .

Algorithm 2 Meta Algorithm for Gradient-based Feature Learning for Sparse Signal Prior

- 1: Input: Initialization θ
- (0) = (θ

#### 5 . 2 S A M P L E C O M P L E X I T Y A N A LY S I S F O R S PA R S E P R I O R

Theorem 5.1 (Sample complexity for sparse prior). *Under [Assumption 4.1,](#page-6-2) consider the sparse prior in* [\(5.1\)](#page-8-0) *with sparsity level* k *satisfying* ω(d ι ) < k < o( √ d) *for a small* ι > 0*. Suppose that the event* E = {|err (t) m,l,i| ≤ d −10s ⋆ , ∀(m, l, i, t) ∈ [M] × [L] × [n] × [T]} *holds with probability at least* 1 − O(d <sup>−</sup>c<sup>0</sup> ) *for some constant* c<sup>0</sup> > 0 *with* (M, L, n, T) *specified as follows. Let* γ = k −1/2 *,* n = Ω((k log<sup>3</sup> k) s ⋆ ·log d)*, and* L = Ω(k (s <sup>⋆</sup>+3)/2 ·log(k) s <sup>⋆</sup>−1 )*. Then with probability at least* 1−O(k −c ) *for some* c > 0*, after running [Algorithm 2](#page-9-2) with* T = 2 *iterations, there are at least* Ω(M) *neurons that have alignment* |⟨θ (T) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| ≥ 1 − O(∆), *where* ∆ = k <sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> ∨ k −(s <sup>⋆</sup>−1)/2 · log(k) −3/2 = o(1)*.*

[Theorem 5.1](#page-9-3) shows that the sample complexity of [Algorithm 2](#page-9-2) is n = Or(k s ⋆ ), matching the SQ lower bound established in [Theorem 5.4](#page-9-0) which will be presented below. Here for simplicity, we essentially use an infinitely large learning rate when updating the weights in Line [8](#page-9-4) of [Algorithm 2,](#page-9-2) so it takes only two iterations to achieve strong alignment. This is equivalent to running the same algorithm with a finite learning rate but with a larger number of iterations, which is omitted for brevity.

Remark 5.2 (Implication for sparse tensor PCA). *The connection between the Gaussian single-index model and tensor PCA has been discussed in [Damian et al.](#page-11-5) [\(2023\)](#page-11-5), by showing that estimating* θ ⋆ *corresponds to a tensor PCA problem defined over the empirical Hermite tensors. Our weight perturbation technique can be potentially applied to iteratively solve sparse tensor PCA problems.*

Conjecture 5.3. *If* d <sup>ι</sup> < k < o(d) *for a small* ι > 0 *and* s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1*, [Algorithm 2](#page-9-2) succeeds with sample complexity* n = Or(k)*. Furthermore, the same guarantee applies even without perturbing the weights.*

Finally, we present the following SQ lower bound for the sparse prior, complementing [Theorem 5.1.](#page-9-3) Theorem 5.4 (SQ lower bound). *Consider the Gaussian single-index model in* [\(2.1\)](#page-2-1) *with generative exponent* s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1*. Suppose* θ ⋆ *is* k*-sparse for* ω((log d) 2 ) ≤ k ≤ d/2*. Take* c > 2 *as a constant. For any (stochastic) algorithm using* exp(Ω((log d) c )) *calls to the* VSTAT(<sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> , n) *oracle with sample size* n*, in order to achieve nontrivial alignment* |⟨θ, θ <sup>p</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| > ρ *with probability at least* 2/3*, it requires*

$$n \gtrsim \frac{k^s}{(\log d)^{cs*}}, \quad \text{where} \quad \rho = \tilde{\omega}(k^{-1}) \quad \text{if } (\log d)^2 < k < \sqrt{d(\log d)^c}, \quad (5.2)$$

$$n \gtrsim \frac{d^{s^{*/2}}}{(\log d)^{cs^{*/2}}}, \quad \text{where} \quad \rho = \tilde{\omega}(d^{-1/2}) \quad \text{if } \sqrt{d(\log d)^c} \leq k \leq d/2. \quad (5.3)$$

More discussion on [Theorem 5.4](#page-9-0) is available in [Remark G.8.](#page-64-0) For k = o( √ d), running [Algorithm 2](#page-9-2) will succeed with Or(k s ⋆ ) samples, matching the lower bound in [\(5.2\)](#page-9-5) for every s <sup>⋆</sup> <sup>≥</sup> <sup>1</sup>. For <sup>k</sup> = Ω(√ d), running [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) will succeed with Or(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) samples, matching the lower bound in [\(5.3\)](#page-9-6) for s <sup>⋆</sup> <sup>≥</sup> <sup>2</sup>. In addition, for <sup>k</sup> = Ω(√ d) with s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1, we conjecture n = Or(k) samples to be sufficient, where the information-theoretic lower bound is Ω(k log(d/k)) [\(Neykov et al.,](#page-12-11) [2016\)](#page-12-11). This gives rise to the paradigm in [Figure 1](#page-1-1) (b).

## A N U M E R I C A L E X P E R I M E N T S

We conduct extensive simulation experiments to validate the sample complexity result established in [Theorem 4.2.](#page-6-0) In specific, for a fixed Gaussian single-index model, we run [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) extensively over a variety of problem instances with diverse scales and report the average accuracy in terms of the alignment. We lay out the details of the experiment setting as follows.

• Gaussian single-index model. We focus on the Gaussian single-index model introduced in [\(2.1\)](#page-2-1) with a deterministic link function p and Gaussian additive noise. Here we set p(x) = x 2 ·exp(−x 2 )+ϵ where ϵ ∼ N (0, σ<sup>2</sup> ) with σ <sup>2</sup> = 0.5. As shown in [Example 2.2,](#page-2-0) the generative exponent of the function p is s ⋆ (p) = 4. In addition, the signal parameter θ ⋆ is uniformly sampled from the unit sphere in R d .

• Neural network architecture. We adopt the two-layer neural network introduced in [Section 2](#page-2-4) with M set to 15 and a<sup>m</sup> = 1 for all m ∈ [M] in all experiments. Since s ⋆ (p) = 4, we set the activation function as σ(x) = h4(x), i.e., the fourth-order Hermite polynomial.

• Training using [Algorithm 1.](#page-5-0) In [Algorithm 1,](#page-5-0) we set ψ(x, y) = y · σ ′ (x), as stated in [Example 4.6.](#page-7-0) Such a ψ is justified by considering the following alignment loss:

$$L(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = 1 - y \cdot f(z; \boldsymbol{\theta}, \mathbf{a}) = 1 - \sum_{m=1}^M a \cdot \sigma(\langle z, \theta_m \rangle) \cdot y, \quad (\text{A.1})$$

where recall that each entry of a is equal to a. As a result, by [\(A.1\)](#page-14-1) we have

$$a^{-1} \cdot \nabla_{\theta_m} L(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = y \cdot \sigma'(\langle z, \theta_m \rangle) \cdot z = \psi(\langle z, \theta_m \rangle, y) \cdot z.$$

As a result, we can alternatively interpret the gradients in [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) as those with respect to the alignment loss L(θ). Thus, the choice of a does not matter in this case, and we set a = 1 for simplicity. Furthermore, other details of [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) are specified as follows:

- The parameters {θn}m∈[M] are initialized as i.i.d. random vectors in <sup>R</sup> <sup>d</sup> uniformly sampled from the unit sphere.
- We fix M = 15, a = 1, η = 3, T = 24, and L = 500 throughout all experiments with different values of n and d.

- We enumerate n and d over a grid with d ∈ [32, 499] and n ∈ [5 × 10<sup>3</sup> , 3 × 10<sup>6</sup> ]. Note that log d ∈ (3, 7), our choice of T satisfies the requirement in Theorem [4.2.](#page-6-0)

• Choices of (d, n). We select 40 different values of d and 30 different values of n within the ranges d ∈ [32, 499] and n ∈ [5 × 10<sup>3</sup> , 3 × 10<sup>6</sup> ], respectively. These values form an evenly spaced grid in terms of log n and log d. See Figure [2](#page-15-0) for an illustration.

![](_page_15_Figure_3.jpeg)

Figure 2: Scatter plots of (d, n) and (log d, log n). In (a) we plot n against d and in (b) we plot log n agains log d. As shown in (b), we choose n and d such that they form an evenly-spaced grid after logarithm.

• Evaluation. We report the accuracy of [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) based on 25 repeated experiments for every choice of (n, d). We report two types of accuracy metrics:

- (i) Average accuracy: We report M−<sup>1</sup> P<sup>M</sup> <sup>m</sup>=1 |⟨θm, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| in each experiment and then average over the 25 experiments.
- (ii) Top-8 accuracy: Given {θm}m∈[M] returned by the algorithm, we sort the alignment values {|⟨θm, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩|}m∈[M] . Then we report the average of the largest 8 numbers. The rationale is that if the top-8 accuracy is close to one, at least half of the neurons correctly find θ ⋆ .

Contour plots. After calculating these two versions of accuracy for every (d, n) pair, we generate the contour plots based on (log d, log n, acc(d, n)), where acc(d, n) is one of the two versions of average accuracy introduced above. We report these two contour plots in Figure [3](#page-16-1) and Figure [4,](#page-16-2) where in Figure [3](#page-16-1) we zoom in to a smaller range of d for better visualization. In these plots, points with the same color indicate (log d, log n) with the same level of accuracy.

Validate Θ( <sup>r</sup> d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) sample complexity. As shown in these figures, the average accuracy and the top-8 accuracy clearly exhibit a linear relationship. That is, for a fixed accuracy level δ, (d, n) satisfying acc(d, n) = δ is a line segment. That is, log n = c<sup>1</sup> · log d + c2. To determine c<sup>1</sup> and c2, we further fit linear models for (log d, log n) with the same accuracy level δ, where δ ∈ {0.6, 0.7, 0.8}. For both the average accuracy and the top-8 accuracy, the coefficient c<sup>1</sup> in the linear models is close to 2. We report the linear models corresponding to different accuracy levels in Table [1.](#page-15-1) This finding indicates that n ∝ d 2 . Note that s <sup>⋆</sup> = 4. Moreover, since we compute the accuracy for all (d, n) on the grid. The fact that c<sup>1</sup> ≈ 2 indicates that the Θ( <sup>r</sup> d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) sample complexity is sharp.

Table 1: Fitted linear equations of the form log n = c<sup>1</sup> · log d + c<sup>2</sup> for n, d with the desired accuracy level. Notably, the slopes of these equations are all close to s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 = 2, which shows that n ∝ d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 .

## B N O TAT I O N , P R E L I M I N A R I E S A N D RE L AT E D WO R K S

Notations. We use N to denote the set of positive integers and N<sup>0</sup> to denote the set of nonnegative integers. For vector z ∈ R d , we denote by <sup>R</sup>n[z] the set of polynomials of degree at most n in z with real coefficients. For s ∈ <sup>N</sup>, we denote by Π<sup>s</sup> the symmetric group of all permutations of [s]. We denote by Nd(·) and N (·) the standard normal distribution in <sup>R</sup> d and R, respectively.

For two tensors S ∈ (<sup>R</sup> d ) ⊗s and T ∈ (<sup>R</sup> d ) <sup>⊗</sup><sup>t</sup> where s ≥ t,

$$(S[T])_{j_1, \dots, j_{s-t}} := \sum_{i_1, \dots, i_t=1}^d S_{j_1, \dots, j_{s-t}, i_1, \dots, i_t} T_{i_1, \dots, i_t}.$$

Here, S[T] produces a tensor of order s − t and dimension d. We also define the symmetrization operation for a tensor T ∈ (<sup>R</sup> d ) ⊗t as

$$\text{Sym}(T)_{i_1, \dots, i_t} := \frac{1}{t!} \sum_{\pi \in \Pi_t} T_{i_{\pi(1)}, \dots, i_{\pi(t)}}.$$

a ≃ b: There exists a constant C = O(1) such that a ≤ Cb and b ≤ Ca. Note that a and b should have the same sign. a = Θ(b) also has the same meaning.

a <sup>≊</sup> b: a ≤ polylog(d) · b and b ≤ polylog(d) · a, and the same for a = Θ( <sup>r</sup> b). a ≲ b: There exists a constant C = O(1) such that a ≤ Cb, and the same for a = Ω(b). The use of a ≳ b is similar.

a ⪅ b: a ≤ polylog(d) · b, and the same for a = Or(b). The use of a ⪆ b and a = Ω( <sup>r</sup> b) is similar. a ≪ b: a ≤ (polylog(d))<sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> · b. The use of a ≫ b is similar.

In addition, we denote by a = b ± ε, a ≃ b ± ε, a <sup>≊</sup> b ± ε that b − ε ≤ a ≤ b + ε, a − ε ≲ b ≲ a + ε, a − ε ⪅ b ⪅ a + ε, respectively.

For square matrices A and B, A ⪯ B means that B − A is positive semi-definite, and A ≾ B means that there exists a constant C = O(1) such that C · B − A is positive semidefinite.

## B . 1 B A C K G R O U N D O N H E R M I T E P O LY N O M I A L S

The probabilist's Hermite polynomials satisfy the following recurrence relations

$$h_s(x)' = \sqrt{s} \cdot h_{s-1}(x), \quad x \cdot h_s(x) = \sqrt{s+1} \cdot h_{s+1}(x) + \sqrt{s} \cdot h_{s-1}(x), \quad (\text{B.1})$$

where we adopt the convention that h−1(x) ≡ 0.

For any function f ∈ L (N (0, 1)), its Hermite expansion is given by

$$f(x) = \sum_{s=0}^{\infty} \hat{f}_s \cdot h_s(x),$$

where we denote by fp<sup>s</sup> the <sup>s</sup>-th coefficient of the Hermite expansion of f.

Gaussian noise operator. For ρ ∈ [−1, 1], define the Gaussian noise operator as

$$\mathbf{U}_\rho f(x) = \mathbb{E}_{x' \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)} [f(\rho x + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2} \cdot x')].$$

Proposition 11.37 of [O'Donnell](#page-12-12) [\(2014\)](#page-12-12) shows that the Hermite expansion of Uρf is given by

$$\text{U}_\rho f(x) \stackrel{L^2(\mathcal{N}(0,1))}{=} \sum_{s=0}^\infty \rho^s \cdot \hat{f}_s \cdot h_s(x).$$

A direct implication of this identity is

$$\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)} [\mathbf{U}_\rho f(x) g(x)] = \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)} [f(x) \mathbf{U}_\rho g(x)] = \sum_{s=0}^{\infty} \rho^s \hat{f}_s \hat{g}_s. \quad (\text{B.2})$$

As a result, for any fixed w, θ ∈ S d−1 , it holds that

$$\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I_d)} [f(\langle w, z \rangle) h_s(\langle \theta, z \rangle)] = \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)} [\text{U}_\rho f \cdot h_s(x)] = \langle w, \theta \rangle^s \cdot \hat{f}_s. \quad (\text{B.3})$$

Hermite tensor. Corresponding to the Hermite polynomials defined for scalar variables, we define the Hermite tensors over z ∈ R d :

$$\mathbf{h}_s(z) := \frac{(-1)^s}{\sqrt{s!}} \cdot e^{\|z\|^2/2} \cdot \nabla^s e^{-\|z\|^2/2} \in (\mathbb{R}^d)^{\otimes s}, \text{ for } s \geq 0.$$

The scalar-valued Hermite polynomials and the tensor-valued Hermite tensors are related as follows:

$$h_s(\langle \theta, z \rangle) = \mathbf{h}_s(z)[\theta^{\otimes s}], \quad \forall \theta \in \mathbb{S}^{d-1}. \quad (\text{B.4})$$

Now let f : R <sup>d</sup> → <sup>R</sup> be a s-times differentiable function such that for all k ≤ s, every component of ∇<sup>k</sup>f belongs to L (N (0, Id)). Then it follows from integration by parts that

$$\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I_d)} [f(z) \mathbf{h}_s(z)] = \frac{1}{\sqrt{s!}} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}(0, I_d)} [\nabla^s f(z)]. \quad (\text{B.5})$$

### B . 2 A D D I T I O N A L RE L AT E D WO R K S

Related Works. Our work contributes to the recent research on the computation-statistical tradeoff in learning single-index models. The information-theoretic limit for estimating the latent signal is n = Ω(d) [\(Bach,](#page-10-2) [2017;](#page-10-2) [Damian et al.,](#page-11-4) [2024\)](#page-11-4), but the sample complexity lower bound varies across computational models, potentially revealing a computational-statistical gap.

The information exponent q ⋆ [\(Dudeja & Hsu,](#page-11-6) [2018;](#page-11-6) [Arous et al.,](#page-10-3) [2021\)](#page-10-3) governs the sample complexity for learning Gaussian single-index models in the CSQ framework [\(Chen et al.,](#page-10-8) [2020;](#page-10-8) [Bietti et al.,](#page-10-4) [2022;](#page-10-4) [Damian et al.,](#page-11-11) [2022;](#page-11-11) [Dandi et al.,](#page-11-12) [2023;](#page-11-12) [Abbe et al.,](#page-10-9) [2023;](#page-10-9) [Ba et al.,](#page-10-10) [2023\)](#page-10-10). Notably, [Arous](#page-10-3) [et al.](#page-10-3) [\(2021\)](#page-10-3) show that online SGD has a sample complexity of n = Or(d q <sup>⋆</sup>−1 ), which is worse than the CSQ lower bound n = Ω(d q <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) [\(Abbe et al.,](#page-10-9) [2023;](#page-10-9) [Damian et al.,](#page-11-11) [2022\)](#page-11-11). This gap can be closed by a loss landscape smoothing technique [\(Damian et al.,](#page-11-5) [2023\)](#page-11-5) originally developed for tensor PCA [\(Anandkumar et al.,](#page-10-11) [2017;](#page-10-11) [Biroli et al.,](#page-10-12) [2020\)](#page-10-12).

Our work extends beyond the CSQ framework, aligning with more general SQ algorithms [\(Feldman](#page-11-13) [et al.,](#page-11-13) [2017;](#page-11-13) [Feldman,](#page-11-14) [2017\)](#page-11-14), where the sample complexity lower bound is Ω(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ), with s ⋆ as the generative exponent [\(Damian et al.,](#page-11-4) [2024\)](#page-11-4). In this context, online SGD with batch reusing suffices for learning polynomial link functions [\(Dandi et al.,](#page-11-10) [2024;](#page-11-10) [Lee et al.,](#page-12-4) [2024\)](#page-12-4), while for s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 3, only the partial trace estimator proposed by [Damian et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4) can match the SQ lower bound.

In the sparse setting, including sparse linear models [\(Vaskevicius et al.,](#page-12-13) [2019;](#page-12-13) [Zhao et al.,](#page-12-14) [2022;](#page-12-14) [Gamarnik & Zadik,](#page-11-15) [2017\)](#page-11-15), sparse PCA [\(Arous et al.,](#page-10-13) [2020\)](#page-10-13), and planted models [\(Bandeira et al.,](#page-10-14) [2022\)](#page-10-14), computational-statistical gaps also exist. For example, in matrix PCA, the best rank-1 estimator achieves a near-optimal sample complexity of Or(d) due to the BBP transition [\(Baik et al.,](#page-10-15) [2005;](#page-10-15) [Choo & d'Orsi,](#page-10-16) [2021\)](#page-10-16). Under extreme sparsity, however, sparse estimators require Or(k 2 ) samples, utilizing methods such as diagonal thresholding [\(Johnstone & Lu,](#page-12-15) [2009\)](#page-12-15) or semidefinite relaxation [\(d'Aspremont et al.,](#page-11-16) [2004\)](#page-11-16). These approaches improve upon the Or(d) sample complexity but reveal a notable computational-statistical gap relative to the information-theoretic lower bound of Ω(k log d) [\(Wang et al.,](#page-12-16) [2016\)](#page-12-16).

Related to our work, [Fan et al.](#page-11-8) [\(2023\)](#page-11-8) provide a Or(k) sample complexity for learning single index models with q <sup>⋆</sup> = 1 using diagonal linear networks, and [Neykov et al.](#page-12-11) [\(2016\)](#page-12-11) report a Or(k 2 ) result for phase retrieval where q <sup>⋆</sup> = 2. However, as previously noted, the information exponent does not fully characterize the intrinsic computational-statistical tradeoff. Our work completes the picture by providing a gradient-based framework that simultaneously handles all sparsity levels and any generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1.

On the other hand, [Ba et al.](#page-10-10) [\(2023\)](#page-10-10) and [Mousavi-Hosseini et al.](#page-12-17) [\(2023\)](#page-12-17) demonstrate that additional structures in the data, such as spiked covariance in the input, can break the d p ⋆ sample complexity lower bound compared to the standard Gaussian single-index model. A promising direction for future research is to explore whether similar structural properties could be utilized to surpass the d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 sample complexity lower bound in the SQ setting.

Other works show manifold method can break CSQ or SQ lower bounds. [Mousavi-Hosseini et al.](#page-12-18) [\(2024\)](#page-12-18) investigate learning multi-index models using two-layer neural networks trained with the Mean-Field Langevin Algorithm (MFLA). In a Riemannian setting, constraining weights to a compact manifold allows MFLA to achieve polynomial-time convergence while surpassing the CSQ sample complexity lower bound.

## C S U P P L E M E N TA RY P R O O F S F O R T H E M A I N C O N T E X T

## C . 1 P R O O F S F O R S E C T I O N [3](#page-3-3)

In this section, we first argue why <sup>E</sup>x∼N(0,1)[yζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y)] = 0 is the major difficulty for vanilla (stochastic) gradient descent to achieve the information-theoretical lower bound O(d) (the same for SQ lower bound) when the information exponent q ⋆ is larger than 2. It has been shown by [Damian et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4) that the generative exponent s ⋆ for polynomial model is either 1 or 2. Consider the information exponent q <sup>⋆</sup> > 2. We have the following lemma saying that the correlation <sup>E</sup>x∼N(0,1)[yζs(y)] = 0 for any s < q<sup>⋆</sup> .

Lemma C.1. *Recall that* ζ<sup>s</sup> *is the coefficient function for degree* s *in the decomposition of the likelihood ratio* <sup>P</sup>(x, y)/Q(x, y) *in* [\(2.2\)](#page-2-2)*. For any* q <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2*, consider the Gaussian single-index model given by* y = β<sup>0</sup> + P <sup>p</sup>≥p<sup>⋆</sup> βphp(x) *with* <sup>x</sup> ∼ N (0, 1)*. Then for any* <sup>1</sup> ≤ s < p<sup>⋆</sup> *,* <sup>E</sup>Q[yζs(y)] = 0*.*

*Proof.* The proof can be done by noting that ζs(y) = <sup>E</sup>Q[P(x, y)/Q(x, y) · hs(x)| y], and

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[y\zeta_s(y)] &= \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[y \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{P}(x,y)}{\mathbb{Q}(x,y)} \cdot h_s(x) \mid y\right]\right] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[y \cdot h_s(x)] \\ &= \beta_0 \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)}[h_s(x)] + \sum_{i \geq p^*} \beta_i \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)}[h_i(x)h_s(x)] = 0,\end{aligned}$$

where the second equality follows from the independence between x and y under Q.

Therefore, the first nonzero term in the informative queries of [\(3.1\)](#page-3-1) is of order at least p ⋆ . This gives rise to sample complexity d p <sup>⋆</sup>−1 for vanilla online SGD [\(Arous et al.,](#page-10-3) [2021\)](#page-10-3) and d p <sup>⋆</sup>/2 for SGD after smoothing the landscape [\(Damian et al.,](#page-11-5) [2023\)](#page-11-5). This sample complexity d p <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> matches the correlated statistical query (CSQ) lower bound with gradient of the form yϕ(z) [\(Abbe et al.,](#page-10-9) [2023;](#page-10-9) [Damian](#page-11-11) [et al.,](#page-11-11) [2022\)](#page-11-11).

#### C . 1 . 1 E Q U I VA L E N C E B E T W E E N D I R E C T L A B E L T R A N S F O R M AT I O N A N D M O D I F I E D L O S S F U N C T I O N

In this derivation, we focus on a single neuron f(z; θ, a) = aσ(⟨θ, z⟩) with θ ∈ S d−1 and sufficiently small a ∈ R. A more general case with multiple neurons is handled in the proof of [Example 4.6](#page-7-0) in [Appendix C.2.2.](#page-27-0) We consider the following cases:

- (i) L<sup>2</sup> loss with direct label transformation y → T (y).
- (ii) Label transformation with modified loss function ℓ(f(x), y).

Let ℓ ′ (f, y) denote the partial derivative of ℓ(f, y) with respect to f. We have the following observations:

Lemma C.2. *Take* T (·) = ℓ ′ (0, ·)*. Assume* ℓ ′ (f, ·) *is* B*-Lipschitz with respect to* f*. The gradients obtained from both cases (i) and (ii) are the same up to a small error term that scales with* a*.*

*Proof.* For case (i), the rescaled negative gradient is obtained as

$$\begin{aligned} g &= -a^{-1} \cdot \nabla_{\theta}(\mathcal{T}(y) - f(z; \theta, a))^2 = (\mathcal{T}(y) - f(z; \theta, a)) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z \\ &= \mathcal{T}(y) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z - a \cdot \sigma(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z \end{aligned}$$

For case (ii), the gradient is given by

$$\begin{aligned} g &= -a^{-1} \cdot \nabla_{\theta} \ell(f(z; \theta, a), y) = -\ell'(f(z; \theta, a), y) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z \\ &= -\ell'(0, y) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z + a \cdot (\ell'(0, y) - \ell'(f(z; \theta, a), y)) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z, \end{aligned}$$

where |ℓ ′ (0, y) − ℓ ′ (f(z; θ, a), y)| ≤ |a · B · σ(⟨θ, z⟩)|. Therefore, in the following we just focus on case (i) and omit the error term that scales with a for simplicity.

The assumption on the Lipschitz constant B is just for the sake of simplicity, which is not required in our main results.

#### C . 1 . 2 M O M E N T C A L C U L AT I O N A R O U N D I N I T I A L I Z AT I O N

Without Weight Perturbation. Consider gradient g = T (y)σ ′ (⟨θ, z⟩ · z for a single neuron with weight θ ∈ S d−1 and input z ∈ R d , where we omit the error term for simplicity. The first moment of the gradient is given by

$$\begin{aligned} & \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\mathcal{T}(y) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z] \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ \mathcal{T}(y) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z \cdot \left( 1 + \sum_{s \geq s^*} \zeta_s(y) h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \right) \right] \\ &= \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\mathcal{T}(y) \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot z]}_{\text{bias}} + \underbrace{\sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\mathcal{T}(y) \zeta_s(y)] \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) z]}_{\text{informative queries}}. \end{aligned}$$

The right-hand side of the above equation gives rise to [\(3.1\)](#page-3-1) in the main text. From the above derivation, we can see clearly that using a direct label transformation or a modified loss function can handle the zero correlation issue <sup>E</sup>Q[yζs(y)] = 0 for the informative queries. Invoking [Lemma H.1](#page-66-0) with ψ(y,⟨w, z⟩) = σ ′ (⟨θ, z⟩) where we replace w → θ and θ → θ ⋆ in the lemma statement, we obtain

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \cdot h_s(\langle \theta^\star, z \rangle) \cdot z] = \sqrt{s+1} \cdot \widehat{(\sigma')}_{s+1} \cdot \langle \theta, \theta^\star \rangle^s \cdot \theta + \sqrt{s} \cdot \widehat{(\sigma')}_{s-1} \cdot \langle \theta, \theta^\star \rangle^{s-1} \cdot \theta^\star,$$

where (y<sup>σ</sup> ′) s is the s-th coefficient of the Hermite expansion of σ ′ , and we have by a simple calculation that (y<sup>σ</sup> ′) <sup>s</sup> = √ s + 1 · σps+1. Since we are only interested in the alignment between the informative queries and the unknown signal θ ⋆ , we only need to focus on the second term for s = s ⋆ as the remaining terms are of higher order in ⟨θ, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ = d −1/2 at initialization. With a debiasing procedure (Line [6](#page-5-3) in [Algorithm 1\)](#page-5-0), we can also cancel the bias term in the first moment of the gradient. For simplicity, we just assume <sup>E</sup>Q[T (y)] = 0 in the following calculations while the more general version is handled in the proofs of both [Theorem 4.2](#page-6-0) and [Theorem 5.1.](#page-9-3) This gives rise to the first moment

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle g, \theta^* \rangle] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\mathcal{T}(y)\zeta_s(y)] \cdot s^* \cdot \hat{\sigma}_{s^*} \cdot \langle \theta, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1} + O(d^{-s^*/2}) = O(d^{-(s^*-1)/2}). \quad (\text{C.1})$$

For the second moment, we have

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\|g\|_2^2] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\mathcal{T}(y)^2 \cdot \sigma'(\langle \theta, z \rangle)^2 \cdot \|z\|_2^2] = O(d),$$

where ∥z∥ 2 2 contributes to the O(d) term.

With Weight Perturbation. Let w<sup>l</sup> = (γθ + ξl)/∥γθ + ξl∥<sup>2</sup> for l ∈ [M] with ξ<sup>l</sup> ∼ Unif(<sup>S</sup> d−1 ) and γ = d −1/4 . As the norm ∥γθ + ξl∥<sup>2</sup> ∈ [1 − γ, 1 + γ] thanks to the triangle inequality, we can just focus on numerator, i.e., w<sup>l</sup> ≈ γθ + ξ<sup>l</sup> . For given w<sup>l</sup> , we denote the previous calculated gradient as g<sup>l</sup> , i.e., g<sup>l</sup> = T (y)σ ′ (⟨w<sup>l</sup> , z⟩) · z. Then, the aggregated gradient over all L perturbed weights is given by g = L P<sup>L</sup> <sup>l</sup>=1 g<sup>l</sup> . For the following calculation, we focus on s <sup>⋆</sup> being an odd number and larger than 1 for simplicity. In the formal proof of [Theorem 4.2,](#page-6-0) we handle the remaining cases separately. Following from the same calculation for [\(C.1\)](#page-20-0), the first moment now becomes

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle g, \theta^* \rangle] = \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \underbrace{\widehat{\sigma}_{s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\mathcal{T}(y)\zeta_s(y)]}_{\neq 0} \cdot s^* \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1} + O(d^{-s^*/2}) = O(d^{-(s^*-1)/2}), \quad (\text{C.2})$$

which gives the same order as [\(C.1\)](#page-20-0). The things are quite different for the second moment. Note that in the first moment, only the s ⋆ -th coefficient of the Hermite expansion of σ contributes to the alignment with θ ⋆ , while the lower order terms are canceled out due to the fact that the likelihood ratio <sup>P</sup>(x, y)/Q(x, y) has the lowest nonzero order s ⋆ . The second moment is now given by

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\|g\|_2^2] &= \frac{1}{L^2} \sum_{l,l'=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\mathcal{T}(y)^2 \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_l, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{l'}, z \rangle) \cdot \|z\|_2^2] \\ &\simeq \frac{d}{L^2} \sum_{l,l'=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\mathcal{T}(y)^2 \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_l, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{l'}, z \rangle)].\end{aligned}$$

The approximation in the second line is a heuristic calculation for the second moment, and the rigorous version is available in the proof of [Proposition E.4](#page-33-0) in [Appendix E.3.](#page-41-0) Now, we plug in the likelihood ratio <sup>P</sup>(x, y)/Q(x, y), and under certain regularity assumption (See Item [Assumption 4.1\(c\)](#page-6-4) for details) we obtain

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\|g\|_2^2] &\simeq \frac{d}{L^2} \sum_{l,l'=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ \mathcal{T}(y)^2 \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_l, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{l'}, z \rangle) \cdot \left( 1 + \sum_{s \geq s^*} \zeta_s(y) h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \right) \right] \\ &= \frac{d}{L^2} \sum_{l,l'=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\mathcal{T}(y)^2] \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\sigma'(\langle w_l, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{l'}, z \rangle)] \\ &\quad + \frac{d}{L^2} \sum_{l,l'=1}^L \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\mathcal{T}(y)^2 \zeta_s(y)] \cdot \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\sigma'(\langle w_l, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{l'}, z \rangle) \cdot h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle)]}_{\text{higher order term}}.\end{aligned}\tag{C.3}$$

Here, we remark that when comparing the two terms on the right-hand side, the higher-order terms s ≥ s ⋆ are of order at least d · d −s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> due to the fact that both <sup>w</sup><sup>l</sup> and <sup>w</sup><sup>l</sup> ′ have a correlation with θ ⋆ of order d −1/2 at initialization. Therefore, when considering the first term in [\(C.3\)](#page-21-1) only, we have

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_\theta^*}[\|g\|_2^2] \simeq \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\mathcal{T}(y)^2] \cdot \left( \frac{d}{L^2} \cdot \sum_{l=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\sigma'(\langle w_l, z \rangle)^2] + \frac{d}{L^2} \cdot \sum_{l \neq l'} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\sigma'(\langle w_l, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{l'}, z \rangle)] \right).$$

The first term in the bracket is of order d/L since <sup>E</sup>Q[σ ′ (⟨w<sup>l</sup> , z⟩) 2 ] = O(1). With large L, the main contribution comes from the second term in the bracket. Suppose the lowest nonzero Hermite coefficient of σ is r, then we have by noting that <sup>E</sup>ξl,ξ<sup>l</sup> [⟨w<sup>l</sup> , w<sup>l</sup> ′ ⟩ r ] ≤ O(γ <sup>2</sup><sup>r</sup> <sup>+</sup>Eξl,ξ<sup>l</sup> [|⟨ξ<sup>l</sup> , ξ<sup>l</sup> ′ ⟩|<sup>r</sup> ]) = O d −r/2 for constant r and hence

$$\frac{d}{L^2} \cdot \sum_{l \neq l'} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\sigma'(\langle w_l, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{l'}, z \rangle)] \simeq \frac{d}{L^2} \cdot \sum_{l \neq l'} \langle w_l, w_{l'} \rangle^{r-1} \leq O(d^{-(r-3)/2}), \quad (\text{C.4})$$

where in the first approximation, we ignore the higher order terms. One would like to pick the largest possible r, which will be s ⋆ as [\(C.2\)](#page-20-1) requires σp<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> ̸= 0. This leads to the second moment

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\|g\|_2^2] = O(d^{-(s^*-3)/2}).$$

The case for r < s<sup>⋆</sup> will be discussed ilater. Here, we remark that the label transformation T (·) is only changing the second moment up to some constant factor as the only difference that occurs for not using label transformation would be changing <sup>E</sup>Q[T (y) 2 ] to <sup>E</sup>Q[y 2 ] in the above calculation. This is also the reason why we can stick to the standard L<sup>2</sup> loss in the discussion of the weight perturbation in [Section 3](#page-3-3) for showing how the weight perturbation can help boost the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) in the second moment.

#### C . 1 . 3 H E U R I S T I C D E R I VAT I O N O F S A M P L E C O M P L E X I T Y

In the following, we first discuss what is the thresholding SNR for developing a O(1) alignment between the neuron weight θ and the unknown signal θ ⋆ . Then, we provide a heuristic derivation of the sample complexity O(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) for the case with weight perturbation. We still focus on the two-layer neural network with a single neuron f(z; θ, a) = aσ(⟨θ, z⟩) as in the previous discussion. We asssume the following to hold in the following discussion:

- (i) s ⋆ is an odd number greater than 1.
- (ii) The activation function σ satisfies the high-pass assumption, i.e., the lowest nonzero Hermite coefficient of σ is r.
- (iii) The alignment |⟨θ, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| = o(1) (to ensure the previous simplified results are valid).
- (iv) The label transformation T (·) is such that <sup>E</sup>Q[T (y)] = 0 and <sup>E</sup>Q[T (y)ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y)] ̸= 0.
- (v) The number of perturbation L is sufficiently large.

Sample Complexity from the View of SNR Recursion. Let us first consider r = s ⋆ , i.e., [Assump](#page-6-3)[tion 4.1\(b\)](#page-6-3) holds. At step t, let the weight be g (t−1)/∥g (t−1)∥<sup>2</sup> and the perturbed weight be

$$w_l^{(t)} = \frac{\gamma \cdot g^{(t-1)} / \|g^{(t-1)}\|_2 + \xi_l^{(t)}}{\|\gamma \cdot g^{(t-1)} / \|g^{(t-1)}\|_2 + \xi_l^{(t)}\|_2}$$

with ξ (t) <sup>l</sup> ∼ Unif(<sup>S</sup> d−1 ). Following the same procedure as in [\(C.2\)](#page-20-1), we have the first moment of the gradient as
