$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle g^{(t)}, \theta^* \rangle] \simeq \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l^{(t)}, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1} \simeq (\gamma \cdot |\langle g^{(t-1)} / \|g^{(t-1)} \|_2, \theta^* \rangle| + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1},$$

where the last equality follows from the fact that s <sup>⋆</sup> −1 is an even number, and ≃ hides some constant factors. A proof of this calculation can be found in [Lemma H.4.](#page-70-0) Following the similar derivation in [\(C.3\)](#page-21-1)-[\(C.4\)](#page-21-2), the second moment is given by

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\|g^{(t)}\|_2^2] \simeq \frac{d}{L^2} \cdot \sum_{l \neq l'} \langle w_l, w_{l'} \rangle^{r-1} \Big|_{r=s^*} \simeq d \cdot (\gamma^2 + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1},$$

where ≃ hides some constant factors that depends on s ⋆ . The last equality follows from the observation that ⟨w<sup>l</sup> , w<sup>l</sup> ′ ⟩ s <sup>⋆</sup>−<sup>1</sup> = p ⟨w<sup>l</sup> , w<sup>l</sup> ′ ⟩ 2 s <sup>⋆</sup>−1 ≃ p γ <sup>4</sup> + ⟨ξ<sup>l</sup> , ξ<sup>l</sup> ′ ⟩ 2 s <sup>⋆</sup>−1 ≃ (γ <sup>2</sup> + |⟨ξ<sup>l</sup> , ξ<sup>l</sup> ′ ⟩|) s <sup>⋆</sup>−1 , and a concentration of |⟨ξ<sup>l</sup> , ξ<sup>l</sup> ′ ⟩|<sup>s</sup> for s ≤ s <sup>⋆</sup> − 1. Hence, the one-sample SNR is given by

$$\text{SNR}^{(t)} = \frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [|g^{(t)}, \theta^*|^2]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\|g^{(t)}\|_2^2]} = \frac{C}{d} \cdot \left( \frac{\gamma^2 \cdot |g^{(t-1)}| / \|g^{(t-1)}\|_2, \theta^*|^2 + d^{-1}}{\gamma^2 + d^{-1/2}} \right)^{s^*-1},$$

where C is some constant factor that depends on s ⋆ . One can replace |⟨g (t−1)/∥g (t−1)∥2, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩|<sup>2</sup> by nSNR(t−1) as the square root of the n sample SNR is roughly the alignment between the gradient and the unknown signal. Under this replacement, we always have nSNR(t−1) = o(1) by our assumption (iii). We also obtain the recursion

$$nd^{1/2} \cdot \text{SNR}^{(t)} = \frac{Cn}{d^{s^*/2}} \cdot \left( \frac{\gamma^2 d^{1/2} \cdot nd^{1/2} \cdot \text{SNR}^{(t-1)} + 1}{\gamma^2 d^{1/2} + 1} \right)^{s^*-1}.$$

Viewing the term inside the bracket as a function of x = γ 2d 1/2 , and taking α (t) = nd<sup>1</sup>/<sup>2</sup> · SNR(t) , we have

$$f(x; \alpha) = \frac{\alpha x + 1}{x + 1}.$$

For α < 1, f is a decreasing function of x, and for α > 1, f is an increasing function of x. At initialization, the alignment is of order d −1/2 , which means α (0) = nd<sup>1</sup>/<sup>2</sup>SNR(0) = O(d −1/2 ) ≪ 1. The best choice is x = 0 and f(x; α (0)) = 1, and the corresponding next-step SNR is given by

$$\alpha^{(1)} = \frac{Cn}{d^{s^*/2}}.$$

If n ≪ d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 , then α (1) ≪ 1, and the same argument applies for α (2) ≪ 1 and so on. This means that the alignment cannot break the barrier O(d −1/4 ). On the other hand, when n ≫ d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 , we have α (1) ≫ 1. Suppose we can choose x as large as possible such that f(x; α (1)) = α (1), then we have another recursion:

$$\alpha^{(t)} = \frac{Cn}{d^{s^*/2}} \cdot (\alpha^{(t-1)})^{s^*-1},$$

which means that the alignment can grow exponentially with t.

Polarization Level as a Trade-off. From the above discussion, we can see that when α ≪ 1, we want to choose x as small as possible such that f(x; α) = 1 and when α ≫ 1, we want to choose x as large as possible such that f(x; α) = α. In fact, setting x = 1, i.e., γ = d −1/4 , is a good choice to balace the two cases, as it is easy to check that

$$f(1; \alpha) = \begin{cases} \frac{1}{2}, & \text{if } \alpha \ll 1, \\ \frac{\alpha}{2}, & \text{if } \alpha \gg 1, \end{cases}$$

Heuristic Derivation of Sample Complexity from Alignment. Previously, we derive from the SNR recursion that why O(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) samples are necessary to achieve nontrivial alignment with weight perturbation and why γ = d −1/4 is a good choice for the polarization level. Now, we provide an alternative view to understand the sample complexity d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 from the perspective of the alignment between the perturbed weight and the unknown signal θ ⋆ . The following discussion is more heuristic in nature when compared to the SNR recursion. We still follow the assumptions (i)-(v) as in the previous discussion. We set γ = d −1/4 .

Consider the case of an infinitely large learning rate η, where at each step, the weight is approximately given by the gradient from the previous step, i.e., w ≈ γg/∥g∥<sup>2</sup> + ξ, where g is the gradient from the previous step, and ξ is a perturbed noise. Here, our goal is for the *perturbed weight* to exhibit nontrivial alignment with θ ⋆ . Specifically, we require

$$\langle w, \theta^* \rangle \approx \gamma \cdot \langle g/\|g\|_2, \theta^* \rangle + \langle \xi, \theta^* \rangle \gg O(d^{-1/2}).$$

Since ⟨ξ, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ is of order d −1/2 , it follows that we need γ · ⟨g/∥g∥2, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ ≫ O(d −1/2 ). This translates into the condition √ nSNR ≫ d −1/4 . From our previous moment calculations, we know that

$$\text{SNR} = \frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle g, \theta^* \rangle]^2}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\|g\|_2^2]} = O\left(\frac{d^{-(s^*-1)}}{d^{-(s^*-3)/2}}\right) = O(d^{-(s^*+1)/2}),$$

which leads to the condition n = Or(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) when combined with √ nSNR ≫ d −1/4 .

The rationale behind this criterion is that if γ · ⟨g, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ does not sufficiently exceed ⟨ξ, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩, then the perturbed noise will dominate. In such a case, the successive gradient updates would not provide more information than those derived from random initialization.

#### C . 1 . 4 FA I L U R E O F H I G H - PA S S A S S U M P T I O N

Previously, we heuristically derive the sample complexity O(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) for the case with weight perturbation and the activation function σ satisfying the high-pass assumption, i.e., r = s ⋆ . Here, we discuss what happens when the high-pass assumption is violated, i.e., 1 ≤ r < s<sup>⋆</sup> . We still focus on s <sup>⋆</sup> being an odd number greater than 1. Here, we have r ≥ 1 as by definition, r is the lowest nonzero Hermite coefficient of σ. This is akin to the case where [Assumption 4.1\(b\)](#page-6-3) is not satisfied. We still assume that σp<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> ̸= 0 as this is needed for the signal term to survive in the first moment calculation. Using the second moment calculation in [\(C.4\)](#page-21-2), we have

$$\text{SNR} = \frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle g, \theta^* \rangle]^2}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\|g\|_2^2]} = O\left(\frac{d^{-(s^*-1)}}{d^{-(r-3)/2}}\right) = O(d^{-(2s^*-r+1)/2}).$$

Combined with the same condition √ nSNR ≫ d −1/4 , we obtain n = Or(d (2s <sup>⋆</sup>−r)/2 ). Hence, for general 1 ≤ r ≤ s ⋆ , the sample complexity interpolates between O(d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ) and O(d s <sup>⋆</sup>−1/2 ). We would like to point out that the above calculation is heuristic, and a formal proof is left for future work. However, we believe that the above calculation provides sufficient insight on the sample complexity when the high-pass assumption is violated.

On the other hand, if r ≥ s ⋆ , this is equivalent to the case where the generative exponent is r (as the terms in the decomposition of the likelihood ratio with degree less than r don't have any effect on both the first and second moment). Hence, we obtain a sample complexity n = Or(d r/2 ). In summary, the sample complexity is

$$n \simeq d^{((2s^\star - r) \vee r)/2}.$$

Notably, if the activation has the lowest nonzero degree r, the oracle ψ, defined as ψ(y, x) = T (y)σ ′ (x), has the lowest degree r − 1.

#### C . 2 . 1 B AT C H - R E U S I N G F O R P O LY N O M I A L L I N K F U N C T I O N

We consider a polynomial link function y = p(x) = P q <sup>⋆</sup>≤q ′≤q βq ′h<sup>q</sup> ′ (x) for general q <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup> and βq ′ ∈ R, where q ⋆ is the information exponent of the link function, and we also denote it by q ⋆ (p) in the sequel. For batch-reusing, we take ψ(y, x) = yσ′ (x) + yσ′ (x + yσ′ (x)), where activation function σ(x) satisfies that

$$\sigma(x) = \sum_{j=0}^{C_q} \alpha_j \cdot h_j(x), \quad \sigma'(x) = \sum_{j=1}^{C_q} \sqrt{j} \cdot \alpha_j \cdot h_{j-1}(x). \quad (\text{C.5})$$

Here the degree C<sup>q</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup><sup>+</sup> only depends on the degree q of the link function and is specified later, and each coefficient α<sup>j</sup> ∼ Unif([0, 1]). The second equality in [\(C.5\)](#page-22-0) follows from the property of Hermite polynomials in [\(B.1\)](#page-17-2). The error term err (t) m,l,i now comes from the difference between ψ(y (t) i ,⟨w (t) m,l, z (t) i ⟩) · z (t) i and the exact form of the update step obtained from two consecutive gradient descent steps on the same data under the square loss. More specifically, let us consider a single neuron whose weight is wm,l and a single data point (z<sup>i</sup> , yi). Here we omit the time index t for convenience. Then two gradient descent step on (z<sup>i</sup> , yi) gives

$$\begin{aligned} -g_{m,l}^{\text{Re}}(z_i, y_i) &= (y_i - f(z_i; \{w_{m,l}\}_{m \in [M]})) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}, z_i \rangle) \cdot z_i \\ &\quad + (y_i - f(z_i; \{w_{m,l}^+\}_{m \in [M]})) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}^+, z_i \rangle) \cdot z_i, \end{aligned} \quad (\text{C.6})$$

where w + m,l = wm,l + η Re i ·(y<sup>i</sup> − f(z<sup>i</sup> ; {wm,l}m∈[M]))· σ ′ (⟨wm,l, zi⟩)· z<sup>i</sup> . Here η Re i is the learning rate for batch reusing, different from the learning rate η in our algorithm. More specifically, to fit the gradient form [\(C.6\)](#page-24-1) into our general framework with oracle function ψ(y, x), we take the batch-reusing learning rate η Re <sup>i</sup> = 1/∥zi∥ 2 2 . Then the error term is given by

$$\text{err}_{m,l,i} = -g_{m,l}^{\text{Re}}(z_i, y_i) - \psi(y_i, \langle w_{m,l}, z_i \rangle) = \text{err}_{m,l,i,1} + \text{err}_{m,l,i,2} + \text{err}_{m,l,i,3}, \quad (\text{C.7})$$

where errm,l,i,1, errm,l,i,2, and errm,l,i,<sup>3</sup> are given by

$$\begin{aligned} \text{err}_{m,l,i,1} &= -f(z_i; \{w_{m,l}\}_{m \in [M]}) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}, z_i \rangle), \\ \text{err}_{m,l,i,2} &= -f(z_i; \{w_{m,l}^+\}_{m \in [M]}) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}^+, z_i \rangle), \\ \text{err}_{m,l,i,3} &= y_i \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}, z_i \rangle + y_i \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}, z_i \rangle) + \text{err}_{m,l,i,1}) \\ &\quad - y_i \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}, z_i \rangle + y_i \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}, z_i \rangle)). \end{aligned} \tag{C.8}$$

*Proof of Corollary [4.5.](#page-7-6)* To prove Corollary [4.5,](#page-7-6) it suffices to show that (i) [Assumption 4.1](#page-6-2) holds, and (ii) the event E holds with the desired high probability. In the following, we first verify [Assumption 4.1,](#page-6-2) and then check the event E.

Verifying [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2) Note that the fact of y = p(x) being a polynomial immediately implies that both the square-integrable condition [\(Assumption 4.1\(a\)\)](#page-6-1) and the polynomial-like tail condition [\(Assumption 4.1\(c\)\)](#page-6-4) are satisfied. It remains to check the high-pass condition [\(Assumption 4.1\(b\)\)](#page-6-3). Since now s <sup>⋆</sup> ≤ 2, we only need to check the condition that |<sup>E</sup>Q[ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y) · <sup>ψ</sup>p<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup>−1(y)]| > 0.

Case 1: s <sup>⋆</sup> = 2. In this case, we have that

$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\psi}_1(y) &= \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ x \cdot \left( y \sigma'(x) + y \sigma'(x + y \sigma'(x)) \right) \right] \\ &= y \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ \sum_{j=1}^{C_q} \sqrt{j} \cdot \alpha_j \cdot x \cdot h_{j-1}(x) + \sum_{j=1}^{C_q} \sqrt{j} \cdot \alpha_j \cdot x \cdot h_{j-1}(x + y \sigma'(x)) \right] \quad (\text{C.9}) \end{aligned}$$

For the first summation in [\(C.9\)](#page-24-2), only the first summand is nonzero, so we obtain

$$y \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ \sum_{j=1}^{C_q} \sqrt{j} \cdot \alpha_j \cdot x \cdot h_{j-1}(x) \right] = \sqrt{2} \alpha_2 \cdot y. \quad (\text{C.10})$$

For the second summation in [\(C.9\)](#page-24-2), we have the following expansion,

$$y \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ \sum_{j=1}^{C_q} j \cdot \alpha_j \cdot x \cdot h_{j-1}(x + y\sigma'(x)) \right]$$

$$\begin{aligned} &= y \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ \sum_{j=1}^{C_q} j \cdot \alpha_j \cdot x \cdot \sum_{k=0}^{j-1} r_{j-1,k} \cdot h_{j-k-1}(x) \cdot (y\sigma'(x))^k \right] \\ &= \sum_{k=0}^{C_q-1} \left\{ \underbrace{\sum_{j=k+1}^{C_q} j \cdot \alpha_j \cdot r_{j-1,k} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ x \cdot h_{j-k-1}(x) \cdot (\sigma'(x))^k \right]}_{:= \varsigma_k(\alpha)} \right\} \cdot y^{k+1}. \end{aligned} \quad (\text{C.11})$$

where ς0(α), . . . , ςCq−1(α) are just polynomials of α = (α1, · · · , αC<sup>q</sup> ) (recall the definition of σ ′ (x) in [\(C.5\)](#page-22-0)) and each rj−1,k is a positive number. Combining [\(C.10\)](#page-24-3) and [\(C.11\)](#page-25-1), we get the following decomposition of <sup>ψ</sup>p1(y):

$$\hat{\psi}_1(y) = \sqrt{2}\alpha_2 \cdot y + \sum_{k=0}^{C_q-1} \varsigma_k(\alpha) \cdot y^{k+1}.$$

Further using y = p(x) and the definition of ζ2(y), we get

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_2(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_1(y)] &= \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h_2(x) \cdot \hat{\psi}_1(y)] \\ &= \sqrt{2}\alpha_2 \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}}[h_2(x) \cdot p(x)] + \sum_{k=0}^{C_g-1} \varsigma_k(\alpha) \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}}[h_2(x) \cdot p(x)^{k+1}] .\end{aligned}$$

According to Proposition 5 of [Lee et al.](#page-12-4) [\(2024\)](#page-12-4), we can set C<sup>q</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup> <sup>+</sup> (only depending on q) such that there exists a smallest I ≤ C<sup>q</sup> such that the information exponent q ⋆ (p I ) ≤ 2. We notice that in this case s ⋆ (p) = 2, where we abuse the notation and let s ⋆ (p) be the generative exponent of the polynomial p. In fact, s ⋆ (p) = 1 means <sup>E</sup>P[T (y) · h1(x)] ≡ 0 for all label transformation T . Hence, the only possibility is that q ⋆ (p I ) = 2 since p I is just a special case of label transformation and we cannot get any first-order term from p I . Therefore, we further simplify the target quantity as

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s^{*-1}}(y)] &= \sqrt{2}\alpha_2 \cdot \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}}[h_2(x) \cdot p(x)]}_{:=b_1} + \underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{C_q} \varsigma_{k-1}(\alpha) \cdot \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}}[h_2(x) \cdot p(x)^k]}_{:=b_k}}_{:=b_k} \\ &= b_1 \cdot \sqrt{2}\alpha_2 + \sum_{k=1}^{C_q} b_k \cdot \varsigma_{k-1}(\alpha),\end{aligned}\tag{C.12}$$

where b<sup>I</sup> ̸= 0 according to the definition of I. Now we take a closer look at the polynomials {ςk(α1, · · · , αC<sup>q</sup> )} Cq−1 k=I−1 . We claim that: (i) they are different polynomials and are linearly independent, and (ii) especially, they are all nonzero. To see these, recall that

$$s_k(\alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_{C_q}) = \sum_{j=k+1}^{C_q} \sqrt{j} \cdot \alpha_j \cdot r_{j-1,k} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ x \cdot h_{j-k-1}(x) \cdot (\sigma'(x))^k \right],$$

where σ ′ (x) = P<sup>C</sup><sup>q</sup> j=1 √ j · α<sup>j</sup> · hj−1(x). We can calculate that for k = 0, ς0(α1, · · · , αC<sup>q</sup> ) = √ 2α<sup>2</sup> which is a non-zero polynomial. For k = 1, we have that

$$\begin{aligned} \varsigma_1(\alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_{C_q}) &= \sum_{j=2}^{C_q} \sqrt{j} \cdot \alpha_j \cdot r_{j-1,1} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}}[x \cdot h_{j-2}(x) \cdot \sigma'(x)] \\ &= 2r_{1,1} \cdot \alpha_2^2 + \sum_{j=3}^{C_q} \sqrt{j} \cdot \alpha_j \cdot r_{j-1,1} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}}[x \cdot h_{j-2}(x) \cdot \sigma'(x)] \end{aligned}$$

which is non-zero (since in the summation from j = 3 to C<sup>q</sup> there would be no term in the form of c 2 ) and is linearly independent of of ς<sup>0</sup> because each terms in the summation here has degree exactly 2. Now consider for k ≥ 2,

$$\varsigma_k(\alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_{C_q}) = \sqrt{2(k+1)}k \cdot r_{k,k} \cdot \alpha_1^{k-1} \alpha_2 \alpha_{k+1}$$

$$+ \sum_{j=k+2}^{C_q} \sqrt{j} \alpha_j r_{j-1,k} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ x \cdot h_{j-k-1}(x) \cdot (\sigma'(x))^k \right].$$

Again, this polynomial is non-zero (since in the summation from j = k + 2 to C<sup>q</sup> there would be no term in the form of α k−1 <sup>1</sup> α2αk+1) and is linearly independent of ς0, · · · , ςk−<sup>1</sup> due to the fact that the highest degree of these polynomials is no larger than k, and the order for each term in ς<sup>k</sup> is exactly k + 1. Thus we have proved the two claims by induction. Now recall that we are aiming at proving the RHS of [\(C.12\)](#page-25-2) is non-zero. By our two claims just proved, the RHS of [\(C.12\)](#page-25-2) is a linear combination of C<sup>q</sup> − I + 2 linearly independent and non-zero polynomials where at least one of the combination coefficient is non-zero (which is b<sup>I</sup> ). Thus we obtain that the RHS of [\(C.9\)](#page-24-2) is a non-zero polynomial of (α1, · · · , αC<sup>q</sup> ) and its zeros form a zero-measure set. This proves that with probability 1 over the randomness of (α1, · · · , αC<sup>q</sup> ), the high-pass condition holds.

Case 2: s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1. For this case of s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1, the proof is almost the same as that for s <sup>⋆</sup> = 2, where we additionally utilize the fact that polynomial link function with generative exponent s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1 can not be an even polynomial [\(Example 2.2\)](#page-2-0) and thus there always exists some I ≤ C<sup>q</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup><sup>+</sup> such that the information exponent q ⋆ (p I ) = 1 (see Proposition 5 of [Lee et al.](#page-12-4) [\(2024\)](#page-12-4)). With this fact, repeating the above argument can give the desired high-pass property.

Verifying the event E. Now we verify that the desired event

$$\mathcal{E} = \{|\text{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}| \leq d^{-10s^*}, \forall (m,l,i,t) \in [M] \times [L] \times [n] \times [T]\}$$

holds with probability at least 1 − O(d <sup>−</sup>c<sup>0</sup> ) for some constant c<sup>0</sup> > 0 that we specify later. With [\(C.7\)](#page-24-4) and [\(C.8\)](#page-24-5), it suffices to look at each of the error terms err (t) m,l,i,1 , err (t) m,l,i,2 , and err (t) m,l,i,3 respectively. For err (t) m,l,i,1 ,

$$\left| \operatorname{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)} \right| \leq \sum_{m'=1}^M |a_{m'}| \cdot \left| \sigma(\langle w_{m',l}^{(t)}, z_i \rangle) \right| \cdot \left| \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i \rangle) \right| \quad (\text{C.13})$$

Note that {⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩}m∈[M] are standard Gaussians since {w (t) m,l}m∈[M] ⊂ <sup>S</sup> d−1 . Therefore, with probability at least 1 − d −c<sup>0</sup> for some constant c<sup>0</sup> > 0, we have that |⟨wm,l, zi⟩| = Or(1). Meanwhile, since that σ and σ ′ are both polynomials with constant order and bounded coefficients, and that M = O(d), then by taking a<sup>m</sup> = d −11s ⋆ , we conclude from [\(C.13\)](#page-26-1) that

$$|\operatorname{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}| \leq \tilde{O}(d^{-10s^*}). \quad (\text{C.14})$$

For the second error term err (t) m,l,i,2 , similarly we have that

$$\left| \operatorname{err}_{m,l,i,2}^{(t)} \right| \leq \sum_{m' \in [M]} |a_{m'}| \cdot \left| \sigma(\langle \langle w_{m',l}^{(t)} \rangle^+, z_i \rangle) \right| \cdot \left| \sigma'(\langle \langle w_{m,l}^{(t)} \rangle^+, z_i \rangle) \right|. \quad (\text{C.15})$$

Note that the one-step updated weights satisfy that

$$\left| \langle (w_{m,l}^{(t)})^+, z_i \rangle \right| = \left| \langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i \rangle + y_i \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i \rangle) + \text{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)} \right| = \tilde{O}(1), \quad (\text{C.16})$$

with probability at least 1 − d <sup>−</sup>c<sup>0</sup> since y<sup>i</sup> = p(⟨θ ⋆ , zi⟩) and p is also a polynomial of constant degree and coefficients. Therefore, with the choice of am's, by [\(C.15\)](#page-26-2), we conclude that

$$\left| \text{err}_{m,l,i,2}^{(t)} \right| \leq \tilde{O}(d^{-10s^*}). \quad (\text{C.17})$$

Finally, regarding err (t) m,l,i,3 , note that with the same argument as [\(C.16\)](#page-26-3), we know that with probability at least 1 − d −c<sup>0</sup> , both ⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩ + y<sup>i</sup> · σ ′ (⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩) + err(t) m,l,i,1 and ⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩ + y<sup>i</sup> · σ ′ (⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩) are Or(1). Since σ ′ is a polynomial, it is Or(1)-Lipschitz continuous for inputs that are Or(1). Therefore, combined with [\(C.14\)](#page-26-4) that we have proved, we can obtain that

$$|\operatorname{err}_{m,l,i,3}^{(t)}| \leq |y_i| \cdot \tilde{O}(|\operatorname{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}|) = \tilde{O}(d^{-10s^*}). \quad (\text{C.18})$$

Finally, combining [\(C.14\)](#page-26-4), [\(C.17\)](#page-26-5), and [\(C.18\)](#page-26-6), we obtain that for given (m, l, i, t), with probability at least 1 − O(d <sup>−</sup>c<sup>0</sup> ), it holds that

$$|\operatorname{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}| = \tilde{O}(d^{-10s^\star}).$$

Finally, taking c<sup>0</sup> as a constant that is larger than 2 and applying a union bound argument, we can obtain that

$$\Pr(\mathcal{E}) \geq 1 - MLnT \cdot \tilde{O}(d^{-c_0}) \geq 1 - \tilde{\Theta}(d^2) \cdot \tilde{O}(d^{-c_0}) \geq 1 - \tilde{O}(d^{-c'_0})$$

for some other constant c ′ <sup>0</sup> > 0. Here we have applied our choice of (M, L, n, T) in our algorithm (see [Algorithm 1,](#page-5-0) [Algorithm 2\)](#page-9-2). Thus we verify the property of the event E, proving Corollary [4.5.](#page-7-6)

#### C . 2 . 2 M O D I F I E D L O S S F O R G E N E R A L s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1

Here we give a specific choice of the activation function σ and the loss function ℓ. We mainly focus on the situation where Q<sup>y</sup> has a continuous cumulative distribution function F<sup>Q</sup><sup>y</sup> with bounded density f<sup>Q</sup><sup>y</sup> . For the situation where Q<sup>y</sup> is a discrete distribution (e.g., classification task), we discuss them in the end of this section. For the activation function σ, we let σ(x) := (1/ √ s <sup>⋆</sup>) · h<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (x). Since then,

$$\hat{\psi}_s(y) = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\psi(x, y) \cdot h_s(x) \mid y] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\sigma'(x) \cdot h_s(x)] \cdot \ell'(y) = 0, \quad \forall s < s^* - 1. \quad (\text{C.19})$$

Regarding the choice of the loss function ℓ, we remark that if one chooses a fixed loss function, there always exist instances such that the second assumption in the high-pass condition fails. To address this issue, we propose to construct a random loss function ℓ. To rule out pathological examples of the underlying distribution <sup>P</sup>, we make the following assumption on the coefficient function ζ<sup>s</sup> ⋆ .

Assumption C.3. *We assume that the expansion of* ζr<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> := ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> ◦ F −1 Q<sup>y</sup> : [0, 1] 7→ <sup>R</sup> *on the Fourier basis* {φi(x)}i≥<sup>0</sup> *of* [0, 1] *has a non-zero coefficient of order at most* D = O(1)*.*

We then choose the loss function ℓ as the following,

$$\ell'(y) = \sum_{i=0}^D \alpha_i \cdot \varphi_i \circ F_{\mathbb{Q}_y}(y), \quad \alpha_i \sim \text{Unif}([0, 1]), \quad \forall 0 \leq i \leq D.$$

Notice that F<sup>Q</sup><sup>y</sup> can be estimated from data using a one dimensional density estimator. Thus here we directly assume the accessibility of the function F<sup>Q</sup><sup>y</sup> . This further gives that

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s^*-1}(y)] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y) \cdot \ell'(y)] = \sum_{i=0}^D \alpha_i \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\text{Unif}([0,1])} \left[ \tilde{\zeta}_{s^*}(\tilde{y}) \cdot \varphi_i(\tilde{y}) \right], \quad (\text{C.20})$$

which is a non-zero polynomial of the coefficients {αi}i≤<sup>D</sup> due to [Assumption C.3.](#page-27-1)

*Proof of Corollary [4.7.](#page-7-7)* To prove [Corollary 4.7,](#page-7-7) it suffices to show that (i) [Assumption 4.1](#page-6-2) holds, and (ii) the event E holds with the desired high probability. In the following, we first verify [Assumption 4.1,](#page-6-2) and then check the event E.

Verifying [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2) First, since σ ′ is a polynomial and ℓ ′ is bounded (the Fourier basis is bounded and D = O(1)), we know that both [Assumption 4.1\(a\)](#page-6-1) and [Assumption 4.1\(c\)](#page-6-4) are satisfied. Then, by the discussions before the proof, we know from [\(C.19\)](#page-27-2) that the first condition in the high-pass assumption [\(Assumption 4.1\(b\)\)](#page-6-3) is satisfied. Furthermore, according to [\(C.20\)](#page-27-3) and [Assumption C.3,](#page-27-1) <sup>E</sup>Q[ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y) · <sup>ψ</sup>p<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup>−1(y)] is a non-zero polynomial of the coefficients {αi}i≤<sup>D</sup> and thus its zeros form a measure-zero set. This means that with probability 1 over the randomness of (α1, · · · , αD), the second condition in the high-pass assumption is also satisfied. This verifies [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2)

Verifying the event E. Recall our definition in [Example 4.6,](#page-7-0) the error term is defined as

$$\text{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)} = \left( \ell'(y_i) - \ell' \left( y_i - f(z_i; \{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m \in [M]}) \right) \right) \cdot \sigma'(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i \rangle).$$

First, since w (t) m,l ∈ <sup>S</sup> d−1 , ⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩ is a standard Gaussian and therefore |⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩| <sup>=</sup> <sup>O</sup>r(1) with probability at least 1 − d −c<sup>0</sup> for some constant c<sup>0</sup> > 0. Since σ ′ is a polynomial of constant degree, we then obtain that |σ ′ (⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩)| <sup>=</sup> <sup>O</sup>r(1) with probability at least <sup>1</sup> <sup>−</sup> <sup>d</sup> −c<sup>0</sup> . Second, consider that the second derivative of the loss function ℓ ′′(y) is given by

$$\ell''(y) = \sum_{i=0}^D \alpha_i \cdot \varphi'_i \left( F_{\mathbb{Q}_y}(y) \right) \cdot f_{\mathbb{Q}_y}(y),$$

which satisfies |ℓ ′′(y)| = O(1) since the derivative of the Fourier basis is still bounded and that the density of Q<sup>y</sup> is assumed to be bounded. Therefore, we have

$$\begin{aligned} \ell'(y_i) - \ell' \left( y_i - f(z_i; \{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m \in [M]}) \right) &= O \left( \left| f(z_i; \{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m \in [M]}) \right| \right) \\ &= O \left( \sum_{m=1}^M |a_m| \cdot \left| \sigma(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i \rangle) \right| \right). \end{aligned}$$

Since σ is a polynomial of constant degree and ⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩ are all standard Gaussians, we have that |σ(⟨w (t) m,l, zi⟩)| <sup>=</sup> <sup>O</sup>r(1) with probability at least <sup>1</sup> <sup>−</sup> <sup>O</sup>(<sup>d</sup> <sup>−</sup>c<sup>0</sup> ). Now given that M = O(d) and taking a<sup>m</sup> = d −11s ⋆ , we have that

$$\ell'(y_i) - \ell' \left( y_i - f(z_i; \{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m \in [M]}) \right) = \tilde{O}(d^{-10s^*}),$$

with probability at least 1 − O(d <sup>−</sup>c<sup>0</sup> ). Therefore, for any given (m, l, i, t), with probability at least 1 − O(d <sup>−</sup>c<sup>0</sup> ), it holds that

$$|\operatorname{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}| = \tilde{O}(d^{-10s^*}) \cdot \tilde{O}(1) = \tilde{O}(d^{-10s^*}).$$

Finally, as in the proof of [Corollary 4.5,](#page-7-6) we take c<sup>0</sup> as a constant that is larger than s <sup>⋆</sup> + 1, and apply a union bound argument, by which we can obtain that

$$\Pr(\mathcal{E}) \geq 1 - MLnT \cdot \tilde{O}(d^{-c_0}) \geq 1 - \tilde{\Theta}(d^{s^*+1/2}) \cdot \tilde{O}(d^{-c_0}) \geq 1 - \tilde{O}(d^{-c'_0})$$

for some other constant c ′ <sup>0</sup> > 0. Thus we verify the property of the event E, proving Corollary [4.7.](#page-7-7)

Remark C.4 (Discrete labels). *For the case of discrete label* y *that supports on a finite set* Y *(e.g., classification tasks), the construction of* ψ *is somehow more direct. In this case, we can still consider an oracle function in the form of* ψ(y, x) = σ ′ (x) · φ(y) *for some function* φ(y)*. The activation function* σ(x) = (1/ √ s <sup>⋆</sup>) · h<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (x) *and the function* φ(y) *is a random function given by*

$$\varphi(y) \sim \text{Unif}([0, 1]), \quad \forall y \in \mathcal{Y}. \quad (\text{C.21})$$

*On the one hand, we can directly conclude as in* [\(C.19\)](#page-27-2) *that* <sup>ψ</sup>ps(y) = 0 *for all* s < s<sup>⋆</sup> − <sup>1</sup>*. On the other hand, we have that*

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s^{-1}}(y)] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y) \cdot \varphi(y)] = \sum_{y \in \mathcal{Y}} \mathbb{Q}_y(y) \cdot \zeta_{s^*}(y) \cdot \varphi(y).$$

*By the definition of generative exponent [\(Definition 2.1\)](#page-2-5),* <sup>E</sup>Q<sup>y</sup> [ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y) 2 ] > 0 *and thus at least one of* {Qy(y) · ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y)}y∈Y *is non-zero. Thus under* [\(C.21\)](#page-28-0)*,* <sup>E</sup>Q[ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y) · <sup>ψ</sup>p<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup>−1(y)] *is non-zero with probability* 1 *over the randomness in* φ*. Thus we have verified [Assumption 4.1\(b\).](#page-6-3) [Assumption 4.1\(a\)](#page-6-1) and [Assumption 4.1\(c\)](#page-6-4) can be verified in the same way as for the continuous case, and thus [Assumption 4.1](#page-6-2) is checked. Finally, we remark that in the discrete case we do not attempt to reduce the oracle function from certain loss derivative and thus we simply set the error terms* err *as zero. Thus all the conditions in Theorem [4.2](#page-6-0) hold and Corollary [4.7](#page-7-7) is proved.*

Remark C.5. *(Non-monotonicity of the loss function) Note that this loss function constructed above is not monotonically decreasing as* f *approaches* y*. However, this non-monotonicity does not impact our analysis since our primary objective is to learn the feature direction.*

### C . 3 D E B I A S I N G T H E G R A D I E N T E S T I M AT O R F O R U N K N O W N O R A C L E

In [Algorithm 1,](#page-5-0) we propose a meta-algorithm that incorporates weight perturbation at each gradient step. Specifically, as outlined in Line [6,](#page-5-3) after computing the gradient estimator

$$g_{m,l,i}^{(t)} = (\psi(y_i^{(t)}, \langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i^{(t)} \rangle) + \text{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}) \cdot z_i^{(t)},$$

we aggregate the gradients and remove bias through the following operation:

$$g_m^{(t)} = \frac{1}{nL} \sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{l=1}^L (g_{m,l,i}^{(t)} - \hat{\psi}_1(y_i^{(t)}) \cdot w_{m,l}^{(t)}).$$

We would like to demonstrate that the debiasing operation is only needed for s <sup>⋆</sup> ≤ 2, as the high-pass assumption already guarantees that the bias term is zero for s <sup>⋆</sup> > 2. Therefore, we only need to consider the case where s <sup>⋆</sup> ≤ 2. In the two examples presented in [Example 4.4](#page-7-3) and [Example 4.6](#page-7-0) (where the modified loss ℓ is known), we demonstrated that the oracle ψ(y, x) can be derived in closed form. Consequently, calculating <sup>ψ</sup>p1(y) to debias the gradient poses no significant challenges in such scenarios.

However, in cases where the gradient oracle is not readily available *in closed form*, additional complexity arises. Examples include scenarios where mini-batch SGD involves multiple passes over the same batch, or when label transformations are applied using a neural network. To handle such cases, we propose to use a debiasing technique based on the U statistics.

To simplify the notation, we omit the time index t. Additionally, we drop the dependence on the neuron index m and the perturbation index l. For a given mini-batch {(z<sup>i</sup> , yi)} n <sup>i</sup>=1, we retain {yi} n i=1 and resample {zrj} N <sup>j</sup>=1 from an isotropic Gaussian distribution. Taking the product of these two sets, we obtain a dataset of size nN:

$$\mathcal{D} = \{(\tilde{z}_j, y_i)\}_{i \in [n], j \in [N]}.$$

The gradient calculated for each sample (zr<sup>j</sup> , yi) is given by:

$$g_{i,j} = (\psi(y_i, \langle w, \tilde{z}_j \rangle) + \text{err}_{i,j}) \cdot \tilde{z}_j.$$

Note that here the error term is uniformly bounded by Or(d −10s ⋆ ) under the assumptions of both [Theorem 4.2](#page-6-0) and [Theorem 5.1.](#page-9-3)

Leveraging the independence between {zrj} N <sup>j</sup>=1 and {yi} n <sup>i</sup>=1, and noting that ψ(y, x)x has a polynomial tail (as established in [Assumption 4.1\(c\)\)](#page-6-4), we apply [Lemma J.3](#page-82-1) to obtain the following result with probability at least 1 − δ:

$$\left| \frac{1}{nN} \sum_{i \in [n], j \in [N]} \langle g_{i,j}, w \rangle - \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)} \left[ \sum_{i=1}^n \psi(y_i, x) x \right] \right| \leq \sqrt{\frac{\log \delta^{-1}}{N}} + \tilde{O}(N^{-1}) + \frac{1}{nN} \sum_{i,j} |\operatorname{err}_{i,j}|$$

By choosing N large enough (e.g., N = d <sup>C</sup> for some large constant C), the estimation error becomes negligible and can be safely absorbed into the error term for our analysis. Note that this debiasing procedure is only needed once at each iteration for a single perturbed weight, and we *don't need to repeat it for each neuron or perturbation* as the distribution of ⟨w, zr<sup>j</sup> ⟩ is the same for all neurons and perturbations. It is also worth noting that N only affects computational complexity, not sample complexity. Furthermore, the computational complexity remains polynomial in d.

In summary, suppose the gradient oracle is not available in closed form, we can instead implement the following procedure for s <sup>⋆</sup> ≤ 2 at each iteration of [Algorithm 1:](#page-5-0)

- 1. Sample isotropic Gaussian vectors {zrj} N <sup>j</sup>=1.
- 2. On synthetic sample {(zr<sup>j</sup> , yi)}i∈[n],j∈[N] , perform a gradient backward to obtain each gi,j for weight w1,1, which is the first perturbation of the first neuron.
- 3. Compute the debiasing term ν = 1 nN P i∈[n],j=∈[N] ⟨gi,j , w1,1⟩.

After this procedure, in Line [6](#page-5-3) of [Algorithm 1,](#page-5-0) we compute g (t) <sup>m</sup> = (nL) <sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> P<sup>n</sup> i=1 P<sup>L</sup> <sup>l</sup>=1 g (t) m,l,i − ν · w (t) m,l instead. The other steps of the meta algorithm remain the same.

#### C . 4 S U M M A RY O F LI M I TAT I O N S

Failure of standard methods. Although being a gradient-based method, our approach is extensively modified to adapt to the structure of the problem. Although we have shown in [4.2.2](#page-7-2) that some fatal issues prevent the vanilla mini-batch SGD from efficiently learning θ ⋆ , it remains unknown whether there is any standard gradient-based methods (for example noisy-SGD) that can succeed in this problem.

Minimizing the expected loss. Current work focuses on estimating θ <sup>⋆</sup> using the two-layer neural networks. However, training the second layer with learned feature goes beyond the scope of this work. We leave it as a future direction to investigate whether a learned feature enables minimizing the expected error efficiently.

Misspecification of the gradient oracle. The validity of [Theorem 4.2](#page-6-0) is based on that the order of the gradient oracle is correctly specified as the generative exponent s ⋆ , i.e., [Assumption 4.1\(b\).](#page-6-3) In general estimating tasks where the link distribution is unknown, the generative exponent is beyond the access and requires additional identification. In [Appendix C.1.4,](#page-23-0) we heuristically derive that size n = Ω(d (2s <sup>⋆</sup>−r)∨r)/2 ) samples are required for a order-r high-pass oracle to obtain a non-trivial SNR. This indicates that both the optimistic or pessimistic specification of the gradient oracle deteriorates the sample efficiency of our algorithm. To address the issue of unknown generative exponent, [Damian](#page-11-4) [et al.](#page-11-4) [\(2024\)](#page-11-4) propose to run their partial-trace algorithm with different order and compare the quality of the estimated feature on the hold-out validation set. We expect that a similar strategy for our algorithm can also tackle this issue. However, this brute-force approach is computationally inefficient. It is an interesting future direction to construct an efficient and adaptive algorithm that works for unknonw s ⋆ .

Constructing modified loss in general cases. In [Appendix C.2.2,](#page-27-0) we provide an example of loss function which provably satisfies [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2) However, the construction of this loss function involves a random linear combination of Fourier basis, which is unnatural and is rarely adopted in practice. Additionally, this construction requires access to the marginal distribution of y under P that needs an ad-hoc estimation. It is unclear whether using an estimated version of P<sup>y</sup> would cause the failure of previous argument. Besides, the minimal order of non-zero Fourier coefficient in [Assumption C.3](#page-27-1) is involved in the construction, which is typically inaccessible in practice. It would be an interesting future direction to investigate if there is any natural choice of loss function that generally follows [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2)

## D P R O O F S K E T C H O F T H E M A I N T H E O R E M F O R U N I F O R M P R I O R

In the following proof sketch, we only focus on the case s <sup>⋆</sup> being an even integer and s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2. The other cases only differs technically and we defer readers to [Appendix E.2](#page-34-0) for a detailded proof. For simplicity, denote ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> := ⟨θ (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩, the alignment between the weights of neuron m and the signal θ ⋆ at time t. Recall from Line [8](#page-5-5) in [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) that the update for neuron m at time step t is

$$\theta_m^{(t+1)} = \frac{\theta_m^{(t)} + \eta \bar{g}_m^{(t)}}{\|\theta_m^{(t)} + \eta \bar{g}_m^{(t)}\|_2}. \quad (\text{D.1})$$

This implies that the alignment of the next iteration, ρ (t+1) <sup>m</sup> , is a convex combination of the previous alignment ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> and the alignment of the update step ⟨g<sup>s</sup> (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ = ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩/∥g (t) <sup>m</sup> ∥2. Therefore, to show that the alignment improves after one iteration, we need to first analyze the scale of ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ and ∥g (t) <sup>m</sup> ∥2; then we will be able to characterize the improvement of ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> across iterations.

Alignment of the update step ⟨g<sup>s</sup> (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩. To this end, we calculate the first moment and and second moment of g (t) <sup>m</sup> over the randomness of the data {(z (t) i , y (t) i )} n <sup>i</sup>=1, and combining these leads to the concentration of ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩/∥g (t) <sup>m</sup> ∥2. More specifically, for the first moment of g (t) <sup>m</sup> , we have

$$\langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [g_m^{(t)}], \theta^* \rangle \approx \rho_m^{(t)} \gamma \cdot (|\rho_m^{(t)}| \gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2},$$

while the magnitude of <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g (t) <sup>m</sup> ] in any other direction orthogonal to θ ⋆ is of strictly higher order. For the second moment of g (t) <sup>m</sup> , setting γ = Θ( <sup>r</sup> d −1/4 ), it can be shown that for any direction v ∈ S d−1 , <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , v⟩ 2 ] = Or(d −(s <sup>⋆</sup>−1)/2 ). Now chooing n = Ω( <sup>r</sup> d s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ), it follows from a Bernstein-type concentration inequality that the fluctuation of ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , v⟩ is of the same order Or(d −s <sup>⋆</sup>/2+1/4 ) for any direction v ∈ S d−1 . Therefore, with high probability,

$$\begin{aligned} \langle g_m^{(t)}, \theta^* \rangle &\geq \rho_m^{(t)} \gamma \cdot (|\rho_m^{(t)}| \gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} - \tilde{O}(d^{-s^*/2+1/4}), \\ \|g_m^{(t)}\|_2 &\leq \rho_m^{(t)} \gamma \cdot (|\rho_m^{(t)}| \gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} + \tilde{O}(d^{-s^*/2+3/4}). \end{aligned} \quad (\text{D.2})$$

Phase 1: from d −1/2 to weak alignment. Due to random initialization, it holds with high probability that ρ (0) <sup>m</sup> = O(d −1/2 ) for Ω(M) many neurons. Therefore, it suffices to consider a neuron with ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> = Ω(d −1/2 ). When Ω(d −1/2 ) ≤ ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> ≤ O(1), by choosing γ = Θ( <sup>r</sup> d −1/4 ), we can ensure that the first term in the lower bound for ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ in [\(D.2\)](#page-31-1) dominates the Or(d −s <sup>⋆</sup>/2+1/4 ) fluctuation. Based on this, we can leverage [\(D.2\)](#page-31-1) to further show that ⟨g<sup>s</sup> (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ = ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩/∥g (t) <sup>m</sup> ∥<sup>2</sup> ≥ (1+c)ρ (t) m for a constant c > 0. Consequently, it follows from [\(D.1\)](#page-30-3) that

$$\rho_m^{(t+1)} \geq (1+c)\rho_m^t \quad \text{for some constant } c > 0.$$

Therefore, it takes O(log d) many steps for ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> to increase from d −1/2 to O(1). During this period, the dynamics will go through two phases separated by a critical alignment level ρ ⋆ such that

$$\rho_m^{(t)} \gamma \cdot (|\rho_m^{(t)}| \gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} \approx \tilde{O}(d^{-s^*/2+3/4}),$$

which gives that ρ <sup>⋆</sup> = Θ( <sup>r</sup> d −1/4 ). After the alignment ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> reaches ρ ⋆ , there is a short period where ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> grows rapidly as ρ (t+1) <sup>m</sup> /ρ<sup>⋆</sup> ≥ (ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> /ρ<sup>⋆</sup> ) s <sup>⋆</sup>−1 , until the alignment reaches d −1/4+1/4(s <sup>⋆</sup>−1). The length of this period is very short compared to the other periods on the road to weak alignment.

Phase 2: from weak to strong alignment. Finally, after ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> grows to a constant scale, we need to track the value of 1 − ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> . Again using [\(D.2\)](#page-31-1), we can show that 1 − ρ (t+1) <sup>m</sup> ≤ (1 + c)(1 − ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> ) for some constant c > 0. Hence, it takes another O(log d) steps to eventually achieve strong alignment.

## E P R O O F O F T H E M A I N T H E O R E M F O R T H E U N I F O R M P R I O R

Now we present the proof for [Theorem 4.2.](#page-6-0) We introduce a shorthand ρ = ⟨θ, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ for the alignment between θ and θ ⋆ . This shorthand inherits the subscript and superscript of θ as well, i.e., ρ (t) <sup>m</sup> = ⟨θ (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩.

Recall from [Algorithm 1](#page-5-0) that at the t-th step, given the normalized gradient step g (t) <sup>m</sup> = g (t) <sup>m</sup> /∥g (t) <sup>m</sup> ∥2, the updated weight parameter is given by

$$\theta_m^{(t+1)} = \frac{\theta_m^{(t)} + \eta g_m^{(t)} / \|g_m^{(t)}\|_2}{\|\theta_m^{(t)} + \eta g_m^{(t)} / \|g_m^{(t)}\|_2\|_2\|_2}.$$

Note that the alignment ⟨θ (t+1) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ depends on the alignment of the previous iterate ⟨θ (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ and the alignment of the current update step ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> /∥g (t) <sup>m</sup> ∥, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩, so we first need to analyze the latter.

Here, we stop to introduce an immediate result that is crucial in characterizing the alignment.

Almost orthogonality of smoothing noise. Recall that the perturbated weights are w (t) m,l = (γθ(t) <sup>m</sup> <sup>+</sup> <sup>ξ</sup>m,l)/∥γθ(t) <sup>m</sup> + ξm,l∥2, where ξm,l <sup>i</sup>.i.d. ∼ Unif(<sup>S</sup> d−1 ). Due to the high dimensionality, ξm,l is almost orthogonal to any designated direction with high probability. In the context, we are primarily interested in the alignment with θ ⋆ . Correspondingly, we decompose g (t) <sup>m</sup> with respect to the following orthonormal basis

$$\{v_{m,1}^{(t)} = \theta^*, v_{m,2}^{(t)} = (1 - \rho^2)^{-1/2} \cdot (\theta_m^{(t)} - \langle \theta_m^{(t)}, \theta^* \rangle \cdot \theta^*), v_{m,3}^{(t)}, \dots, v_{m,d}^{(t)}\} \quad (\text{E.1})$$

We justify this property by defining the following nice event for ϵ > 0:

$$\mathcal{E}_m^{(t)}(\epsilon) = \left\{ \max_{1 \leq i \leq d} |\langle \xi_{m,l}, v_{m,i}^{(t)} \rangle| < \epsilon, \forall l \in [L] \right\}.$$

This event helps in characterizing the alignment between the expected gradient <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g (t) <sup>m</sup> ] and the signal θ ⋆ . Additionally, we define another event for rϵ > 0:

$$\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_m^{(t)}(\tilde{\epsilon}) = \{|\langle \xi_{m,l}, \xi_{m,l'} \rangle| < \tilde{\epsilon}, \forall l, l' \in [L] \text{ s.t. } l \neq l'\}.$$

This event controls the correlation between the noise vectors, which helps in controlling the fluctuation of the gradient. The following lemma provides some direct benefits of these events.

Lemma E.1 (Polarized weight on the nice event). *For the orthonormal directions* {θ ⋆ , v (t) m,2 , . . . , v (t) m,d} *defined in* [\(E.1\)](#page-31-2)*, suppose that the corresponding nice event* E (t) <sup>m</sup> (ϵ) ∩ Er(t) <sup>m</sup> (rϵ) *holds and the polarization level* γ ∈ (0, 1/2)*. Then we have for any* l ∈ [L] *that*

$$\begin{aligned} |\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, \theta^* \rangle| &\leq 2(\gamma |\langle \theta_m^{(t)}, \theta^* \rangle| + \epsilon), & |\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, v_{m,1} \rangle| &\leq 2\left(\gamma \sqrt{1 - \langle \theta_m^{(t)}, \theta^* \rangle^2} + \epsilon\right), \\ |\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, v_{m,i}^{(t)} \rangle| &\leq 2 \cdot \epsilon, & 2 \leq i \leq d. \end{aligned}$$

*Additionally, for any* l ̸= l ′ *, we have that*

$$\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, w_{m,l'}^{(t)} \rangle \leq 4(\gamma^2 + 2\gamma\epsilon + \tilde{\epsilon}).$$

*Proof of [Lemma E.1.](#page-32-1)* See [Appendix E.3.](#page-39-0)

Characterizing ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩. Note that g (t) <sup>m</sup> = n <sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> P i g (t) m,i, where

$$g_{m,i}^{(t)} = \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \left( \psi(y_i^{(t)}, \langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i^{(t)} \rangle) \cdot z_i^{(t)} - \hat{\psi}_1(y_i^{(t)}) \cdot w_{m,l} \right).$$

We characterize the alignment ⟨g (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩/∥g (t) <sup>m</sup> ∥<sup>2</sup> in [Appendix E.1](#page-32-0) with two steps, stated in two key propositions as follows:

- 1. In [Proposition E.3,](#page-33-1) we analyze the magnitude of <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [⟨g
- (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩] and ∥<sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [P ⊥ θ <sup>⋆</sup> ⟨g
- (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩]∥.
- 2. In [Proposition E.4,](#page-33-0) we control the fluctuation of ⟨g
- (t) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ around its expectation using the polynomial-tail like property in [Assumption 4.1\(c\).](#page-6-4)

Both propositions are established under the nice event E (t) <sup>m</sup> (ϵ) ∩ Er(t) <sup>m</sup> (rϵ). The proof of these propositions is deferred to [Appendix E.3.](#page-39-0) Finally, with these two propositions, we prove [Theorem 4.2](#page-6-0) in [Appendix E.2.](#page-34-0)

#### E . 1 P R O P E R T I E S O F T H E G R A D I E N T S T E P

In this part, we characterize the alignment of normalized update g (t) <sup>m</sup> /∥g (t) <sup>m</sup> ∥ with the signal θ ⋆ , given θ (t) <sup>m</sup> . Since we are focusing on the one step behavior for a fixed neuron m ∈ [M], we omit the neuron index m and time index t in the sequel. To facilitate the presentation, we propose the following simplified setup that extract all the essential elements to describe the one-step behavior.

Definition E.2. *Fix* θ *and* θ <sup>⋆</sup> *and let* ρ = ⟨θ, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩*. Suppose the data points* (z1, y1), . . . ,(zn, yn) *are i.i.d. generated from* P<sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> *. Define* w<sup>l</sup> = (γθ + ξl)/∥γθ + ξl∥<sup>2</sup> *for* l = 1, . . . , L*, where* ξ1, . . . ξ<sup>L</sup> <sup>i</sup>.i.d. ∼ *Unif*(S d−1 ) *are independent of* {(z<sup>i</sup> , yi)} n <sup>i</sup>=1*. Given the oracle* ψ : <sup>R</sup> × <sup>R</sup> → <sup>R</sup>*, we define*

$$g = \frac{1}{nL} \sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{l=1}^L (\psi(y_i, \langle w_l, z_i \rangle) \cdot z_i - \hat{\psi}_1(y_i) \cdot w_l).$$

*To describe the associated good event, we fix an orthonormal basis:*

$$v_1 = \theta^\star, v_2 = \frac{\theta - \rho\theta^\star}{\sqrt{1 - \rho^2}}, v_3, \dots, v_d,$$

*and define*

$$\mathcal{E}(\epsilon) = \left\{ |\langle \xi_l, \theta^* \rangle| < \epsilon, \quad \max_{2 \leq i \leq d} |\langle \xi_l, v_i \rangle| < \epsilon, \quad \forall l \in [L] \right\};$$

$$\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\tilde{\epsilon}) = \{ |\langle \xi_l, \xi_{l'} \rangle| < \tilde{\epsilon}, \quad \forall l, l' \in [L] \quad \text{s.t.} \quad l \neq l' \}.$$

As mentioned in [Appendix D,](#page-30-0) we can reduce this problem to first characterizing <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g], and then control the fluctuation of g around its expectation. To this end, we first introduce a lemma that characterizes the first moment of the gradient step. This lemma is valid for both the non-sparse and sparse setting and is helpful in understanding the structure of the expected gradient.

Lemma H.2 (Decomposition of the first moment). *Suppose that we are working with the setting in [Definition E.2,](#page-32-2) where the oracle function* ψ *follows [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2) Then it holds that*

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[g] &= \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s-1}(y)] \cdot \frac{\sqrt{s}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s-1} \cdot \theta^* \\ &+ \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)] \cdot \frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^s \cdot w_l.\end{aligned}$$

*Proof of [Lemma H.2.](#page-67-0)* See [Appendix H.](#page-67-0)

One can easily see that the first summation term corresponds to the signal, while the second corresponds to the resilience of current weight. The structure of w<sup>l</sup> guarantees ⟨w<sup>l</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ is small, therefore only the leading term in the geometric series above is dominant. Also we note that the leading term from the signal is larger than the leading term from the resilience, which indicates that the expected gradient is highly aligned with the true signal. This is justified in the next proposition.

Before stating it, we fix M to be a sufficiently large constant that does not scale with d and T = O(log d). The involvement of M, T here is merely for the union bound argument in [Appendix E.2.](#page-34-0)

Proposition E.3 (Alignment of expected gradient). *Suppose that we are working with the setting in [Definition E.2,](#page-32-2) where the oracle function* ψ *follows [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2) Additionally, we set* γ = o(1)*,* L = Ω (ϵ∨γ) s <sup>⋆</sup>−1 · d s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> ∨(d log d) *and* ϵ = o(1) *is chosen such that* Pr(E(ϵ)) = 1 − O(d −s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 )*. Then there exists a* {ξl}l∈[L] *-measurable event* E<sup>1</sup> *with* Pr(E1) ≥ 1 − d −c (MT) −1 *, such that on the event* E<sup>1</sup> ∩ E(ϵ)*, it holds that*

$$\langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [g], \theta^* \rangle \simeq \begin{cases} \gamma \rho \cdot (\gamma |\rho| + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} & \text{if } s^* \text{ is even;} \\ (\gamma |\rho| + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1} & \text{if } s^* \text{ is odd,} \end{cases}$$

*and that*

$$||\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[g]||_2 - |\langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}g, \theta^* \rangle| \lesssim (\gamma|\rho| + d^{-1/2})^{s^*},$$

*as long as* γ|ρ| = ω(d −1 )*.*

*Proof of [Proposition E.3.](#page-33-1)* See [Appendix E.3.](#page-39-0)

From this proposition, it is already clear that the expected gradient is highly aligned with the signal θ ⋆ in the sense that ∥P ⊥ θ <sup>⋆</sup> <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g]∥<sup>2</sup> < |⟨<sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g], θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| whenever γ|ρ| = ω(d −1 ). Later we will see that this is indeed the case during the trajectory of [Algorithm 1.](#page-5-0)

Proposition E.4 (Fluctuation of mini-batch gradient). *Under the simplified setting introduced in [Definition E.2](#page-32-2) where* ψ : R × R → R *follows [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2) Suppose that we choose* ϵ *and* rϵ *such that*

$$\epsilon^2 \leq \tilde{\epsilon} \ll 1; \quad 2\gamma\epsilon \leq \tilde{\epsilon}.$$

*Also, suppose that sample size*

$$n = \Omega\left(((\gamma^2 + \tilde{\epsilon})^{s^*-1} + L^{-1})^{-1} \cdot \log(d)^{2C_p+2}\right)$$

*where* C<sup>p</sup> *is defined in [Assumption 4.1\(c\).](#page-6-4) Then there exists a* {(y<sup>i</sup> , zi)}i∈[n] *-measurable event* E<sup>2</sup> *with* Pr(E<sup>2</sup> c ) ≤ d −c · (MT) −1 *. And it holds on* E<sup>2</sup> ∩ E(ϵ) ∩ Er(rϵ) *that*

$$|\langle g, \theta^* \rangle - \langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[g], \theta^* \rangle| \lesssim \sqrt{\frac{((\gamma^2 + \tilde{\epsilon})^{s^*-1} + L^{-1}) \cdot \log(d)}{n}},$$

*and that*

$$\|g - \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[g]\|_2 \lesssim \sqrt{\frac{((^2 + \tilde{\epsilon})^{s-1} + L^{-1}) \cdot d \log(d)}{n}}$$

*Proof of [Proposition E.4.](#page-33-0)* See [Appendix E.3.](#page-41-0)

#### E . 2 P R O O F O F T H E M A I N T H E O R E M F O R U N I F O R M P R I O R

Now we are ready to present the proof of [Theorem 4.2.](#page-6-0)

*Proof of [Theorem 4.2.](#page-6-0)* We first establish the good events required for the proof, characterize the properties of the update step on these events, and then put things together to establish the alignment of the model weights with the signal.

Preparations. To start with, we clarify the event we will work with by verifying that our configuration is compatible with the conditions in [Proposition E.3](#page-33-1) and [Proposition E.4.](#page-33-0) Recall that we set L = Ω d (s <sup>⋆</sup>−1)/<sup>2</sup> ∨ (d log d) 1/2 and is at most polynomial in d, the scale of L clearly satisfy that L = Ω (d 1/2 ϵ) s ⋆ ∨ (d log d) . During our algorithm, γ = (d −1 · log d) <sup>1</sup>/<sup>4</sup> = o(1) is fixed. Choosing ϵ = d −1/2 log d, we have by [Lemma J.6](#page-83-0) that for any t and m, it holds that

$$1 - \Pr(\mathcal{E}_m^{(t)}(\epsilon)) \leq Ld \cdot \left( \exp(-d/16) + d^{-\log d/4} \right),$$

which decays faster than any constant-degree polynomial in d. Therefore, for sufficiently large d, it holds that Pr E (t) <sup>m</sup> (ϵ) c = O(d −s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 ). So far, we see that all the conditions in [Proposition E.3](#page-33-1) are satisfied and we denote the associated event as E (t) m,1 .

Next, we verify the conditions in [Proposition E.4.](#page-33-0) We choose rϵ = q 4 c + log<sup>d</sup> (MT L<sup>2</sup>) · d−<sup>1</sup> log d, then it holds by [Lemma J.6](#page-83-0) that

$$1 - \Pr \left( \tilde{\mathcal{E}}_m^{(t)}(\tilde{\epsilon}) \right) \leq L^2 \cdot \left( \exp(-d/16) + d^{-\tilde{\epsilon}^2 \cdot \log d/4} \right) \lesssim d^{-c}/MT.$$

Additionally, we see that both ϵ <sup>2</sup> ≤ <sup>r</sup>ϵ ≪ 1 and 2γϵ ≤ <sup>r</sup>ϵ is satisfied for sufficiently large d. It is easily verified that our choice of L = Ω d (s <sup>⋆</sup>+1)/<sup>2</sup> ∨ (d log d) clearly meets the condition that

$$L \gtrsim (\epsilon \vee \gamma)^{s^*-1} \cdot d^{s^*/2} \vee d \log d$$

we have that the sample size threshold is now

$$\frac{\log(d)^{2C_p+2}}{((\gamma^2 + \tilde{\epsilon})^{s^*-1} + L^{-1})} \lesssim \log(d)^{2C_p+2} \cdot d^{(s^*-1)/2},$$

which is satisfied by our choice <sup>n</sup> = Θ (d log d) s <sup>⋆</sup>/<sup>2</sup> ∨ d log d log d . Hence, all the conditions in [Proposition E.4](#page-33-0) are satisfied and we denote the associated event as E (t) m,2 .

Recalling that the gradient in [Definition E.2](#page-32-2) does not include the error term err (t) m,l,i, we additionally need an event that controls the norm of the inputs z<sup>i</sup> (t) 2 , which helps to control ∥err (t) m,l,i · zi∥2. For this purpose, we define

$$\mathcal{E}_{m,3}^{(t)} = \left\{ \max_{i \in [n]} \|z_i^{(t)}\|_2 \leq \sqrt{d} \right\}.$$

By standard Bernstein's inequality, we have that Pr E c m,3 (t) ≤ Ld · exp{−d/8} = O(exp{−C ′d}) for some C ′ > 0. To put things together, we work on the following event:

$$\mathcal{E} = \bigcap_{m=1}^M \bigcap_{t=1}^T (\mathcal{E}_m^{(t)}(\epsilon) \cap \tilde{\mathcal{E}}_m^{(t)}(\tilde{\epsilon}) \cap \mathcal{E}_{m,1}^{(t)} \cap \mathcal{E}_{m,2}^{(t)} \cap \mathcal{E}_{m,3}^{(t)}),$$

which is of Pr(E) ≥ 1 − O(d −c ) for some c > 0 by the union bound argument. Denote

$$\bar{g}_m^{(t)} = \frac{1}{nL} \sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{l=1}^L (\psi(y_i^{(t)}, \langle w_{m,l}^{(t)}, z_i^{(t)} \rangle) \cdot z_i^{(t)} - \hat{\psi}_1(y_i^{(t)}) \cdot w_{m,l}),$$

then g<sup>s</sup> (t) <sup>m</sup> and the mini-batch data {(y (t) i , z (t) i )}i∈[n] match the definition in [Definition E.2,](#page-32-2) which allows us to apply [Proposition E.3](#page-33-1) and [Proposition E.4.](#page-33-0) Thanks to the event E (t) m,3 , we always have for any v ∈ S d−1 that

$$\begin{aligned} |\langle g_m^{(t)}, v \rangle - \langle \bar{g}_m^{(t)}, v \rangle| &\leq ||g_m^{(t)}|_2 - |\bar{g}_m^{(t)}|_2| \\ &\leq d^{1/2} \cdot \max_{l,i} |\text{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}| \\ &\leq d^{-9s^*}. \end{aligned} \tag{E.2}$$

In the sequel, we restrict our attention to neurons that have d <sup>−</sup>1/<sup>2</sup>/2 alignment, i.e., the index m such that |⟨θ (0) <sup>m</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| ≥ d <sup>−</sup>1/<sup>2</sup>/2. From now on, we will drop the neuron index m and the iteration index (t) in the following analysis for simplicity. The updated weight parameter is denoted as θ ′ , and the alignment after the update is denoted as ρ ′ = ⟨θ ′ , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩. Note that for large M ≫ 1, the number of neurons with initial alignment |ρ| ≥ d <sup>−</sup>1/<sup>2</sup>/2 is at least Ω(M). For our convenience, in the following we will denote by

$$\kappa := \frac{n}{(d \log d)^{s^*/2} \vee d \log d} \cdot (\log d)^{-1} = \Omega(1).$$

Under the preceding configuration, [Proposition E.4](#page-33-0) and Eq. [\(E.2\)](#page-35-0) together imply that the fluctuations of ⟨g, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ can be further bounded by

$$\begin{aligned} |\langle g, \theta^* \rangle - \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle \bar{g}, \theta^* \rangle]| &\lesssim \sqrt{\frac{((\gamma^2 + \tilde{c})^{s^*-1} + L^{-1}) \cdot \log(d)}{n}} + |\langle \bar{g}, \theta^* \rangle - \langle g, \theta^* \rangle| \\ &\lesssim \sqrt{\frac{(d^{-1} \log d)^{(s^*-1)/2} \cdot \log(d)}{n}} + d^{-9s^*} \\ &= \begin{cases} d^{-(2s^*-1)/4} \cdot (\log d)^{-1/4} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2} & \text{if } s^* \geq 2, \\ d^{-1/2} \cdot (\log d)^{-1/2} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2} & \text{if } s^* = 1. \end{cases} \end{aligned} \quad (\text{E.3})$$

On the other hand, we have by [Proposition E.3](#page-33-1) and Eq. [\(E.2\)](#page-35-0) that

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\langle g, \theta^* \rangle] &\geq \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\langle \bar{g}, \theta^* \rangle] - |\langle g, \theta^* \rangle - \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\langle \bar{g}, \theta^* \rangle]| \\ &\geq |\rho| \gamma (|\rho| \gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} - d^{-9s^*} \\ &\geq d^{-(2s^*-1)/4} \cdot (\log d)^{1/4}, \end{aligned}$$

whenever |ρ|γ = Ω(d −3/4 ). Therefore, when κ is sufficiently large, we have the fluctuations to be strictly bounded by half of the signal strength. Thus, we have

$$|\langle g, \theta^* \rangle| \geq \frac{1}{2} \cdot |\langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\bar{g}], \theta^* \rangle|.$$

For the norm of g, we have that

$$\begin{aligned} \|g\|_2 &\leq \|g\|_2 - \|\bar{g}\|_2 + \|\bar{g}\|_2 - \|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\bar{g}]\|_2 \\ &\quad + \|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\bar{g}]\|_2 - \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle \bar{g}, \theta^* \rangle] + \|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle \bar{g}, \theta^* \rangle] \\ &\leq d^{-9s^*} + \|\bar{g} - \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\bar{g}]\|_2 + \|\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\bar{g}]\|_2 - \langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\bar{g}], \theta^* \rangle + |\langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\bar{g}], \theta^* \rangle| \\ &\lesssim |\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\langle \bar{g}, \theta^* \rangle]| + (\gamma|\rho| + d^{-1/2})^{s^*} + \sqrt{\frac{(d^{-1} \log d)^{(s^*-1)/2} \cdot d \log d}{n}}, \end{aligned} \quad (\text{E.4})$$

where in the second inequality, we apply Eq. [\(E.2\)](#page-35-0) and the triangular inequality that ∥gs∥ − <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [gs]  ≤ g<sup>s</sup> − <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [gs] . And the last inequality is deduced by combining the result [Propo](#page-33-1)[sition E.3](#page-33-1) and the fact that d −9s <sup>⋆</sup> ≪ d −s <sup>⋆</sup>/2 . For the leading term, it holds by [Proposition E.3](#page-33-1) that

$$|\langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\bar{g}], \theta^* \rangle| \simeq \begin{cases} (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1} & \text{if } s^* \text{ is odd,} \\ |\rho|\gamma(|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} & \text{if } s^* \text{ is even.} \end{cases} \quad (\text{E.5})$$

Recall that the alignment admits the following iterative update rule:

$$|\langle \theta', \theta^* \rangle| = \left| \left\langle \frac{\theta + \eta G}{\|\theta + \eta G\|_2}, \theta^* \right\rangle \right| \geq \frac{|\langle G, \theta^* \rangle| - \eta^{-1}|\langle \theta, \theta^* \rangle|}{1 + \eta^{-1}},$$

where G = g/∥g∥2. In the following, we will define ρ <sup>⋆</sup> = d −1/4 (log d) 1/4 as a critical threshold before the weak alignment. Specifically, in the phase I of weak alignment, we assume that |ρ| ≤ ρ ⋆ . When the training process goes across this critical threshold, the dominant term in <sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [⟨g, θ <sup>s</sup> ⋆ ⟩] <sup>≂</sup> (γρ) <sup>1</sup>{s ⋆ is even} (γρ + d −1/2 ) ⋆−1−1{s ⋆ is even} becomes γρ instead of d −1/2 .

Stage I of weak alignment. Case I: s <sup>⋆</sup> ̸= 1, s ⋆ is odd. Combining the results in Eq. [\(E.3\)](#page-35-1), [\(E.4\)](#page-36-0) and [\(E.5\)](#page-36-1), we conclude that for s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 3 being odd,

$$\begin{aligned} |\langle g, \theta^* \rangle| &\gtrsim (|\rho| \cdot (d \log d)^{-1/4} + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1}, \\ \|g\|_2 &\lesssim (|\rho| \cdot (d \log d)^{-1/4} + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1} + d^{-(2s^*-3)/4} \cdot (\log d)^{-1/4} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}. \end{aligned}$$

It is important to note that here "≳" and "≲" only hides constants that are independent of d and n. Combining these two inequalities, we have that

$$\begin{aligned} \frac{|\langle g, \theta^* \rangle|}{\|g\|_2} &\approx \frac{(|\rho|d^{1/4}(\log d)^{-1/4} + 1)^{s^*-1}}{(|\rho|d^{1/4}(\log d)^{-1/4} + 1)^{s^*-1} + d^{1/4}(\log d)^{-1/4} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}} \\ &= \frac{(|\rho|/\rho^* + 1)^{s^*-1}}{(|\rho|/\rho^* + 1)^{s^*-1} + \kappa^{-1/2}/\rho^*}. \end{aligned}$$

Thus, if |ρ| ≤ ρ ⋆ and take κ to be a sufficiently large constant, after the first gradient update, the alignment will grow to at least ρ <sup>⋆</sup> by noting that |⟨g, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩|/∥g∥<sup>2</sup> ≳ ρ ⋆κ 1/2 and that

$$\begin{aligned} |\rho'| &\gtrsim \frac{\rho^* \kappa^{1/2} - \eta^{-1} |\rho|}{1 + \eta^{-1}} \\ &\geq \rho^* \cdot \frac{\sqrt{\kappa} - \eta^{-1}}{1 + \eta^{-1}} \geq \rho^*. \end{aligned}$$

As a summary of Case I(a), with one step of gradient update, the alignment will grow to at least ρ ⋆ if |ρ| ≤ ρ ⋆ .

Stage I of weak alignment. Case II: s ⋆ is even. In the case where s ⋆ is even, we have by the previous arguments that

$$\begin{aligned} |\langle g, \theta^* \rangle| &\gtrsim |\rho| \cdot (d \log d)^{-1/4} \cdot (|\rho| \cdot (d \log d)^{-1/4} + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2}, \\ \|g\|_2 &\lesssim |\rho| \cdot (d \log d)^{-1/4} \cdot (|\rho| \cdot (d \log d)^{-1/4} + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} \\ &\quad + d^{-(2s^*-3)/4} \cdot (\log d)^{-1/4} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2} \end{aligned}$$

Here, we use the following fact that

$$(|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*} \lesssim (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} \cdot (\rho^2\gamma^2 + d^{-1}) \lesssim (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2}|\rho|\gamma,$$

where the last inequality holds since |ρ| ≥ d <sup>−</sup>1/<sup>2</sup>/2. Thus, we conclude that

$$\begin{aligned} \frac{|\langle g, \theta^* \rangle|}{\|g\|_2} &\geq \frac{|\rho| \cdot (|\rho| d^{1/4} (\log d)^{-1/4} + 1)^{s^*-2}}{|\rho| \cdot (|\rho| d^{1/4} (\log d)^{-1/4} + 1)^{s^*-2} + \kappa^{-1/2}} \\ &= \frac{|\rho| \cdot (|\rho|/\rho^* + 1)^{s^*-2}}{|\rho| \cdot (|\rho|/\rho^* + 1)^{s^*-2} + \kappa^{-1/2}}. \end{aligned} \quad (\text{E.6})$$

Note that κ = Ω(1). Hence before the alignment reaches ρ ⋆ , κ <sup>−</sup>1/<sup>2</sup> will dominate the denominator in Eq. [\(E.6\)](#page-37-0), which gives us that |⟨g, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩|/∥g∥<sup>2</sup> <sup>≳</sup> <sup>|</sup>ρ| · √ κ. Importantly, the "≳" hides constants that are independent of d and κ. Thus, by taking κ to be a sufficiently large constant, we can conclude that

$$|\rho'| \geq |\rho| \cdot \frac{\sqrt{\kappa} - \eta^{-1}}{1 + \eta^{-1}} \geq 2|\rho|.$$

As a summary of Case II(a), before the alignment reaches ρ ⋆ , the alignment will grow exponentially fast, and this phase takes at most O log(d) steps. In the following, we consider the case when |ρ| ≥ ρ ⋆ for both cases I and II.

Stage II of weak alignment. Case I&II combined. Now we consider the case when |ρ| ≥ ρ ⋆ for both cases I and II, i.e., s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2. For this case, we have |ρ|/ρ<sup>⋆</sup> + 1 ≃ |ρ|/ρ<sup>⋆</sup> . Let us define r = |ρ|/ρ<sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 1 and r ′ = |ρ ′ |/ρ<sup>⋆</sup> , and it follows that

$$\frac{|\langle g, \theta^* \rangle|}{\|g\|_2} \gtrsim \frac{r^{s^*-1}}{r^{s^*-1} + \kappa^{-1/2}/\rho^*},$$

and consequently:

$$\begin{aligned} r' &\lesssim \frac{r^{s^*-1} \cdot (r^{s^*-1} \rho^* + \kappa^{-1/2})^{-1} - \eta^{-1} r}{1 + \eta^{-1}} \\ &\geq \frac{(\sqrt{\kappa} \cdot r^{s^*-1}) \wedge \rho^{*-1} - \eta^{-1} r}{1 + \eta^{-1}} \end{aligned}$$

It can be noted that the maximal ratio r ≤ (ρ ⋆ ) −1 , and also √ κ · r s <sup>⋆</sup>−<sup>1</sup> ≥ 2η −1 r given that κ is sufficiently large and r ≥ 1. Thus, we conclude that in this case

$$r' \gtrsim (\sqrt{\kappa} \cdot r^{s^*-1}) \wedge \rho^{*-1}.$$

For this case, the growth of the alignment will be also at least exponentially fast, until it reaches Ω (ρ ⋆ ) −1 , i.e., |ρ| = C for some constant C. This phase takes at most O(log(d)) steps.

Strong alignment. Case I&II combined. We need a more careful analysis for this case in order to achieve strong alignment. When the alignment is on a constant level, we can deduce from its original form that

$$|\langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[\bar{g}], \theta^* \rangle| = B(\rho, \{\xi_l\}_{l \in [L]}) \cdot (|\rho|\gamma)^{s^*-1} + E_1,$$

where B(ρ, {ξl}l∈[L]) = Ω(1) is a constant that depends on ρ and the random perturbations {ξl}l∈[L] and the error term follows that |E| ≤ O(d −s ⋆ ). . In the following, we will drop the dependency on ρ and {ξl}l∈[L] and use B for simplicity. We have by Eq. [\(E.3\)](#page-35-1) that

$$\begin{aligned} |\langle g, \theta^* \rangle| &\geq B(|\rho|\gamma)^{s^*-1} - O\left(d^{-(2s^*-1)/4} \cdot (\log d)^{-1/4} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}\right) \\ &= B(|\rho|\gamma)^{s^*-1} - O\left((d \log d)^{-s^*/4} \cdot \gamma^{s^*-1} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}\right) \\ &= \gamma^{s^*-1} \cdot \left(B|\rho|^{s^*-1} - (d \log d)^{-s^*/4} \cdot O(\kappa^{-1/2})\right), \end{aligned}$$

and also

$$\begin{aligned} \|g\|_2 &\leq B(|\rho|\gamma)^{s^*-1} + O\left((|\rho|\gamma)^{s^*} + d^{-(2s^*-3)/4} \cdot (\log d)^{-1/4} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}\right) \\ &\leq B(|\rho|\gamma)^{s^*-1} + O\left((|\rho|\gamma)^{s^*} + d^{-(s^*-2)/4} \cdot (\log d)^{-s^*/4} \cdot \gamma^{s^*-1} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}\right) \\ &\leq \gamma^{s^*-1} \cdot (B|\rho|^{s^*-1} + O(d^{-(s^*-2)/4} \cdot (\log d)^{-s^*/4} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2})). \end{aligned}$$

Therefore, once the alignment reaches a constant level |ρ| ≥ O(1), we have

$$|\langle G, \theta^* \rangle| = \frac{|\langle g, \theta^* \rangle|}{\|g\|_2} \geq \frac{B|\rho|^{s^*-1} - O(d^{-s^*/4})}{B|\rho|^{s^*-1} + O(d^{-1/4}(\log d)^{1/4})} \geq 1 - O(d^{-1/4}(\log d)^{1/4}) =: 1 - \Delta.$$

Here, ∆ ≃ d −1/4 (log d) 1/4 . Thus, as long as η > 2, after one step gradient,

$$\begin{aligned} |\rho'|^2 &= \frac{\langle G + \eta^{-1}\theta, \theta^* \rangle^2}{\langle G + \eta^{-1}\theta, \theta^* \rangle^2 + \|P_{\theta^*}^\perp (G + \eta^{-1}\theta)\|_2^2} \\ &\geq \frac{(1 - \Delta - \eta^{-1}|\rho|)^2}{(1 - \Delta - \eta^{-1}|\rho|)^2 + (\sqrt{1 - (1 - \Delta)^2} + \eta^{-1}\sqrt{1 - \rho^2})^2} \\ &\geq \frac{(1 - \eta^{-1} - \Delta)^2}{(1 - \eta^{-1} - \Delta)^2 + \eta^{-2}(1 - \rho^2) + 2\sqrt{2\Delta} + 2\Delta^2} \\ &= \frac{(1 - \eta^{-1})^2}{(1 - \eta^{-1})^2 + \eta^{-2}(1 - \rho^2)} - O(\sqrt{\Delta}). \end{aligned}$$

Here, the first equality holds by the Pythagorean theorem, the first inequality holds by the triangle inequality, and in the last line, we separate the major term and the error term that scales with √ ∆, where we use the fact that 1 − η <sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> > 1/2 with η > 2. In addition, by letting τ = η <sup>−</sup><sup>2</sup>/(1 − η −1 ) 2 , we have

$$\begin{aligned} 1 - (\rho')^2 &= 1 - \frac{(1 - \eta^{-1})^2}{(1 - \eta^{-1})^2 + \eta^{-2}(1 - \rho')^2} + O(\sqrt{\Delta}) \\ &= \frac{\tau(1 - \rho^2)}{1 + \tau(1 - \rho^2)} + O(\sqrt{\Delta}) \\ &\leq \tau(1 - \rho^2) + O(\sqrt{\Delta}). \end{aligned}$$

Therefore, we conclude that as long as τ < 1, i.e., η > 2, 1 − ρ <sup>2</sup> will exponentially decrease to O((1 − τ ) −1 · √ ∆), and achieves strong alignment in O((log ∆<sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> )/(log τ −1 )) steps.

Weak & strong alignment. Case III: s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1. In this case, we conclude from the previous arguments that regardless of the alignment level, it always holds that

$$|\langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} [\bar{g}], \theta^* \rangle| = B = O(1),$$

which gives us

$$|\langle g, \theta^\star \rangle| \geq B - O(d^{-1/2} \cdot (\log d)^{-1/2} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}),$$

and

$$\|g\|_2 \leq B + O(d^{-1/4}(\log d)^{1/4} + (\log d)^{-1/2} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}),$$

where we use the fact that n = κ · d(log d) 2 . Therefore, we also have

$$|\langle G, \theta^* \rangle| = \frac{|\langle g, \theta^* \rangle|}{\|g\|_2} \geq 1 - O((\log d)^{-1/2} \cdot \kappa^{-1/2}) = 1 - \Delta,$$

where in this case, we also have ∆ ≃ (log d) −1/2 just like s <sup>⋆</sup> = 2 in the previous case, and the rest of the proof follows the same arguments as in the previous case for the strong alignment.

## E . 3 P R O O F O F K E Y RE S U LT S

*Proof of [Lemma E.1.](#page-32-1)* We begin with proving the first part of the lemma. For conciseness, we drop the supscript (t) and simply denote θ<sup>m</sup> as the present weight. We have the projection of wm,l onto θ ⋆ as

$$|\langle w_{m,l}, \theta^* \rangle| = \left| \frac{\gamma \langle \theta_m, \theta^* \rangle + \langle \xi_{m,l}, \theta^* \rangle}{\|\gamma \theta_m + \xi_{m,l}\|_2} \right| \leq 2(\gamma |\rho_m| + \epsilon).$$

For direction vm,2, by definition we have

$$\begin{aligned} |\langle w_{m,l}, v_{m,2} \rangle| &= \left| \frac{\gamma \langle \theta_m, v_{m,2} \rangle + \langle \xi_{m,l}, v_{m,2} \rangle}{\|\gamma \theta_m + \xi_{m,l}\|_2} \right| \\ &\leq 2 \cdot \left( \gamma \left| \left\langle \theta_m, \frac{\theta_m - \rho_m \theta^*}{\|\theta_m - \rho_m \theta^*\|_2} \right\rangle \right| + \epsilon \right) \\ &= 2(\gamma \sqrt{1 - \rho_m^2} + \epsilon). \end{aligned}$$

For the remaining directions, we always have

$$|\langle w_{m,l}, v_{m,i} \rangle| = \left| \frac{\gamma\langle \theta_m, v_{m,i} \rangle + \langle \xi_{m,l}, v_{m,i} \rangle}{\|\gamma\theta_m + \xi_{m,l}\|_2} \right| \leq 2 \cdot \epsilon,$$

where we use the fact that ⟨θm, vm,i⟩ = 0 for i ≥ 2. This completes the proof for the first part.

On the joint nice event Erm(rϵ), we have that

$$\begin{aligned} |\langle w_{m,l}, w_{m,l'} \rangle| &\leq 4 \cdot \langle \gamma \theta_m + \xi_{m,l}, \gamma \theta_m + \xi_{m,l'} \rangle \\ &\leq 4(\gamma^2 + \tilde{\epsilon} + \gamma \langle \theta_m, \xi_{m,l} \rangle + \gamma \langle \theta_m, \xi_{m,l'} \rangle). \end{aligned}$$

On the other hand, it holds on the event Em(ϵ) that

$$|\langle \theta_m, \xi_{m,l} \rangle| = |\langle \sqrt{1-\rho^2} \cdot v_{m,2} + \rho v_{m,1}, \xi_{m,l} \rangle| \leq (\sqrt{1-\rho^2} + |\rho|) \cdot \epsilon \leq 2\epsilon.$$

Therefore, we have for any l ̸= l ′ that

$$|\langle w_{m,l}, w_{m,l'} \rangle| \leq 4(\gamma^2 + 4\epsilon\gamma + \tilde{\epsilon}).$$

*Proof of [Proposition E.3.](#page-33-1)* Invoking [Lemma H.2](#page-67-0) with the fact that ∥θ <sup>⋆</sup>∥<sup>2</sup> = 1, we can decompose ⟨<sup>E</sup><sup>P</sup>θ<sup>⋆</sup> [g], θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ as

$$\begin{aligned} \langle \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[g], \theta^* \rangle &= \sum_{s \geq s^*} \frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)] \cdot \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s+1} \\ &\quad + \sum_{s \geq s^*} \frac{\sqrt{s}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s-1}(y)] \cdot \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s-1} \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s^*-1}(y)] \cdot \frac{\sqrt{s^*}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1} + R, \end{aligned} \tag{E.7}$$

where all the remainder terms are collected by R, defined as

$$R = \sum_{s \geq s^*} \frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \left( \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)] \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle + \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s+1}(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_s(y)] \right) \cdot \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^s.$$

Below we will analyze the scale of each term in Eq. [\(E.7\)](#page-39-1), and show that the remainder R is negligible compared to the first term in Eq. [\(E.7\)](#page-39-1) with high probability over the randomness of the injected noise ξ1, . . . , ξL.

Analysis for the remainder term R in Eq. [\(E.7\)](#page-39-1). To bound |R|, we apply the triangle inequality with the fact that |⟨w<sup>l</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| ≤ 1 to get

$$|R| \leq \sum_{s \geq s^*} \frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ |\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)| + |\zeta_{s+1}(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_s(y)| \right] \cdot |\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle|^s.$$

Since <sup>E</sup>Q[ζs+1(y) 2 ] ≤ 1 for all s ≥ 0 by the property of the decomposition of the likelihood ratio, we have

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[|\zeta_{s+1}(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_s(y)|] &\leq \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s+1}(y)^2]^{1/2} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\hat{\psi}_s(y)^2]^{1/2} \\ &\leq \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\hat{\psi}_s(y)^2]^{1/2} \\ &\leq \sqrt{\sum_{s=0}^{\infty} \mathbb{E}[\hat{\psi}_s(y)^2]} = O(1),\end{aligned}$$

and similarly for <sup>E</sup>Q[|ζs(y) · <sup>ψ</sup>ps+1(y)|]. It then suffices to bound P s≥s <sup>⋆</sup> ( √ s + 1)/L · P<sup>L</sup> <sup>l</sup>=1 |⟨w<sup>l</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩|s . Recall that we restrict ourselves to the following nice event

$$\mathcal{E}(\epsilon) : \left\{ |\langle \xi_l, \theta^* \rangle| < \epsilon, \quad \max_{2 \leq i \leq d} |\langle \xi_l, v_i \rangle| < \epsilon, \quad \forall l \in [L] \right\},$$

where {v<sup>1</sup> = θ ⋆ , v<sup>2</sup> = (θ − ρθ<sup>⋆</sup> )/ p 1 − ρ <sup>2</sup>, v3, . . . vd} is an orthonormal basis. Since we assume that γ = o(1), it follows from [Lemma E.1](#page-32-1) that |⟨w<sup>l</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩| < 1/2 for all l ∈ [L] on E(ϵ). Consequently, it holds on E(ϵ) that

$$\begin{aligned} \sum_{s \geq s^*} \frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L |\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle|^s &\leq \sum_{s \geq s^*} \sqrt{s+1} \cdot \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{s-s^*} \cdot \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L |\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle|^{s^*} \\ &\lesssim \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L |\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle|^{s^*}, \end{aligned} \quad (\text{E.8})$$

where ≲ hides a constant that depends on s ⋆ . Now it reduces to upper bound the right hand side in Eq. [\(E.8\)](#page-40-0). To proceed, we define

$$\tilde{w}_l = \begin{cases} w_l & \text{if } \sup_i |\langle w_l, v_i \rangle| < \epsilon; \\ 0 & \text{otherwise.} \end{cases}$$

It can be easily verified that wr<sup>l</sup> = w<sup>l</sup> for any l ∈ [L] on E(ϵ), and {wrl}l∈[L] is a sequence of independent and bounded random vectors. By [Lemma E.1,](#page-32-1) we have that

$$|\langle \tilde{w}_l, \theta^* \rangle| \leq 2\gamma \vee \epsilon.$$

To find its second moment, we have by definition that

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{w}_l}[\langle \tilde{w}_l, \theta^\star \rangle^{2s^*}] &= \mathbb{E}_{w_l}[\langle w_l, \theta^\star \rangle^{2s^*} \cdot \mathbb{1}_{\{ \sup_i |\langle w_l, v_i \rangle| \leq \epsilon \}] \\ &\leq \mathbb{E}_{w_l}[\langle w_l, \theta^\star \rangle^{2s^*}] \\ &\simeq (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{2s^*},\end{aligned}$$

where the last line holds by [Lemma H.4.](#page-70-0) Therefore, we can apply the Bernstein's inequality [\(Lemma J.1\)](#page-81-1) to the right hand side of Eq. [\(E.8\)](#page-40-0) restricted to E(ϵ). We deduce from it that there exists a event E1,<sup>1</sup> with Pr(E1,1) ≥ 1 − d <sup>−</sup><sup>c</sup>/(MT), and it holds on E1,<sup>1</sup> ∩ E(ϵ) that

$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L |\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle|^{s^*} &= \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L |\langle \tilde{w}_l, \theta^* \rangle|^{s^*} \\ &\lesssim \left(1 + \sqrt{L^{-1} \log d}\right) \cdot (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*} + \frac{(\epsilon \vee \gamma)^{s^*} \log d}{L} \\ &\lesssim (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*} \end{aligned} \tag{E.9}$$

Here we use the fact that M and T are at most polynomial in d and the last line holds since we choose <sup>L</sup> = Ω d 1/2 · (ϵ ∨ γ) s ⋆ · log d ∨ log d .

Analysis for the dominant term in Eq. [\(E.7\)](#page-39-1). We then consider the major term L <sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> P<sup>L</sup> <sup>l</sup>=1⟨w<sup>l</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ s <sup>⋆</sup>−1 . By the definition of wr<sup>l</sup> , we can approximate its expectation as follows:

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{w}_l}[\langle \tilde{w}_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1}] &= \mathbb{E}_{w_l} [\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1} \cdot \mathbf{1} \{ \sup_i |\langle w_l, v_i \rangle| \leq \epsilon \}] \\ &\simeq \mathbb{E}_{w_l} [\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1}] \pm \Pr \left( \sup_i |\langle w_l, v_i \rangle| > \epsilon \right) \\ &\simeq \mathbb{E}_{w_l} [\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1}] \pm \Pr (\mathcal{E}(\epsilon)^c,\end{aligned}$$

where we use the fact that |⟨w<sup>l</sup> , θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩|s <sup>⋆</sup>−<sup>1</sup> <sup>≤</sup> <sup>1</sup> and the event {sup<sup>i</sup> |⟨w<sup>l</sup> , vi⟩| > ϵ} ⊂ E(ϵ) c . Again, we have by [Lemma H.4](#page-70-0) that

$$\mathbb{E}_{w_l}[\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s^*-1}] \simeq \begin{cases} (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1} & \text{if } s^* \text{ is odd}; \\ \rho\gamma(|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-2} & \text{if } s^* \text{ is even}. \end{cases}$$

Similarly, we have for the second moment that

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{w}_l}[\langle \tilde{w}_l, \theta^* \rangle^{2(s^*-1)}] &= \mathbb{E}_{w_l}[\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{2(s^*-1)} \cdot \mathbf{1}_{\{ \sup_i |\langle w_l, v_i \rangle| \leq \epsilon \}] \\ &\leq \mathbb{E}_{w_l}[\langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{2(s^*-1)}] \\ &\simeq (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{2(s^*-1)}.\end{aligned}$$

Given the boundedness on E(ϵ) and the second moment characterization, the Bernstein's inequality [\(Lemma J.1\)](#page-81-1) implies that there exists E1,<sup>2</sup> with Pr(E1,2) ≥ 1 − d <sup>−</sup><sup>c</sup>/(MT). Furthermore, it holds on E1,<sup>2</sup> ∩ E(ϵ) that

$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l, \theta^\star \rangle^{s^\star - 1} &= \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle \tilde{w}_l, \theta^\star \rangle^{s^\star - 1} \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{w_l} [\langle w_l, \theta^\star \rangle^{s^\star - 1}] + E, \end{aligned}$$

where the error term E is absolutely bounded as

$$\begin{aligned} |E| &\lesssim (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*-1} \cdot \sqrt{\frac{\log d}{L}} + \frac{(\epsilon \vee \gamma)^{s^*-1} \log d}{L} + \Pr(\mathcal{E}(\epsilon)^c) \\ &\lesssim (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*} + \Pr(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_m^{(t)}(\epsilon)) \\ &\lesssim (|\rho|\gamma + d^{-1/2})^{s^*}. \end{aligned}$$
