## G S TAT I S T I C A L QU E RY L O W E R B O U N D F O R S PA R S E S I G N A L RE C O V E RY

In this section, we provide a k s ⋆ sample complexity lower bound for the single index model with k-sparse signal when querying a VSTAT oracle. The statistical query (SQ) framework was developed in [Feldman et al.](#page-11-13) [\(2017\)](#page-11-13) and for completeness, we present essential definition and results here.

Definition G.1 (VSTAT Oracle). *Let* D<sup>⋆</sup> *be the input distribution over domain* X *. For a sample size parameter* n > 0*,* VSTAT(D<sup>⋆</sup> , n) *oracle is the oracle that for any query function* h : X → [0, 1]*, returns a value* v ∈ [p − τ, p + τ ]*, where* p = <sup>E</sup>x∼D<sup>⋆</sup> [h(x)] *and* τ = max{t −1 , p p(1 − p)/n}*.*

To define a key concept *statistical query dimension*, we first introduce the following notation.

Definition G.2 (Relative Pairwise Correlation). *Given two distributions* D1, D<sup>2</sup> ∈ ∆(X ) *and a reference distribution* D ∈ ∆(X )*,*

$$\chi_D(D_1, D_2) = \mathbb{E}_{x \sim D} \left[ \frac{D_1(x)}{D(x)} \cdot \frac{D_2(x)}{D(x)} \right] - 1.$$

Definition G.3 (Statistical Dimension). *For* γ ><sup>s</sup> 0*,* η ∈ (0, 1)*, domain* X *, a set of distributions* D *over* X *, the statistical dimension* SDA(D, γ, η <sup>s</sup> ) *of* D *with average correlation* γ<sup>s</sup> *and solution set bound* η *is defined as the largest value* m′ *such that there exists a* reference distribution D ∈ ∆(X ) *and a finite set of distributions* D<sup>D</sup> ⊆ D *which can depend on the reference* D *with the following property: for any solution* D<sup>⋆</sup> ∈ D*,*

$$(i) |\mathcal{D}_D \setminus \{D^*\}| \geq (1 - \eta)|\mathcal{D}_D|;$$

*(ii) for any subset* D′

<sup>D</sup> ⊆ D<sup>D</sup> \ {D<sup>⋆</sup>} *such that* |D′

<sup>D</sup>| ≥ |D<sup>D</sup> \ {D<sup>⋆</sup>}|/m′

*,*

$$\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}'_D|^2} \sum_{D_i, D_j \in \mathcal{D}'_D} \chi_D(D_i, D_j) \leq \bar{\gamma}.$$

The above definition of the statistical dimension is a speical case of the original Definition 3.1 in [Feldman et al.](#page-11-13) [\(2017\)](#page-11-13) where we consider a search problem of *exact recovery* of the ground truth D<sup>⋆</sup> . Definition G.4 ((γ, β)-correlated Distributions). *We say that a set of* m *distributions* D = {D1, . . . , Dm} *over* X *is* (γ, β)*-correlated relative to a reference distribution* D ∈ ∆(X ) *if:*

$$\chi_D(D_i, D_j) \leq \begin{cases} \beta & \text{for } i = j \in [m] \\ \gamma & \text{for } i \neq j \in [m]. \end{cases}$$

The following lemma borrowed from Lemma 3.10 of [Feldman et al.](#page-11-13) [\(2017\)](#page-11-13) provides a lower bound on the statistical dimension in terms of the (γ, β)-correlation property of the set of candidate distributions.

Lemma G.5. *Given a set of candidate distributions* D *that are* (γ, β)*-correlated with respect to a reference distribution* D*, then for any* γ ′ > 0 *and* η > |D|<sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> *,*

$$\text{SDA}(\mathcal{D}, \gamma + \gamma', \eta) \geq \frac{(|\mathcal{D}| - 1)\gamma'}{\beta - \gamma}.$$

The main result in the SQ framework is the following statement that relates the number of queries required to the statistical dimension, which is borrowed from Theorem 3.2 of [Feldman et al.](#page-11-13) [\(2017\)](#page-11-13).

Lemma G.6. *Let* X *be a domain and* D *be a set of candidate distributions over* X *. For any* γ ><sup>s</sup> 0 *and* η ∈ (0, 1)*, Any randomized SQ algorithm that solves the problem of finding the input distribution* D<sup>⋆</sup> ∈ D *with probability at least* α > η *requires at least* (α − η)/(1 − η) · SDA(D, γ, η <sup>s</sup> ) *calls to the* VSTAT(D<sup>⋆</sup> ,(3γs) −1 ) *oracle.*

Our strategy for proving the lower bound is to first construct a set of candidate distributions D that are (ω(k −1 ), β)-correlated with respect to reference distribution Q with β = Dχ<sup>2</sup> (<sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> ∥ Q) and |D| exponentially large. Then by [Lemma G.5](#page-62-1) and [Lemma G.6,](#page-62-2) we can derive the desired hardness result. It remains to construct the set of candidate distributions D that are (ω(k −1 ), β)-correlated with respect to Q. To this end, we introduce the following result on the packing number of k-sparse vectors.

Lemma G.7 (Packing Number for k-Sparse Vectors). *Define* ρ(u, v) = |⟨u, v⟩|*. Let packing number* Mρ(d, k, t) *be the maximal cardinality of the set of* k*-sparse vectors in* <sup>S</sup> d−1 *such that* ρ(u, v) < t *for any* u ̸= v *in the set. We have for any* t ∈ (1/k, 1) *that*

$$\mathcal{M}_\rho(d, k, t) \geq \frac{1}{2} \cdot \exp\left(\frac{\min\{(d-k)t^2, 3kt\}}{8}\right).$$

With all these ingredients in place, we are ready to prove the main theorem.

*Proof of [Theorem 5.4.](#page-9-0)* Let us pick parameter κ<sup>d</sup> ∈ ((log d) 2 , k/4) that scales with d and set

$$t \geq \max \left\{ \sqrt{\frac{\kappa_d}{d-k}}, \frac{\kappa_d}{3k} \right\} \in (1/k, 1/2). \quad (\text{G.1})$$

Note that t ∈ (1/k, 1/2) is able to hold by our choice of κ<sup>d</sup> and condition that ω((log d) 2 ) ≤ k ≤ d/2. In this vein, we can pick D to be the *maximal* set of distributions <sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> for some k-sparse vectors θ ∈ S d−1 satisfying ρ(θ, θ′ ) < t for any θ ̸= θ ′ in the set. It follows from plugging [\(G.1\)](#page-63-1) into [Lemma G.7](#page-62-3) that |D| ≥ exp(κd/8)/2, which is super polynomially large in d for our choice of κd.

Next, we configure the remaining parameters in [Lemma G.5](#page-62-1) and [Lemma G.6.](#page-62-2) We choose the reference distribution to be Q, in which the covariate z is independent of the output y. For β, we note that

$$\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{P}_\theta, \mathbb{P}_\theta) = D_{\chi^2}(\mathbb{P}_\theta \parallel \mathbb{Q}) = O(1),$$

which is a constant independent of θ due to the rotational invariance of the likelihood ratio with respect to θ. Thus, we define this quantity as B can just set β = Dχ<sup>2</sup> (<sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> ∥ Q) = B. For γ, we note that for any two <sup>P</sup>θ, <sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> ′ in D for θ ̸= θ ′ ,

$$\begin{aligned} |\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta}, \mathbb{P}_{\theta'})| &= \left| \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathbb{Q}} \left[ \frac{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}(x)}{\mathbb{Q}(x)} \cdot \frac{\mathbb{P}_{\theta'}(x)}{\mathbb{Q}(x)} \right] - 1 \right| \\ &= \left| \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathbb{Q}} \left[ \left( 1 + \sum_{s \geq s^*} \zeta_s(y) h_s(\langle \theta, z \rangle) \right) \cdot \left( 1 + \sum_{s' \geq s^*} \zeta_{s'}(y) h_{s'}(\langle \theta', z \rangle) \right) \right] - 1 \right| \\ &= \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y)^2] \cdot |\langle \theta, \theta' \rangle|^s \leq \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y)^2] \cdot t^s \leq \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y)^2] \cdot t^{s^*} + \frac{t^{s^*+1}}{1-t}. \end{aligned}$$

Here, the third equality follows from the fact that only when s = s ′ , the cross term <sup>E</sup>Q[hs(⟨θ, z⟩)hs(⟨θ ′ , z⟩)] is non-zero. In particular, by the property of the Gaussian noise operator introduced in [\(B.3\)](#page-17-4), we have that <sup>E</sup>Q[hs(⟨θ, z⟩)hs(⟨θ ′ , z⟩)] = ⟨θ, θ′ ⟩ <sup>s</sup> < t<sup>s</sup> . For the last inequality above, we simply use the fact that <sup>E</sup>Q[ζs(y) 2 ] ≤ 1 for any s [\(Damian et al.,](#page-11-4) [2024\)](#page-11-4) and t < 1. Now, we conclude that

$$|\chi_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{P}_\theta, \mathbb{P}_{\theta'})| \leq \left( \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y)^2] + \frac{t}{1-t} \right) \cdot t^{s^*} \leq (\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^*}(y)^2] + 1) \cdot t^{s^*}.$$

We thus set γ ′ = γ = <sup>E</sup>Q[ζ<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> (y) 2 ] + 1 ·t s ⋆ = Θ(t s ⋆ ). Finally, we set η = 1/3 and α = 2/3. Then all the conditions in both [Lemma G.5](#page-62-1) and [Lemma G.6](#page-62-2) are satisfied and we have

$$\text{SDA}(\mathcal{D}, 2\gamma, 1/3) \geq \frac{(|\mathcal{D}| - 1)\gamma}{\beta - \gamma} \geq \frac{|\mathcal{D}|\gamma}{2\beta} \geq \frac{\gamma \exp(\kappa_d/8)}{4\beta}.$$

Lastly, recall that we have |⟨θ, θ′ ⟩| ≤ t for any θ ̸= θ ′ in D, which means that in order to achieve alignment at least 2t with the true signal θ ⋆ , we need to *exactly* identify the distribution P<sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> . Consequently, by [Lemma G.6,](#page-62-2) we have that any randomized SQ algorithm that solves the problem of achieving alignment 2t with probability at least 2/3 requires at least γ exp(κd/8)/(8B) calls to the VSTAT(<sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> ,(6γ) −1 ) oracle.

Simplification of the lower bound. To simplify the lower bound, let us take κ<sup>d</sup> = (log d) <sup>c</sup>/2 for some constant c > 2. Thus, the alignment 2t is upper bounded by

$$2t \leq \begin{cases} \tilde{\omega}(k^{-1}) & \text{if } k < \sqrt{d} \\ \tilde{\omega}(d^{-1/2}) & \text{if } k \geq \sqrt{d} \end{cases},$$

where ωr(·) hides some poly-logarithmic factors. The number of queries is still super polynomially large in d. Following from [\(G.1\)](#page-63-1), we can safely set

$$t = \begin{cases} (\log d)^c / k & \text{if } (\log d)^2 < k < \sqrt{d(\log d)^c} \\ \sqrt{(\log d)^c / d} & \text{if } \sqrt{d(\log d)^c} \leq k \leq d/2 \end{cases},$$

Hence, the number of sample

$$(6\gamma)^{-1} = \frac{t^{-s^*}}{6} \simeq \begin{cases} \frac{k^{s^*}}{(\log d)^{cs^*}} & \text{if } (\log d)^2 < k < \sqrt{d(\log d)^c} \\ \frac{d^{s^*/2}}{6(\log d)^{cs^*/2}} & \text{if } \sqrt{d(\log d)^c} \leq k \leq d/2 \end{cases}$$

Hence, we have established the desired lower bound on the sample complexity.

Remark G.8 (Difference between CSQ and SQ lower bound). *The following is a comparison between our SQ lower bound and the CSQ lower bound in [Vural & Erdogdu](#page-12-6) [\(2024\)](#page-12-6):*

- *Difference: CSQ is constrained to operate on a specific set of functions, whereas SQ is not. In this sense, CSQ creates a large family of functions with small average correlation, while SQ constructs a large covering net over the support of* θ *as a hard instance, allowing for arbitrary query forms.*
- *Similarity: Both methods rely on Bernstein concentration inequalities on the correlation level for two random sparse vectors. This leads to the critical correlation threshold* max{1/ √ d, 1/k}*, where* d *is the dimensionality and* k *is the sparsity level.*

#### G . 1 P R O O F O F TE C H N I C A L LE M M A S F O R S Q L O W E R B O U N D

*Proof of [Lemma G.7.](#page-62-3)* We use the probability method to prove the existence of a set of k-sparse vectors in S <sup>d</sup>−<sup>1</sup> with the desired property. We i.i.d. sample m vectors ω (1), . . . , ω(m) from the following distribution:

$$\omega : \phi \sim \text{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_k), \quad \omega_j = \begin{cases} \frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}, & \text{w.p. } \frac{1}{2} \text{ if } j \in \phi \\ -\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}, & \text{w.p. } \frac{1}{2} \text{ if } j \in \phi, \\ 0, & j \notin \phi. \end{cases}$$

where we recall that S<sup>k</sup> is the set of all size-k subsets in [d]. Since each ω (i) is i.i.d. sampled, we can equivalently view ⟨ω (i) , ω(j) ⟩ for i ̸= j as a random variable sampled from the following distribution:

$$\langle \omega^{(i)}, \omega^{(j)} \rangle = \frac{d}{k} \frac{R_X}{k}, \quad \text{where} \quad R_X = r_1 + \dots, r_X, \quad X \sim \text{Hypergeometric}(d, k, k), \quad (\text{G.2})$$

where r1, r2, . . . are i.i.d. Rademacher random variables. Let us consider random variable W distributed as

$$W = \frac{d}{k} \frac{R_Y}{k}, \quad \text{where} \quad R_Y = r_1 + \dots + r_Y, \quad Y \sim \text{Binomial}\left(k, \frac{k}{d-k}\right). \quad (\text{G.3})$$

We will invoke the following fact on the tail probability regarding the above two random variables.

Proposition G.9. *For* R<sup>X</sup> *and* R<sup>Y</sup> *defined in* [\(G.2\)](#page-64-3) *and* [\(G.3\)](#page-64-4)*, respectively, we have that* <sup>P</sup>(R<sup>X</sup> ≥ t) ≤ 2P(R<sup>Y</sup> ≥ t) *for any* t > 1*.*

The proof of the proposition is deferred to the end of the proof. Thus, it suffices to study the tail probability of W. Note that W d= P<sup>k</sup> <sup>j</sup>=1 w<sup>j</sup> where w<sup>j</sup> are i.i.d. sampled from

$$w_j = \begin{cases} \frac{1}{k}, & \text{w.p. } \frac{k}{2(d-k)} \\ -\frac{1}{k}, & \text{w.p. } \frac{k}{2(d-k)} \\ 0, & \text{w.p. } 1 - \frac{k}{d-k} \end{cases}, \quad j \in [k].$$

where <sup>E</sup>[w<sup>j</sup> ] = 0 and <sup>E</sup>[w 2 j ] = (k(d − k))<sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> . Hence, we can apply the Bernstein inequality to obtain that for any t > 1/k,

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{P}(\langle \omega^{(i)}, \omega^{(j)} \rangle \geq t) &\leq 2\mathbb{P}(W \geq t) \leq 2 \exp\left(-\frac{k(t/k)^2/2}{(k(d-k))^{-1} + t/(3k^2)}\right) \\ &= 2 \exp\left(-\frac{k^2 t^2}{2k^2/(d-k) + 2kt/3}\right) \leq 2 \exp\left(-\min\left\{\frac{(d-k)t^2}{4}, \frac{3kt}{4}\right\}\right).\end{aligned}$$

Suppose we randomly sample m i.i.d. ω (i) from the same distribution. Then the probability that all such pair |⟨ω (i) , ω(j) ⟩| < t for t > 1/k is lower bounded by

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}(|\langle \omega^{(i)}, \omega^{(j)} \rangle| < t, \forall i \neq j) &\geq 1 - m^2 \cdot 2\mathbb{P}(\langle \omega^{(i)}, \omega^{(j)} \rangle \geq t) \\ &\geq 1 - 4m^2 \cdot \exp \left( -\min \left\{ \frac{(d-k)t^2}{4}, \frac{3kt}{4} \right\} \right). \end{aligned}$$

Ensuring that the probability is nonzero will give us a valid construction of the set D. Therefore, there must exist a D satisfying |⟨ω (i) , ω(j) ⟩| < t for any i ̸= j and with size

$$|\mathcal{D}| \geq \frac{1}{2} \cdot \exp \left( \frac{\min \{ (d-k)t^2, 3kt \}}{8} \right)$$

.

Hence, we complete the proof.

Next, we aim to present the proof of [Proposition G.9.](#page-64-5) To proceed, let us introduce the definition of stochastic dominance.

Definition G.10 (Stochastic Dominance). *For any real-valued random variable* X *and* Y *, we say that* X *is* stochastically dominated *by* Y *, denoted by* X s.t. ≤ Y *, if* <sup>P</sup>(X ≥ t) ≤ <sup>P</sup>(Y ≥ t) *for every* t*.* The following result is from Theorem A, Chapter 2 of [Szekli](#page-12-19) [\(2012\)](#page-12-19).

Proposition G.11. *We have* X s.t. ≤ Y *if and only if there exists a coupling* (X, <sup>p</sup> Yp) *with* law(Xp) = law(X) *and* law(Yp) = law(Y ) *such that* X<sup>p</sup> ≤ Y<sup>p</sup> *almost surely.*

Proposition G.12 (Theorem 1.1, [Klenke & Mattner](#page-12-20) [\(2010\)](#page-12-20)). Hypergeometric(d, k, k) s.t. ≤ Binomial(k, k/(d − k))*.*

Another way to think of the problem is that Hypergeometric(d, k, k) corresponds to the number of times a black ball is drawn when sampling for k times from an urn with d − k white ball and k black ball without replacement, while Binomial(k, k/(d − k)) corresponds to sampling in the same urn but with replacement. We claim the following fact on the tail probability of sum of Rademacher random variables.

Proposition G.13 (Sum of Rademacher Random Variables). *Let* r1, r2, . . . *be i.i.d. Rademacher random variables. Let* R<sup>l</sup> = r<sup>1</sup> + . . . + r<sup>l</sup> *for* l = 1, 2, . . .*. Let* pl(·) *be the probability mass function of* B<sup>l</sup> *. Then the following holds for any* l = 1, 2, . . .*:*

- *1.* p<sup>l</sup> *is symmetric and supported on the set of odd integers if* l *is odd, and supported on the set of even integers if* l *is even.*
- *2. For* i ∈ supp(pl) *and* i ≥ 0*,* pl(i) *is a non-increasing function of* i*.*
- *3.* <sup>P</sup>(R<sup>l</sup> ≥ t) ≤ <sup>P</sup>(Rl+2 ≥ t) *for any* t > 1*.*
- *4.* <sup>P</sup>(R<sup>l</sup> ≥ t) ≤ 2P(Rl+1 ≥ t) *for any* t > 1*.*
- *5.* <sup>P</sup>(R<sup>l</sup> ≥ t) ≤ 2P(Rl+<sup>l</sup> ′ ≥ t) *for any* l ≥ 1 *and* l ′ ≥ 1*.*

*Proof of [Proposition G.13.](#page-65-1)* The first claim is immediate from the symmetry of the Rademacher random variables and the fact that the sum of an odd number of Rademacher random variables is odd, while the sum of an even number of Rademacher random variables is even. For the second claim, we note that

$$p_l(i) = 2^{-l} \cdot \binom{l}{(i+l)/2}, \quad i \in \text{supp}(p_l),$$

which is a non-increasing function for i ≥ 0. For the third claim, we let t <sup>⋆</sup> = 2⌈t/2⌉ if l is even and t <sup>⋆</sup> = 2⌈(t − 1)/2⌉ + 1 if l is odd. In other words, t <sup>⋆</sup> = min{τ ∈ supp(pl) : τ ≥ t}. Then we have

that

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{P}(R_{l+2} \geq t) &= \mathbb{P}(R_l \geq t^* + 2) + \mathbb{P}(R_l = t^*) \cdot \mathbb{P}(r_{l+1} + r_{l+2} \geq 0) \\ &\quad + \mathbb{P}(R_l = t^* - 2) \cdot \mathbb{P}(r_{l+1} + r_{l+2} = 2) \\ &= \mathbb{P}(R_l \geq t^*) + (\mathbb{P}(R_l = t^* - 2) - \mathbb{P}(R_l = t^*)) \cdot \mathbb{P}(r_{l+1} + r_{l+2} = 2) \\ &\geq \mathbb{P}(R_l \geq t^*) = \mathbb{P}(R_l \geq t).\end{aligned}$$

where in the first equality we use the fact that rl+1 + rl+2 is supported on {−2, 0, 2} and in the second equality we use the symmetric property of the distribution of rl+1 + rl+2. The last inequality follows from the monotonicity of the probability mass function of R<sup>l</sup> for t <sup>⋆</sup> − 2 ≥ 0 when t > 1. For the forth claim, we similarly have that

$$\mathbb{P}(R_{l+1} \geq t) \geq \mathbb{P}(R_l \geq t^*) - \mathbb{P}(R_l = t^*) \cdot \mathbb{P}(r_{l+1} = -1) \geq \frac{1}{2} \mathbb{P}(R_l \geq t^*) = \frac{1}{2} \mathbb{P}(R_l \geq t).$$

The last claim follows from a combination of the third and forth claims where

$$\mathbb{P}(R_{l+l'} \geq t) \geq \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(R_{l+2[l'/2]} \geq t) \geq \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(R_{l+2[l'/2]-2} \geq t) \geq \dots \geq \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(R_l \geq t).$$

Hence, the proof is complete.

Next, we proceed to the proof of [Proposition G.9.](#page-64-5)

*Proof of [Proposition G.9.](#page-64-5)* By [Proposition G.12](#page-65-2) and [Proposition G.11,](#page-65-3) there exists a coupling X, <sup>p</sup> Y<sup>p</sup> with law(Xp) = law(X) and law(Yp) = law(Y ) such that X<sup>p</sup> ≤ Y<sup>p</sup> almost surely where X ∼ Hypergeometric(d, k, k) and Y ∼ Binomial(k, k/(d − k)).

Consider i.i.d. Rademacher random variables r1, r2, . . . , rk. Let R<sup>l</sup> = r<sup>1</sup> + . . . + r<sup>l</sup> for l = 1, 2, . . .. Since <sup>R</sup>X<sup>x</sup> <sup>=</sup> <sup>r</sup><sup>1</sup> <sup>+</sup> . . . <sup>+</sup> <sup>r</sup>X<sup>x</sup> <sup>|</sup> <sup>X</sup><sup>p</sup> <sup>d</sup>= 2Binomial(X, <sup>p</sup> <sup>1</sup>/2) <sup>−</sup> <sup>L</sup> and <sup>R</sup>Y<sup>p</sup> <sup>=</sup> <sup>r</sup><sup>1</sup> <sup>+</sup> . . . <sup>+</sup> <sup>r</sup>Y<sup>p</sup> <sup>|</sup> <sup>Y</sup><sup>p</sup> d= 2Binomial(Y , <sup>p</sup> 1/2) − L for the coupling (X, <sup>p</sup> Yp) with X<sup>p</sup> ≤ Yp, we consider the conditional random variable

$$r_{\widehat{X}+i} \mid (R_{\widehat{X}+i-1}, \widehat{X}, \widehat{Y}) = r_{\widehat{X}+i} = \begin{cases} 1, & \text{w.p. } 1/2 \\ -1, & \text{w.p. } 1/2 \end{cases}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots, \widehat{Y} - \widehat{X}$$

The equality holds by the i.i.d. property of these Rademacher random variables. From the distributional perspective, the distribution of RY<sup>p</sup> is obtained by conducting convolution with the Rademacher distribution for <sup>Y</sup><sup>p</sup> <sup>−</sup> <sup>X</sup><sup>p</sup> times on the distribution of <sup>R</sup>X<sup>x</sup>. Invoking [Proposition G.13,](#page-65-1) we directly conclude that <sup>P</sup>(RY<sup>p</sup> <sup>≥</sup> <sup>t</sup><sup>|</sup> X, <sup>p</sup> <sup>Y</sup>p) <sup>≥</sup> <sup>P</sup>(RX<sup>x</sup> <sup>≥</sup> <sup>t</sup><sup>|</sup> X, <sup>p</sup> <sup>Y</sup>p)/<sup>2</sup> for any t > <sup>1</sup> and <sup>Y</sup><sup>p</sup> <sup>≥</sup> <sup>X</sup>p. As <sup>Y</sup><sup>p</sup> <sup>≥</sup> <sup>X</sup><sup>p</sup> holds almost surely, by the law of total probability, we arrive at the conclusion that <sup>P</sup>(RY<sup>p</sup> ≥ t) ≥ <sup>P</sup>(RX<sup>x</sup> ≥ t)/2 for any t > 1.

## H S U P P O R T I N G LE M M A S O N M O M E N T C A L C U L AT I O N S

Lemma H.1 (First moment). *Under [Assumption 4.1,](#page-6-2) for any* s ≥ 0*, it holds for any* y ∈ R *and* w, θ ∈ S d−1 *that*

$$\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) z \cdot h_s(\langle \theta, z \rangle)] = \sqrt{s+1} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y) \cdot \langle w, \theta \rangle^s w + \sqrt{s} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s-1}(y) \cdot \langle w, \theta \rangle^{s-1} \theta,$$

*in the* L 2 *sense over the marginal distribution of* y *under* Q*.*

*Proof of [Lemma H.1.](#page-66-0)* For convenience, we denote ρ := ⟨w, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩. We claim the following identities:

$$\begin{aligned} & \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\psi(y, w^\top z) z \cdot h_s(\theta^{\star\top} z)] \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot \theta^{\star\top} z \cdot h_s(\theta^{\star\top} z) \right] \cdot \theta^\star + \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot h_s(\theta^{\star\top} z) \cdot P_{\theta^\star}^\perp z \right] \quad (\text{H.1}) \\ &= \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot \theta^{\star\top} z \cdot h_s(\theta^{\star\top} z) \right]}_{(\text{I})} \cdot \frac{\theta^\star - \rho w}{1 - \rho^2} + \underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot w^\top z \cdot h_s(\theta^{\star\top} z) \right]}_{(\text{II})} \cdot \frac{w - \rho \theta^\star}{1 - \rho^2}. \end{aligned}$$

Here, in the first identity, we project z in the direction of θ ⋆ and the orthogonal complement of θ ⋆ , where P ⊥ θ <sup>⋆</sup> = I − θ ⋆ θ <sup>⋆</sup><sup>⊤</sup> is the projection operator onto the orthogonal complement of θ ⋆ . To see how the second identity holds, we first look at the second term <sup>E</sup>z∼N<sup>d</sup> h ψ(y, w⊤z) · hs(θ ⋆⊤ z) · P ⊥ θ <sup>⋆</sup> z i . For each direction v orthogonal to both θ ⋆ and w, we have

$$\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot h_s(\theta^\star z) \cdot \langle P_{\theta^\star}^\perp z, v \rangle \right] = \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d, x \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot h_s(\theta^\star z) \cdot x \right] = 0.$$

Also, by projection P ⊥ θ <sup>⋆</sup> z is always orthogonal to θ ⋆ . Thus, the only direction left for consideration is v = (w − ρθ<sup>⋆</sup> )/ p 1 − ρ <sup>2</sup>, for which we have

$$\begin{aligned} & \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot h_s(\theta^{\star\top} z) \cdot \langle P_{\theta^{\star}}^\perp z, v \rangle \right] \cdot v \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot h_s(\theta^{\star\top} z) \cdot \frac{w^\top z - \rho \theta^{\star\top} z}{\sqrt{1 - \rho^2}} \right] \cdot \frac{w - \rho \theta^\star}{\sqrt{1 - \rho^2}} \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \psi(y, w^\top z) \cdot h_s(\theta^{\star\top} z) \cdot (w^\top z - \rho \theta^{\star\top} z) \right] \cdot \frac{w - \rho \theta^\star}{1 - \rho^2}. \end{aligned} \quad (\text{H.2})$$

Plugging Eq. [\(H.2\)](#page-67-1) into the second term of line 2 in Eq. [\(H.1\)](#page-66-2), we thus have the last identity in Eq. [\(H.1\)](#page-66-2). Next, we analyze terms (I) and (II) in Eq. [\(H.1\)](#page-66-2). For our convenience, we define U<sup>ρ</sup> as the Gaussian noise operator such that

$$\text{U}_\rho \psi(y, x) = \mathbb{E}_{x' \sim \mathcal{N}} \left[ \psi(y, \rho x + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2} x') \right].$$

For term (I), we have by the definition of U<sup>ρ</sup> that

$$\begin{aligned} (\mathbf{I}) &= \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{N}}} [\mathbf{U}_\rho \psi(y, x) \cdot x \cdot h_s(x)] \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\sqrt{s+1} \cdot \mathbf{U}_\rho \psi(y, x) \cdot h_{s+1}(x) + \sqrt{s} \cdot \mathbf{U}_\rho \psi(y, x) \cdot h_{s-1}(x)] \\ L^2 &\stackrel{(\mathbb{Q})}{=} \sqrt{s+1} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y) \cdot \rho^{s+1} + \sqrt{s} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s-1}(y) \cdot \rho^{s-1}. \end{aligned} \quad (\text{H.3})$$

where the second line follows from the recurrence relation of the Hermite polynomials in Eq. [\(B.1\)](#page-17-2), and the last line follows from the property of the Gaussian noise operator in Eq. [\(B.3\)](#page-17-4). Similarly for term (II), we have

$$\begin{aligned} (\mathbf{II}) &= \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} [\mathbf{U}_\rho(\psi(y, x)x) \cdot h_s(x)] \\ &\stackrel{L^2(\mathbb{Q})}{=} \rho^s \cdot \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} [\psi(y, x) \cdot x \cdot h_s(x)] \\ &\stackrel{L^2(\mathbb{Q})}{=} \rho^s \cdot \left( \sqrt{s+1} \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s+1}(y) + \sqrt{s} \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s-1}(y) \right), \end{aligned} \quad (\mathbf{H.4})$$

where in the last line we borrow the calculation in Eq. [\(H.3\)](#page-67-2) by letting ρ = 1. Plugging Eq. [\(H.3\)](#page-67-2) and [\(H.4\)](#page-67-3) into Eq. [\(H.1\)](#page-66-2), we hence have

$$\begin{aligned}
 (H.1) \quad & {}^{L^2(\mathbb{Q})} \left( \sqrt{s+1} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y) \cdot \rho^{s+1} + \sqrt{s} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s-1}(y) \cdot \rho^{s-1} \right) \cdot \frac{\theta^* - \rho w}{1 - \rho^2} \\
 & + \rho^s \cdot \left( \sqrt{s+1} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y) + \sqrt{s} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s-1}(y) \right) \cdot \frac{w - \rho \theta^*}{1 - \rho^2} \\
 & = \sqrt{s+1} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y) \cdot \rho^s w + \sqrt{s} \cdot \widehat{\psi}_{s-1}(y) \cdot \rho^{s-1} \theta^*,
 \end{aligned}$$

which completes the proof.

An implication of the previous lemma is that

$$\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h_{s^*}(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \cdot \sigma'(\langle z, \theta \rangle) \cdot \langle z, \theta^* \rangle] = s \cdot \hat{\sigma}^{(s^*)} \cdot \langle \theta^*, \theta \rangle^{s^*-1} + \sqrt{(s+1)(s+2)} \cdot \hat{\sigma}^{(s^*+2)} \cdot \langle \theta^*, \theta \rangle^{s^*+1},$$

where we take σp (s) as the s-th normalized Hermite coefficient of σ. Here, we take ψ(y, x) as σ ′ (x) and thus <sup>ψ</sup>ps(y) = √ s + 1 · σp (s+1) .

Lemma H.2 (Decomposition of first order moment). *Suppose that* ψ *follows [Assumption 4.1](#page-6-2) and*

$$g = \frac{1}{nL} \sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{l=1}^L (\psi(y_i, \langle w_l, z_i \rangle) \cdot z_i - \hat{\psi}_1(y_i) \cdot w_l),$$

*where* (z<sup>i</sup> , yi) <sup>i</sup>.i.d. ∼ <sup>P</sup><sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> *and* {wl}l≤<sup>L</sup> *is fixed. Then it holds that*

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[g] &= \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s-1}(y)] \cdot \frac{\sqrt{s}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s-1} \cdot \theta^* \\ &\quad + \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)] \cdot \frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^s \cdot w_l. \end{aligned}$$

*Proof of [Lemma H.2.](#page-67-0)* Applying a change of measure from P<sup>θ</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> to Q and invoking Eq. [\(2.2\)](#page-2-2), we get

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[g] &= \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}} \left[ \psi(y, \langle w_l, z \rangle) \cdot z - \hat{\psi}_1(y) \cdot w_l \right] \\ &= \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ \psi(y, \langle w_l, z \rangle) z \cdot \left( 1 + \sum_{s \geq s^*} \zeta_s(y) h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \right) - \hat{\psi}_1(y) w_l \right], \quad (\text{H.5})\end{aligned}$$

Note that for s = 0, we have for the first term in the summation of Eq. [\(H.5\)](#page-68-2) that

$$L^{-1} \sum_{l=1}^L \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\psi(y, \langle w_l, z \rangle) z] = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\hat{\psi}_1(y)] \cdot \frac{1}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L w_l,$$

which is cancelled out by the debiasing term in the algorithm. Applying the result of [Lemma H.1](#page-66-0) to the remaining terms in Eq. [\(H.5\)](#page-68-2) yields

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^*}}[g] &= \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)] \cdot \frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^s \cdot w_l \\ &\quad + \sum_{s \geq s^*} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_s(y) \cdot \hat{\psi}_{s-1}(y)] \cdot \frac{\sqrt{s}}{L} \sum_{l=1}^L \langle w_l, \theta^* \rangle^{s-1} \cdot \theta^*, \end{aligned} \quad (\text{H.6})$$

where the <sup>E</sup>Q[ψp1(y)] · 1 L P<sup>L</sup> <sup>l</sup>=1 w<sup>l</sup> term from [Lemma H.1](#page-66-0) with s = 0 is cancelled out by the debiasing term in the algorithm.

Lemma H.3 (Second moment on nice event). *Suppose* ψ : R × R → R *satisfies the quadrupleintegrable and high-pass assumptions in [Assumption 4.1.](#page-6-2) Let* s <sup>⋆</sup> *be the generative exponent defined in [Definition 2.1.](#page-2-5) Suppose* <sup>E</sup>Q[ζs(y) 2 ] ≤ C *for some universal* C = O(1) *and for all* s ≥ s ⋆ *. For any* w, w′ , θ<sup>⋆</sup> , v ∈ S <sup>d</sup>−<sup>1</sup> *where either* v = θ <sup>⋆</sup> *or* ⟨v, θ<sup>⋆</sup> ⟩ = 0 *in the non-sparse case, and either* v = e<sup>j</sup> *for* j ∈ supp(θ ⋆ ) *or* v = e<sup>j</sup> *for* j /∈ supp(θ ⋆ ) *in the sparse case, suppose that*

$$\max\{|\langle v, w \rangle|, |\langle v, w' \rangle|\} \leq \epsilon_0, \quad \max\{|\langle \theta^*, w \rangle|, |\langle \theta^*, w' \rangle|\} \leq \epsilon, \quad |\langle w, w' \rangle| \leq \epsilon_1$$

*for some* ϵ, ϵ0, ϵ<sup>1</sup> *such that* 4es<sup>⋆</sup> ϵ < 1/2*. Then, we have for* s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2 *that*

$$\begin{aligned} & \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ \psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \langle v, z \rangle^2 \cdot \left( 1 + \sum_{s=s^*}^{\infty} \zeta_s(y) h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \right) \right] \\ & \lesssim \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \cdot \left( 1 + \frac{\epsilon^2}{\epsilon_1} + \left( \frac{\epsilon^2}{\epsilon_1} \right)^{s^*-1} \cdot \epsilon + \mathbb{1}(v \perp \theta^*) \cdot \left( \frac{\epsilon^2}{\epsilon_1} \right)^{s^*-2} \cdot \frac{\epsilon_0^2}{\epsilon_1} \cdot (\epsilon^2 + \epsilon \cdot \mathbb{1}(s^* \geq 4)) \right), \end{aligned}$$

*Proof.* Using the results from [Proposition I.1,](#page-71-2) we have that

$$\begin{aligned} h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \langle v, z \rangle^2 &= \sqrt{(s+2)(s+1)} \cdot \mathbf{h}_{s+2}(z) [\langle \theta^* \rangle^{\otimes s} \otimes v^{\otimes 2}] + \mathbf{h}_s(z) [\langle \theta^* \rangle^{\otimes s}] \\ &\quad + 2s \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z) [\langle \theta^* \rangle^{\otimes s-1} \otimes v]^\top \cdot \langle \theta^*, v \rangle + \sqrt{s(s-1)} \cdot \mathbf{h}_{s-2}(z) [\langle \theta^* \rangle^{\otimes s-2}] \cdot \langle \theta^*, v \rangle^2. \end{aligned} \quad (\text{H.7})$$

Thus, we only need to focus on these degree terms in ψ(y,⟨w, z⟩)ψ(y,⟨w ′ , z⟩). Our goal is to compute the following quantity, which we denoted by F:

$$\begin{aligned} F &= \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ \psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \langle v, z \rangle^2 \cdot \left( 1 + \sum_{s=s^*}^{\infty} \zeta_s(y) h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle) \right) \right] \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \langle v, z \rangle^2] \\ &\quad + \sum_{s=s^*}^{\infty} |\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\zeta_s(y) \psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \langle v, z \rangle^2 h_s(\langle \theta^*, z \rangle)]|. \end{aligned} \quad (\text{H.8})$$

Here, for the term corresponding to s = 0 in Eq. [\(H.8\)](#page-69-0), we plug in Eq. [\(H.7\)](#page-69-1) and have by [Lemma I.3](#page-73-0) that

$$\begin{aligned} & |\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \langle v, z \rangle^2]| \\ &= \left| \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} \left[ \psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) (\sqrt{2} \cdot \mathbf{h}_2(z) [v^{\otimes 2}] + 1) \right] \right| \\ &\lesssim \epsilon_1^{(s^*-2) \vee 0} \cdot \epsilon_0^{2 \wedge c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(2-c_0) \vee 0} + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \lesssim \epsilon_1^{(s^*-2) \vee 0} \cdot \epsilon_0^2 + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \lesssim \mathbb{1}(s^* = 1) + \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \epsilon_0^2 + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1}. \end{aligned}$$

Here, to use [Lemma I.3,](#page-73-0) for h2(z)[v ⊗2 ] we take test tensor T<sup>2</sup> = v ⊗2 and set c<sup>0</sup> = 2. The last line also holds by using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for <sup>E</sup>Q[|ζs(y)| · ψ(y, x) 2 ] ≤ <sup>E</sup>Q[|ζs(y)| 2 1/2 · <sup>E</sup>Q[ψ(y, x) 4 <sup>1</sup>/<sup>2</sup> ≤ <sup>E</sup>Q[ψ(y, x) 4 <sup>1</sup>/<sup>2</sup> = O(1). As for the case s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1, we already have a constant outside, and noting that the second moment is at most O(1) due to the quadruple-integrable assumption, it suffices to consider in the following s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2. For the second part of Eq. [\(H.8\)](#page-69-0), we can split the expectation according to Eq. [\(H.7\)](#page-69-1). For the first term in Eq. [\(H.7\)](#page-69-1) which corresponds to p (s + 2)(s + 1) · hs+2(z)[(θ ⋆ ) <sup>⊗</sup><sup>s</sup> ⊗ v ⊗2 ], we take test tensor T<sup>s</sup> = v <sup>⊗</sup><sup>2</sup> ⊗ (θ ⋆ ) <sup>⊗</sup>(s−2) with c<sup>0</sup> = 2, s<sup>0</sup> = s <sup>⋆</sup> + 2 and have by [Proposition I.4](#page-75-0) that

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| \sum_{s=s^*}^{\infty} \sqrt{(s+2)(s+1)} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\zeta_s(y) \psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \mathbf{h}_{s+2}(z) [(\theta^*)^{\otimes s} \otimes v^{\otimes 2}]] \right| \\ & \lesssim \mathbf{1}(s_0 \leq c_0) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor s_0/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s_0} + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor c_0/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \right) + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(2s^*) \vee s_0-c_0} \\ & \quad + \mathbf{1}(s_0 \leq 2(s^*-1)) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor (c_0+1)/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{2(s^*-1)+1-c_0} \right) \\ & \lesssim \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-2} + \mathbf{1}(s^* \geq 4) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \cdot \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-3} \right). \end{aligned}$$

For the second term hs(z)[(θ ⋆ ) ⊗s ], we take test tensor T<sup>s</sup> = (θ ⋆ ) <sup>⊗</sup><sup>s</sup> with c<sup>0</sup> = 0, s<sup>0</sup> = s ⋆ and have by [Proposition I.4](#page-75-0) that

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| \sum_{s=s^*}^{\infty} \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\zeta_s(y)\psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle)\psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle)\mathbf{h}_s(z)[(\theta^*)^{\otimes s}]] \right| \\ & \lesssim \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(2s^*) \vee s_0 - c_0} + \left( \epsilon_1^{s^* - 1 - \lfloor (c_0 + 1)/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{2(s^* - 1) + 1 - c_0} \right) \\ & \lesssim \epsilon^{2s^*} + \epsilon_1^{s^* - 1} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon^{2s^* - 1} \lesssim \epsilon_1^{s^* - 1} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon^{2s^* - 1}. \end{aligned}$$

For the third term 2s · hs(z)[(θ ⋆ ) <sup>⊗</sup>s−<sup>1</sup> ⊗ v] <sup>⊤</sup> · ⟨θ ⋆ , v⟩, we take test tensor T<sup>s</sup> = v ⊗ (θ ⋆ ) <sup>⊗</sup>s−<sup>1</sup> with c<sup>0</sup> = 1, s<sup>0</sup> = s ⋆ and have by [Proposition I.4](#page-75-0) that

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| \sum_{s=s^*}^{\infty} 2s \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\zeta_s(y)\psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle)\psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \mathbf{h}_s(z)[(\theta^*)^{\otimes s-1} \otimes v]^\top] \right| \\ & \lesssim \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(2s^*) \vee s_0 - c_0} + \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor (c_0+1)/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{2(s^*-1)+1-c_0} \right) \\ & \lesssim \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-1} + \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \cdot \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-2} \lesssim \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \cdot \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-2}. \end{aligned}$$

For the last term p s(s − 1) · hs−2(z)[(θ ⋆ ) ⊗s−2 ] · ⟨θ ⋆ , v⟩ 2 , we take test tensor T<sup>s</sup> = (θ ⋆ ) <sup>⊗</sup><sup>s</sup> with c<sup>0</sup> = 0, s<sup>0</sup> = s <sup>⋆</sup> − 2 and have by [Proposition I.4](#page-75-0) that

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| \sum_{s=s^*}^{\infty} \sqrt{s(s-1)} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\zeta_s(y) \psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \mathbf{h}_{s-2}(z) [(\theta^*)^{\otimes s-2}] \cdot \langle \theta^*, v \rangle^2] \right| \\ & \lesssim \mathbf{1}(s^* = 2) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor s_0/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s_0} + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor c_0/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \right) + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(2s^*) \vee s_0-c_0} \\ & \quad + \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor (c_0+1)/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{2(s^*-1)+1-c_0} \right) \\ & \lesssim \mathbf{1}(s^* = 2) \epsilon_1 + \epsilon^{2s^*} + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon^{2s^*-1} \lesssim \mathbf{1}(s^* = 2) \epsilon_1 + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon^{2s^*-1}. \end{aligned}$$

Summing up the above terms, we have that for s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2,

$$\begin{aligned}
F &\lesssim \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \epsilon_0^2 + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \right) + \left( \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-2} + \mathbb{1}(s^* \geq 4) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \cdot \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-3} \right) \right) \\
&\quad + \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon^{2s^*-1} \right) + \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \cdot \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-2} + \mathbb{1}(s^* = 2) \epsilon_1 + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon^{2s^*-1} \right) \mathbb{1}(v = \theta^*) \\
&\lesssim \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \epsilon_0^2 + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} + \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-2} + \mathbb{1}(s^* \geq 4) \cdot \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-3} + \epsilon^{2s^*-1} \\
&\quad + \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \cdot \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-2} \right) \mathbb{1}(v = \theta^*).
\end{aligned}$$

If v = θ ⋆ , then we additionally have ϵ<sup>0</sup> = ϵ, which simplifies the above bound to

$$F|_{s^* \geq 2, v=\theta^*} \lesssim \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \epsilon^2 + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} + \epsilon^{2s^*-1} = \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \cdot \left( 1 + \frac{\epsilon^2}{\epsilon_1} + \left( \frac{\epsilon^2}{\epsilon_1} \right)^{s^*-1} \cdot \epsilon \right).$$

For v ⊥ θ ⋆ , we have that

$$\begin{aligned} F|_{s^* \geq 2, v \perp \theta^*} &\lesssim \epsilon_1^{s^*-2} \epsilon_0^2 + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} + \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-2} + \mathbf{1}(s^* \geq 4) \cdot \epsilon_0^2 \cdot \epsilon^{2s^*-3} + \epsilon^{2s^*-1} \\ &\lesssim \epsilon_1^{s^*-1} \cdot \left( 1 + \frac{\epsilon^2}{\epsilon_1} + \left( \frac{\epsilon^2}{\epsilon_1} \right)^{s^*-1} \cdot \epsilon + \left( \frac{\epsilon^2}{\epsilon_1} \right)^{s^*-2} \cdot \frac{\epsilon_0^2}{\epsilon_1} \cdot (\epsilon^2 + \epsilon \cdot \mathbf{1}(s^* \geq 4)) \right). \end{aligned}$$

Where for s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1, we have F|<sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup>=1 ≲ 1. Hence, we complete the proof.

Lemma H.4. *For polarization level* γ = o(1)*, take the polarized random vector*

$$w = \frac{\gamma e_1 + \xi}{\|\gamma e_1 + \xi\|_2}, \quad \text{where} \quad \xi \sim \text{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}),$$

*where* e<sup>1</sup> = (1, 0, . . . , 0)<sup>⊤</sup> *is the first standard basis vector in* <sup>R</sup> d *. Let* θ <sup>⋆</sup> = (ρ, p 1 − ρ <sup>2</sup>, 0, . . . , 0) ∈ <sup>d</sup>−<sup>1</sup> *be a fixed direction. Then, we have that*

$$\mathbb{E}[(\theta^*, w)^s] \simeq \begin{cases} \left(|\rho|(\gamma + d^{-1/2}) + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2 d^{-1/2}}\right)^s & \text{if } s \text{ is even} \\ \rho\gamma \left(|\rho|(\gamma + d^{-1/2}) + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2 d^{-1/2}}\right)^{s-1} & \text{if } s \text{ is odd.} \end{cases}$$

*Proof of [Lemma H.4.](#page-70-0)* For w, we have by [Lemma I.9](#page-78-1) that the first moment is given by

$$\mathbb{E}[w] = C(e_1, \gamma) \cdot \gamma \cdot e_1 \simeq \gamma e_1,$$

and the second moment is controlled by

$$\mathbb{E}[ww^\top] = \begin{bmatrix} C(2e_1, \gamma) \cdot (\gamma + d^{-1/2})^2 & 0 & \cdots & 0 \\ 0 & & & \\ \vdots & C(2e_2, \gamma)d^{-1} \cdot I_{d-1} & & \\ 0 & & & \end{bmatrix} \sim \begin{bmatrix} (\gamma + d^{-1/2})^2 & 0 & \cdots & 0 \\ 0 & & & \\ \vdots & & & \\ 0 & & & \end{bmatrix}.$$

$$\mathbb{E} [\langle \theta^*, w \rangle^s \cdot w_\tau] = \mathbb{E} \left[ (\rho w_1 + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2} w_2)^s w_\tau \right].$$

Note that if τ ̸= 1, 2, then the expectation is zero. For more generality, let us take r1, r<sup>2</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup>. We study the following expectation:

$$\begin{aligned} & \mathbb{E} [\langle \theta^*, w \rangle^s \cdot w_1^{r_1} w_2^{r_2}] \\ &= \sum_{j=0}^s \binom{s}{j} \rho^j \sqrt{1 - \rho^{2^{s-j}}} \mathbb{E} \left[ w_1^{j+r_1} w_2^{s-j+r_2} \right] \cdot \mathbf{1}(s - j + r_2 \text{ even}) \\ &= \begin{cases} \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s/2 \rfloor} \binom{s}{2j} \rho^{2j} \sqrt{1 - \rho^{2^{s-2j}}} \mathbb{E} \left[ w_1^{2j+r_1} w_2^{s-2j+r_2} \right] & \text{if } s + r_2 \text{ is even,} \\ \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor (s-1)/2 \rfloor} \binom{s}{2j+1} \rho^{2j+1} \sqrt{1 - \rho^{2^{s-2j-1}}} \mathbb{E} \left[ w_1^{2j+1+r_1} w_2^{s-2j-1+r_2} \right] & \text{if } s + r_2 \text{ is odd.} \end{cases} \end{aligned}$$

Here, the first equality holds by noting that each term in the sum is zero if the degree on w<sup>2</sup> is odd due to the symmetry in the distribution of w2. Next, we invoke [Lemma I.9](#page-78-1) for the moment as

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E} [\langle \theta^\star, w \rangle^s \cdot w_1^{r_1} w_2^{r_2}] \\ \simeq \begin{cases} \text{if } s + r_2 \text{ is even:} \\ \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s/2 \rfloor} \binom{s}{2j} \rho^{2j} \sqrt{1 - \rho^{2^{s-2j}} \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(r_1 \text{ odd})}} \left( \gamma + d^{-1/2} \right)^{2j+2\lfloor r_1/2 \rfloor} \left( d^{-1/2} \right)^{s-2j+r_2} \\ \text{if } s + r_2 \text{ is odd:} \\ \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor (s-1)/2 \rfloor} \binom{s}{2j+1} \rho^{2j+1} \sqrt{1 - \rho^{2^{s-2j-1}} \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(r_1 \text{ even})}} \left( \gamma + d^{-1/2} \right)^{2j+2\lfloor \frac{r_1+1}{2} \rfloor} \left( d^{-1/2} \right)^{s-2j-1+r_2} \end{cases} \\ \simeq \begin{cases} \text{if } s + r_2 \text{ is even:} \\ \sqrt{1 - \rho^{2^{\mathbb{1}(s \text{ odd})}} \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(r_1 \text{ odd})}} \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{2\lfloor \frac{r_1}{2} \rfloor} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{r_2+\mathbb{1}(s \text{ odd})} \left( |\rho|(\gamma + d^{-1/2}) + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2} d^{-1/2} \right)^{2\lfloor \frac{s}{2} \rfloor} \\ \text{if } s + r_2 \text{ is odd:} \\ \rho \sqrt{1 - \rho^{2^{\mathbb{1}(s \text{ even})}} \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(r_1 \text{ even})}} \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{2\lfloor \frac{r_1+1}{2} \rfloor} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{r_2+\mathbb{1}(s \text{ even})} \left( |\rho|(\gamma + d^{-1/2}) + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2} d^{-1/2} \right)^{2\lfloor \frac{s-1}{2} \rfloor} \end{cases} \end{aligned}$$

Here, the symbol ≃ conceals some constant factors that are governed by upper and lower bounds dependent on s only. Using the above calculation, we have We can specialize the above results to the case r<sup>1</sup> = r<sup>2</sup> = 0 and obtain

$$\mathbb{E}[\langle \theta^*, w \rangle^s] \simeq \begin{cases} \left( |\rho|(\gamma + d^{-1/2}) + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2} d^{-1/2} \right)^s & \text{if } s \text{ is even} \\ \rho \gamma \left( |\rho|(\gamma + d^{-1/2}) + \sqrt{1 - \rho^2} d^{-1/2} \right)^{s-1} & \text{if } s \text{ is odd}, \end{cases}$$

which completes the proof.

## I TE C H N I C A L RE S U LT S

## I . 1 TE C H N I C A L RE S U LT S F O R H E R M I T E TE N S O R

Proposition I.1. *Let* s ∈ <sup>N</sup>0*. For any* z ∈ <sup>R</sup> d *, we have*

$$z_i z_j \mathbf{h}_s(z) = \text{Sym}\left(\sqrt{(s+2)(s+1)} \cdot \mathbf{h}_{s+2}(z)[e_i \otimes e_j] + \delta_{ij} \mathbf{h}_s(z) + s \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)[e_j] \otimes e_i + s \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)[e_i] \otimes e_j + \sqrt{s(s-1)} \cdot \mathbf{h}_{s-2}(z) \otimes e_i \otimes e_j\right),$$

*where we define* h−1(z) *and* h−2(z) *to be all zero tensors of any conformable shape.*

*Proof of [Proposition I.1.](#page-71-2)* Note that each element of hs(θ ⋆⊤ z)zz<sup>⊤</sup> must lie in the polynomial space with degree at most s + 2, i.e., <sup>R</sup>s+2[z]. We take a test function F : <sup>R</sup> <sup>d</sup> → <sup>R</sup> such that R ∈ <sup>R</sup>s+2[z]. Thus, we can write down the inner product of F and hs(θ ⋆⊤ z)ziz<sup>j</sup> for i, j ∈ [d] as

$$\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[F(z)h_s(\theta^{\star\top}z)z_iz_j] = \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[F(z)z_iz_j \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)[(\theta^{\star})^{\otimes s}]],$$

where in the equation, we use [\(B.4\)](#page-17-5) to rewrite the Hermite polynomial in terms of the Hermite tensor. It suffices to understand the tensor F(z)zizjhs(z). Note that F is differentiable to any order, and the tensor obtained by differentiating F to any order is square-integrable with respect to the standard normal distribution. By the Stein's lemma for Hermite tensor [\(B.5\)](#page-17-6), we have

$$\begin{aligned} \sqrt{s!} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[F(z) z_i z_j \mathbf{h}_s(z)] &= \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s (F(z) z_i z_j)] \\ &= \text{Sym} \left( \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z) z_i z_j] + s \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^{s-1} F(z) \nabla(z_i z_j)] + \frac{s(s-1)}{2} \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^{s-2} F(z) \nabla^2(z_i z_j)] \right) \\ &= \text{Sym}(\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z) \delta_{ij}] + \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^{s+2} F(z) [e_i \otimes e_j]] + s \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z) [e_j] \otimes e_i] \\ &\quad + s \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z) [e_i] \otimes e_j] + s(s-1) \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^{s-2} F(z) \otimes e_i \otimes e_j]). \end{aligned} \tag{1.1}$$

Here, the "Sym" operation symmetrizes the tensor in the parentheses. The last equality holds by the following calculations. For <sup>E</sup>z∼N<sup>d</sup> [∇<sup>s</sup>F(z)ziz<sup>j</sup> ], we have

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z)z_i z_j] &= \text{Sym}(\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z)\delta_{ij}] + \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^{s+1} F(z)[e_i \otimes z_i]) \\ &= \text{Sym}(\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z)\delta_{ij}] + \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^{s+2} F(z)[e_i \otimes e_j]])\end{aligned}$$

,

where we use the Stein's lemma for both equalities. For <sup>E</sup>z∼N<sup>d</sup> [∇<sup>s</sup>−<sup>2</sup>F(z)∇<sup>2</sup> (ziz<sup>j</sup> )], we have

$$\begin{aligned} \text{where we use the Stein's lemma for both equalities. For } \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^{s-2}F(z)\nabla^2(z_i z_j)], \text{ we have } \\ \text{Sym } (\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^{s-1}F(z)\nabla(z_i z_j)]) = \text{Sym } (\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[z_j \nabla^{s-1}F(z) \otimes e_i + z_i \nabla^{s-1}F(z) \otimes e_j]) \\ = \text{Sym } (\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z)[e_j] \otimes e_i] + \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\nabla^s F(z)[e_i] \otimes e_j]). \end{aligned}$$

Now, for each derivative of F in [\(I.1\)](#page-72-0), we have by the Stein's lemma stated in [\(B.5\)](#page-17-6) that <sup>E</sup>z∼N<sup>d</sup> [∇<sup>s</sup>F(z)] = √ s! · <sup>E</sup>z∼N<sup>d</sup> [F(z)hs(z)], which gives us

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[F(z)z_i z_j \mathbf{h}_s(z)] &= \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}\left[F(z) \cdot \text{Sym}(\delta_{ij} \mathbf{h}_s(z) + \sqrt{(s+2)(s+1)} \cdot \mathbf{h}_{s+2}(z)[e_i \otimes e_j] \right. \\ &\quad \left. + s \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)[e_j] \otimes e_i + s \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)[e_i] \otimes e_j + \sqrt{s(s-1)} \cdot \mathbf{h}_{s-2}(z) \otimes e_i \otimes e_j \right]. \end{aligned}$$

Since F ∈ <sup>R</sup>s+2[z] is arbitrary, we conclude that

$$z_i z_j \mathbf{h}_s(z) = \text{Sym}\left(\sqrt{(s+2)(s+1)} \cdot \mathbf{h}_{s+2}(z)[e_i \otimes e_j] + \delta_{ij} \mathbf{h}_s(z) + s \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)[e_j] \otimes e_i + s \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)[e_i] \otimes e_j + \sqrt{s(s-1)} \cdot \mathbf{h}_{s-2}(z) \otimes e_i \otimes e_j\right).$$

The proof is completed by further taking the tensor inner product operation with respect to (θ ⋆ ) <sup>⊗</sup><sup>s</sup> on both side.

Proposition I.2. *Let* w, w′ ∈ <sup>S</sup> <sup>d</sup>−<sup>1</sup> *and* s ∈ <sup>N</sup>0*. We have*

$$\begin{aligned} & \mathbb{E}[h_i(\langle w, z \rangle) h_j(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)] \\ &= \sum_{\tau=0}^s \mathbf{1}(j = i + s - 2\tau, i \geq \tau) \cdot \binom{s}{\tau} \sqrt{\frac{i! j!}{s!((i - \tau)!)^2}} \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{i - \tau} \cdot \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes \tau} \otimes w'^{\otimes s - \tau}). \end{aligned}$$

*The above term is equal to*

$$\begin{pmatrix} s \\ (i-j+s)/2 \end{pmatrix} \cdot \sqrt{\frac{i!j!}{s!}} \cdot \frac{\langle w, w' \rangle^{(i+j-s)/2}}{((i+j-s)/2)!} \cdot \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes (i-j+s)/2} \otimes w'^{\otimes (j-i+s)/2}).$$

*if* |i − j| ≤ s ≤ i + j *and* s ≡ i − j mod 2*. Otherwise the expectation gives the zero tensor.*

*Proof of [Proposition I.2.](#page-72-1)* By the Stein's lemma for the Hermite tensor [\(B.5\)](#page-17-6), we have

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}[h_i(\langle w, z \rangle) h_j(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)] &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{s!}} \cdot \mathbb{E}[\nabla^s(h_i(\langle w, z \rangle) h_j(\langle w', z \rangle))] \\ &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{s!}} \cdot \sum_{\tau=0}^s \binom{s}{\tau} \mathbb{E}[\text{Sym}(\nabla^\tau h_i(\langle w, z \rangle) \otimes \nabla^{s-\tau} h_j(\langle w', z \rangle))] \\ &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{s!}} \cdot \sum_{\tau=0}^s \binom{s}{\tau} \sqrt{\frac{i! j!}{(i-\tau)!(j-s+\tau)!}} \mathbb{E}[h_{i-\tau}(\langle w, z \rangle) \otimes h_{j-s+\tau}(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes \tau} \otimes w'^{\otimes s-\tau})] \\ &= \sum_{\tau=0}^s \mathbb{1}(j = i + s - 2\tau, i \geq \tau) \cdot \frac{\binom{s}{\tau}}{(i-\tau)!} \sqrt{\frac{i! j!}{s!}} \langle w, w' \rangle^{i-\tau} \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes \tau} \otimes w'^{\otimes s-\tau}). \end{aligned}$$

the condition can be translated into |i − j| ≤ s ≤ i + j and s ≡ i − j mod 2. Then, we can take τ = (i − j + s)/2, s − τ = (j − i + s)/2, i − τ = (i + j − s)/2 to obtain the desired result.

Lemma I.3. *Let* ψ : R → R *such that* ψ <sup>2</sup> ∈ L (N )*. Suppose that* ψ *is high-pass in the sense that* <sup>ψ</sup>p<sup>i</sup> = 0 *for any* i < s<sup>⋆</sup> − <sup>1</sup> *for some* <sup>s</sup> <sup>⋆</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup>0*. For* w, w′ ∈ <sup>S</sup> d−1 *, take a series of test tensor* {T<sup>s</sup> = v<sup>1</sup> ⊗ v<sup>2</sup> ⊗ . . . ⊗ v<sup>s</sup> ∈ (<sup>R</sup> d ) <sup>⊗</sup><sup>s</sup>}<sup>∞</sup> <sup>s</sup>=0 *such that* supi>c<sup>0</sup> {|⟨w, vi⟩| ∨ |⟨w ′ , vi⟩|} ≤ ϵ *for some* ϵ ∈ (0, 1/2) *and integer* c<sup>0</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup>0*. Let*

$$\epsilon_0 := \max_{1 \leq i \leq c_0} \{|\langle w, v_i \rangle| \vee |\langle w', v_i \rangle|\}.$$

*Suppose that* |⟨w, w′ ⟩| ≤ ϵ1*. Then we have for any* s ∈ <sup>N</sup><sup>0</sup> *that*

<sup>E</sup>z∼N<sup>d</sup> ψ(⟨w, z⟩)ψ(⟨w ′ , z⟩) · hs(z)[Ts]   ≤ 4∥ψ∥ 2 (4es<sup>⋆</sup> ) s/<sup>2</sup> √ s + s <sup>⋆</sup> · ϵ (s <sup>⋆</sup>−1−⌊s/2⌋)∨0 · ϵ s∧c<sup>0</sup> · ϵ (s−c0)∨0 , *where* ∥ψ∥ 2 <sup>2</sup> = <sup>E</sup>x∼N [ψ 2 (x)]*.*

$$\left| \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\psi(\langle w, z \rangle) \psi(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z) [T_s]] \right| \leq 4 \|\psi\|_2^2 (4es^*)^{s/2} \sqrt{s+s^*} \cdot \epsilon_1^{(s^*-1-\lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(s-c_0) \vee 0},$$
where  $\|\psi\|_2^2 = \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} [\psi^2(x)]$ .

*Proof of [Lemma I.3.](#page-73-0)* As ψ(x)ψ(x ′ ) is also square-integrable, we are able to extract the s-th tensor coefficient of the Hermite expansion of ψ(x)ψ(x ′ ) as

$$\begin{aligned} & \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\psi(\langle w, z \rangle) \psi(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z)] \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} \left[ \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} \sum_{j=0}^{\infty} \widehat{\psi}_i \widehat{\psi}_j h_i(\langle w, z \rangle) h_j(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z) \right] \\ &= \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} \sum_{j=0}^{\infty} \sum_{\tau=0}^s \mathbb{1}(j = i + s - 2\tau, i \geq \tau) \cdot \binom{s}{\tau} \sqrt{\frac{i! j!}{s!((i - \tau)!)^2}} \widehat{\psi}_i \widehat{\psi}_j \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{i-\tau} \cdot \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes \tau} \otimes w'^{\otimes s-\tau}) \\ &= \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} \sum_{\tau=0}^s \mathbb{1}(i \geq \tau) \binom{s}{\tau} \sqrt{\frac{1}{s!}} \sqrt{\frac{i!(i + s - 2\tau)!}{((i - \tau)!)^2}} \widehat{\psi}_i \widehat{\psi}_{i+s-2\tau} \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{i-\tau} \cdot \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes \tau} \otimes w'^{\otimes (s-\tau)}), \end{aligned}$$

where the last second identity follows from [Proposition I.2,](#page-72-1) and in the last line we restrict the condition j = i + s − 2τ . Note that the double sums are interchangeable only if the series converges for each τ . However, by our condition that ⟨w, w′ ⟩ ≤ 1 − ϵ for some ϵ > 0, then for each τ , we have for any test tensor T = v<sup>1</sup> ⊗ v<sup>2</sup> ⊗ . . . ⊗ v<sup>s</sup> with ∥vi∥<sup>2</sup> = 1 that

$$\begin{aligned} & \left| \sum_{i=\tau}^{\infty} \sqrt{\frac{i!(i+s-2\tau)!}{((i-\tau)!)^2}} \hat{\psi}_i \hat{\psi}_{i+s-2\tau} \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{i-\tau} \cdot \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes \tau} \otimes w'^{\otimes(s-\tau)}) [T] \right| \\ & \leq \sum_{i=\tau}^{\infty} (i+s)^{s/2} \cdot \left| \hat{\psi}_i \hat{\psi}_{i+s-2\tau} \right| \cdot (1-\epsilon)^{i-\tau} < \infty, \end{aligned}$$

where we note that 1 − ϵ will dominate the polynomial growth of (i + s) s/2 , and also using the fact that <sup>ψ</sup>piψpi+s−2<sup>τ</sup> is uniformly bounded by P<sup>∞</sup> <sup>i</sup>=0 <sup>ψ</sup>p<sup>2</sup> <sup>i</sup> < ∞. Now, we interchange the double sum and apply the high-pass assumption and have that

$$\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\psi(\langle w, z \rangle) \psi(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z) [T]] \\ &= \sum_{\tau=0}^s \binom{s}{\tau} \sqrt{\frac{1}{s!}} \underbrace{\sum_{i=i_0}^{\infty} \sqrt{\frac{i!(i+s-2\tau)!}{((i-\tau)!)^2}} \hat{\psi}_i \hat{\psi}_{i+s-2\tau} \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{i-\tau} \cdot \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes \tau} \otimes w'^{\otimes (s-\tau)})} [T]. \end{aligned}$$

where we define

$$i_0 = \max \{(s^* - 1), (s^* - 1 + 2\tau - s), \tau\}$$

Note that the tensor product part is independent of i. Hence, we pull out term (I) and have

$$\begin{aligned} (\mathbf{I}) &= \sqrt{\frac{i_0!(i_0 + s - 2\tau)!}{((i_0 - \tau)!)^2}} \hat{\psi}_{i_0} \hat{\psi}_{i_0 + s - 2\tau} \langle w, w' \rangle^{i_0 - \tau} \\ &\pm \|\psi\|_2^2 \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{i_0 - \tau + 1} \left( \sum_{j=0}^{\infty} (j + s + s^*)(j + s + s^* - 1) \cdots (j + s^* + \lceil(s + 1)/2\rceil) |\langle w, w' \rangle|^j \right). \end{aligned}$$

Here, the first term is given by splitting out the term with i = i<sup>0</sup> from the summation, and the second term is for i ≥ i<sup>0</sup> + 1. For the second term, we have the following argument:

$$\begin{aligned} \max\{i_0, i_0 + s - 2\tau\} &= (s^* - 1) \vee (s^* - 1 + 2\tau - s) \vee \tau \vee (s^* - 1 + s - 2\tau) \vee (s^* - 1) \vee (s - \tau) \\ &= (s^* - 1 + 2\tau - s) \vee (s^* - 1 + s - 2\tau) \vee \tau \vee (s - \tau) \\ &= (s^* - 1 + |2\tau - s|) \vee (|\tau - s/2| + s/2) \leq s^* + s - 1. \end{aligned}$$

Hence, we then have that for any i ≥ i<sup>0</sup> + 1 with j = i − (i<sup>0</sup> + 1) that

max{i, i + s − 2τ} ≤ s

<sup>⋆</sup> + s + j.

Therefore, for any i ≥ i<sup>0</sup> + 1, we have

$$\begin{aligned} \sqrt{\frac{i!(i+s-2\tau)!}{((i-\tau)!)^2}} &= \sqrt{i(i-1)\cdots(i-\tau+1)} \cdot \sqrt{(i+s-2\tau)(i+s-2\tau-1)\cdots(i-\tau+1)} \\ &\leq \sqrt{(j+s^*+s)(j+s^*+s-1)\cdots(j+s^*+1)} \\ &\leq (j+s^*+s)(j+s^*+s-1)\cdots(j+s^*+\lceil(s+1)/2\rceil). \end{aligned}$$

To characterize the second term, we use [Proposition J.5](#page-82-2) where we have conditions |⟨w, w′ ⟩| < 1/2 and s <sup>⋆</sup> + s ≥ 2⌊(s + 1)/2⌋ − 1 satisfied, which gives us

$$\begin{aligned} & \sum_{j=0}^{\infty} (j + s + s^*)(j + s + s^* - 1) \cdots (j + s^* + \lceil (s + 1)/2 \rceil) |\langle w, w' \rangle| \\ & \leq 2(s + s^*) \cdots (s^* + \lceil (s + 1)/2 \rceil) \cdot \frac{1}{1 - |\langle w, w' \rangle|} \\ & \leq 4(s + s^*) \cdots (s^* + \lceil (s + 1)/2 \rceil). \end{aligned}$$

Combining these results, we have for (I) that

$$\begin{aligned} (\mathbf{I}) &= \sqrt{\frac{i_0!(i_0 + s - 2\tau)!}{((i_0 - \tau)!)^2}} \hat{\psi}_{i_0} \hat{\psi}_{i_0 + s - 2\tau} \langle w, w' \rangle^{i_0 - \tau} \pm \|\psi\|_2^2 \cdot 4(s + s^*) \cdots (s^* + \lceil (s + 1)/2 \rceil) \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{i_0 - \tau + 1} \\ &= C(s^*, s, \tau, \langle w, w' \rangle) \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{i_0 - \tau}, \end{aligned}$$

where we define C(s ⋆ , s, τ,⟨w, w′ ⟩) = (I)/⟨w, w′ ⟩ i0−τ as the coefficient, which is given by

$$C(s^*, s, \tau, \langle w, w' \rangle) = \sqrt{\frac{i_0!(i_0 + s - 2\tau)!}{((i_0 - \tau)!)^2}} \hat{\psi}_{i_0} \hat{\psi}_{i_0 + s - 2\tau} \pm \|\psi\|_2^2 \cdot 4(s + s^*) \cdots (s^* + \lceil (s + 1)/2 \rceil) \cdot |\langle w, w' \rangle|,$$

and also enjoys the following upper bound

$$|C(s^*, s, \tau, \langle w, w' \rangle)| \leq \|\psi\|_2^2 \cdot 4(s + s^* - 1) \cdots (s^* - 1 + \lceil (s + 1)/2 \rceil). \quad (1.2)$$

Here, the upper bound can be obtained by noting that the previous upper bound for terms i ≥ i<sup>0</sup> + 1 can be also applied to i ≥ i0.

Case s ≥ 1. Let us plug in test tensor T<sup>s</sup> into the expression, which gives us

$$\begin{aligned} & |\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\psi(\langle w, z \rangle) \psi(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z) [T_s]]| \\ & \leq \sum_{\tau=0}^s \binom{s}{\tau} \sqrt{\frac{1}{s!}} |C(s^\star, s, \tau, \langle w, w' \rangle)| \cdot |\langle w, w' \rangle|^{i_0 - \tau} \cdot \left| \text{Sym}(w^{\otimes \tau} \otimes w'^{\otimes (s - \tau)}) [T_s] \right| \\ & \leq \sum_{\tau=0}^s \binom{s}{\tau} \sqrt{\frac{1}{s!}} |C(s^\star, s, \tau, \langle w, w' \rangle)| \cdot |\langle w, w' \rangle|^{i_0 - \tau} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{s!} \sum_{\pi \in \Pi_s} \prod_{i=1}^{\tau} |\langle w, v_{\pi(i)} \rangle| \prod_{j=\tau+1}^s |\langle w', v_{\pi(j)} \rangle| \right), \end{aligned}$$

where Π<sup>s</sup> denotes the set of all permutations of s elements. We further have this term bounded by

$$\begin{aligned} & |\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\psi(\langle w, z \rangle) \psi(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z) [T_s]]| \\ & \leq \sum_{\tau=0}^s \binom{s}{\tau} \sqrt{\frac{1}{s!} \max_{0 \leq \tau \leq s} |C(s^*, s, \tau, \langle w, w' \rangle)| \cdot |\langle w, w' \rangle|^{i_0-\tau} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(s-c_0) \vee 0}} \\ & \leq \frac{2^s}{\sqrt{s!}} \max_{0 \leq \tau \leq s} |C(s^*, s, \tau, \langle w, w' \rangle)| \cdot |\langle w, w' \rangle|^{(s^* - 1 - \lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(s-c_0) \vee 0}. \end{aligned} \quad (1.3)$$

where the last inequality follows from the following fact

$$\begin{aligned} i_0 - \tau &= (s^* - 1 - \tau) \vee (s^* - 1 + \tau - s) \vee 0 = (s^* - 1 - s/2 + |\tau - s/2|) \vee 0 \\ &\geq (s^* - 1 - s/2 + \mathbf{1}(s \text{ odd})/2) \vee 0 = (s^* - 1 - \lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0. \end{aligned}$$

Plugging [\(I.2\)](#page-74-0) into [\(I.3\)](#page-74-1), and by noting that |⟨w, vi⟩| ∨ |⟨w ′ , vi⟩| ≤ ϵ for any v<sup>i</sup> ∈ {θ ⋆ , v} and |⟨w, w′ ⟩| ≤ ϵ, we have that

$$\begin{aligned}
& |\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\psi(\langle w, z \rangle) \psi(\langle w', z \rangle) \cdot \mathbf{h}_s(z) [T_s]]| \\
& \leq \frac{2^{s^*}}{\sqrt{s!}} \|\psi\|_2^2 \cdot 4(s + s^* - 1) \cdots (s^* - 1 + \lceil (s + 1)/2 \rceil) \cdot \epsilon_1^{(s^* - 1 - \lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(s - c_0) \vee 0} \\
& \leq \left( \frac{4e(s + s^* - 1)}{s} \right)^{s/2} \cdot \sqrt{s + s^* - 1} \cdot 4 \|\psi\|_2^2 \cdot \epsilon_1^{(s^* - 1 - \lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(s - c_0) \vee 0} \\
& \leq (4es^*)^{s/2} \cdot \sqrt{s + s^*} \cdot 4 \|\psi\|_2^2 \cdot \epsilon_1^{(s^* - 1 - \lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(s - c_0) \vee 0}.
\end{aligned}$$

Here, the second inequality follows from the Stirling's approximation, and the last inequality holds because s ≥ 1.

Case s = 0. For the case s = 0, we have that

$$|\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d}[\psi(\langle w, z \rangle)\psi(\langle w', z \rangle)]| = \left| C(s^*, 0, 0, \langle w, w' \rangle) \cdot \langle w, w' \rangle^{s^*-1} \right| \leq 4\|\psi\|_2^2 \epsilon_1^{s^*-1},$$

which can also be upper bounded by the quantity derived for the case s ≥ 1. Hence, the proof is completed.

Proposition I.4. *Let* ψ(·, ·) 2 *satisfies the quadratic integrability condition and high-pass condition in [Assumption 4.1](#page-6-2) with* s <sup>⋆</sup> *being the generative exponent. Suppose the remaining definitions (*c0*,* {Ts}<sup>∞</sup> <sup>s</sup>=0*,* ϵ, ϵ0, ϵ1*) and conditions are the same as in [Lemma I.3.](#page-73-0) Suppose* c<sup>0</sup> ∈ {0, 1, 2} *and* s<sup>0</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup>0*. Take function series* {ζs(·)}<sup>∞</sup> <sup>s</sup>=0 *satisfying* <sup>E</sup>Q[ζs(y) ] < C, ∀s ∈ <sup>N</sup><sup>0</sup> *for some universal* C = O(1)*. Suppose* 4eϵ<sup>2</sup> < 1/2*. If* s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1*, then we have that*

$$\sum_{s \geq s_0} (s+2) \cdot |\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\zeta_s(y)\psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle)\psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \mathbf{h}_s(z) [T_s]]| \lesssim \epsilon_0^{s_0} + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \epsilon^{(s_0-c_0) \vee 0}.$$

*If* s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2*, then we have that*

$$\begin{aligned} & \sum_{s \geq s_0} (s + 2) \cdot |\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\zeta_s(y) \psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \mathbf{h}_s(z) [T_s]]| \\ & \lesssim \mathbf{1}(s_0 \leq c_0) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^* - 1 - \lfloor s_0/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s_0} + \epsilon_1^{s^* - 1 - \lfloor c_0/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \right) + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(2s^*) \vee s_0 - c_0} \\ & \quad + \mathbf{1}(s_0 \leq 2(s^* - 1)) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^* - 1 - \lfloor (c_0 + 1)/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{2(s^* - 1) + 1 - c_0} \right). \end{aligned}$$

*Here,* ≲ *hides constants that depend on* s0, c0, C, <sup>E</sup>Q[ψ(y, x) 4 ]*.*

*Proof of [Proposition I.4.](#page-75-0)* Let F denote the target quantity. Invoking [Lemma I.3](#page-73-0) for each degree s, we have that

$$\begin{aligned}
F &\leq \sum_{s \geq s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{y \sim \mathbb{Q}} [\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \mathcal{N}_d} [\zeta_s(y) \psi(y, \langle w, z \rangle) \psi(y, \langle w', z \rangle) \mathbf{h}_s(z) [T_s]]] \\
&\leq \sum_{s \geq s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)} \cdot \mathbb{E}_{y \sim \mathbb{Q}} [4 |\zeta_s(y)| \mathbb{E}_{x \sim \mathcal{N}} [\psi(y, x)^2]] (4es^*)^{s/2} \sqrt{s+s^*} \epsilon_1^{(s^*-1-\lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \epsilon^{(s-c_0) \vee 0} \\
&\leq \sum_{s \geq s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)} \cdot 4 \sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\zeta_s(y)^2] \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}} [\psi(y, x)^4]} (4es^*)^{s/2} \sqrt{s+s^*} \epsilon_1^{(s^*-1-\lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \epsilon^{(s-c_0) \vee 0},
\end{aligned}$$

where the last inequality follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Noting that by our assumptions, <sup>E</sup>Q[ζs(y) 2 ]<sup>E</sup>Q[ψ(y, x) 4 ] = O(1) uniformly over s, which gives us

$$F \lesssim \sum_{s \geq s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)(s+s^\star)} (4es^\star)^{s/2} \epsilon_1^{(s^\star-1-\lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \epsilon^{(s-c_0) \vee 0}.$$

For the case s <sup>⋆</sup> = 1, we have the above term controlled by

$$\begin{aligned} F \mid_{s^*=1} &\lesssim \sum_{s \geq s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)^2} (4e)^{s/2} \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \epsilon^{(s-c_0) \vee 0} \\ &\lesssim \mathbf{1}(c_0 > s_0) \cdot \sup_{s_0 \leq s \leq c_0-1} \epsilon_0^s + \sum_{s \geq c_0 \vee s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)^2} (4e)^{s/2} \epsilon_0^{c_0} \epsilon^{s-c_0} \\ &\lesssim \mathbf{1}(c_0 > s_0) \cdot \epsilon_0^{s_0} + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \epsilon^{(s_0-c_0) \vee 0} \sum_{s \geq c_0 \vee s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)^2} (4e\epsilon^2)^{(s-c_0 \vee s_0)/2} \\ &\lesssim \mathbf{1}(s_0 < c_0) \cdot \epsilon_0^{s_0} + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \epsilon^{(s_0-c_0) \vee 0} \lesssim \epsilon_0^{c_0 \wedge s_0} \epsilon^{(s_0-c_0) \vee 0}, \end{aligned}$$

where ≲ only hides constants that depend on s0, c0. The last second inequality holds by noting that 4eϵ<sup>2</sup> < 1/2.

For the case s <sup>⋆</sup> ≥ 2, we note that c<sup>0</sup> ≤ 2 ≤ 2(s <sup>⋆</sup> − 1), and we have

$$\begin{aligned}
F \mid_{s^* \geq 2} &\lesssim \sum_{s \geq s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)(s+s^*)} (4es^*)^{s/2} \epsilon_1^{(s^*-1-\lfloor s/2 \rfloor) \vee 0} \epsilon_0^{s \wedge c_0} \epsilon^{(s-c_0) \vee 0} \\
&\lesssim \mathbb{1}(s_0 \leq c_0) \cdot \max_{s_0 \leq s \leq c_0} \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor s/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^s \\
&\quad + \mathbb{1}(s_0 \leq 2(s^*-1)) \cdot \max_{c_0+1 \leq s \leq 2(s^*-1)+1} \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor s/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{s-c_0} \\
&\quad + \sum_{s \geq (2(s^*-1)+2) \vee s_0} \sqrt{s(s+1)(s+s^*)} (4es^*)^{s/2} \epsilon_0^{c_0} \epsilon^{s-c_0} \\
&\lesssim \mathbb{1}(s_0 \leq c_0) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor s_0/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{s_0} + \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor c_0/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \right) + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{(2(s^*-1)+2) \vee s_0-c_0} \\
&\quad + \mathbb{1}(s_0 \leq 2(s^*-1)) \cdot \left( \epsilon_1^{s^*-1-\lfloor (c_0+1)/2 \rfloor} \cdot \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon + \epsilon_0^{c_0} \cdot \epsilon^{2(s^*-1)+1-c_0} \right).
\end{aligned}$$

Thus, we complete the proof.

### I . 2 TE C H N I C A L RE S U LT S F O R U N I F O R M D I S T R I B U T I O N O N T H E S P H E R E

Lemma I.5 (Moment of polynomial on a sphere, adapted from [Folland](#page-11-17) [\(2001\)](#page-11-17)). *Let* ξ = (ξ1, ξ2, . . . , ξd) ∼ *Unif*(<sup>S</sup> d−1 ) *and* s1, s2, . . . , s<sup>d</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup>0*. Let* s = P<sup>d</sup> <sup>i</sup>=1 s<sup>i</sup> *. Then we have*

$$\mathbb{E}_{\xi} \left[ \prod_{i=1}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] = \left\{ \prod_{i=1}^d \frac{\Gamma((s_i + 1)/2)}{\Gamma(1/2)} \cdot \frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\Gamma((s + d)/2)} \right. \quad \text{if some } s_i \text{ is odd,} \\ \quad \left. \text{if all } s_i \text{ are even,} \right.$$

To evaluate this moment, we provide the following bound.

Fact I.6. *Take even degrees* s1, s2, . . . , s<sup>d</sup> ∈ <sup>N</sup><sup>0</sup> *and* s = P<sup>d</sup> <sup>i</sup>=1 s<sup>i</sup> *. Then we have*

$$\left(\frac{1}{d}\right)^{s/2} \leq \prod_{i=1}^d \frac{\Gamma((s_i + 1)/2)}{\Gamma(1/2)} \cdot \frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\Gamma((s + d)/2)} \leq \left(\frac{s}{d}\right)^{s/2},$$

*where we define* (0)<sup>0</sup> = 1*.*

*Proof of [Fact I.6.](#page-76-3)* Note that we can rewrite the product as

$$\prod_{i=1}^d \frac{\Gamma((s_i + 1)/2)}{\Gamma(1/2)} \cdot \frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\Gamma((s + d)/2)} = \frac{\prod_{i=1}^d (s_i - 1)!!}{(s + d - 2) \cdot (s + d - 4) \cdots d}.$$

Using the fact that a/b ≥ (a − 1)/(b − 1) for 2 ≤ a ≤ b, we can recursively apply this inequality to the factorial until it gives us the desired lower bound of (1/d) s/2 . For the upper bound, we can lower bound the denominator by d s/2 and upper bound the numerator by s s/2 . We thus have the desired result.

Note that the second moment <sup>E</sup>[ξ i ] ≍ d −1 , thus [Fact I.6](#page-76-3) can be viewed as some kind of *reverse Holder's inequality* where we use lower moment to control higher moment. Notably, the reverse inequality gives a *dimension-free* bound for the moments of the polynomial on the sphere. The following proposition formalizes this intuition.

Proposition I.7. *Suppose* ξ = (ξ1, ξ2, . . . , ξd) ∼ *Unif*(<sup>S</sup> d−1 )*. Let* f : <sup>R</sup> <sup>d</sup> → <sup>R</sup> *be a function such that* <sup>E</sup>[(f(ξ) − 1)<sup>2</sup> ] ≤ ε *. Take nonnegative degree* s = (s1, s2, . . . , sd) *with* ∥s∥<sup>1</sup> = s*, and suppose each* s<sup>i</sup> *is even for* i ∈ [d]*. We then have*

$$(1 - (2s)^{s/2}\varepsilon) \cdot \left(\frac{1}{d}\right)^{s/2} \leq \mathbb{E} \left[ f(\xi) \cdot \prod_{i=1}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] \leq (1 + 2^{s/2}\varepsilon) \cdot \left(\frac{s}{d}\right)^{s/2}.$$

*Proof of [Proposition I.7.](#page-77-0)* By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have for any j = 0, 1, . . . , s1/2,

$$\left| \mathbb{E} \left[ (f(\xi) - 1) \cdot \prod_{i=1}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] \right| \leq \sqrt{\mathbb{E}[(f(\xi) - 1)^2]} \cdot \sqrt{\mathbb{E} \left[ \prod_{i=1}^d \xi_i^{2s_i} \right]} \leq \varepsilon \cdot \left( \frac{2s}{d} \right)^{s/2},$$

where we use [Lemma I.5](#page-76-2) and the upper bound in [Fact I.6](#page-76-3) for the second inequality. Additionally, note that each s<sup>i</sup> is even, we use the same argument to have

$$\left(\frac{1}{d}\right)^{s/2} \leq \mathbb{E} \left[ \prod_{i=1}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] \leq \left(\frac{s}{d}\right)^{s/2}.$$

Combining these two inequalities, we conclude the proof.

Proposition I.8. *Let* ξ = (ξ1, ξ2, . . . , ξd) ∼ *Unif*(<sup>S</sup> d−1 )*,* γ > 0 *be a fixed parameter, and* s = (s1, s2, . . . , sd) *be a nonnegative integer vector with* ∥s∥<sup>1</sup> = s*. It then holds that*

$$\begin{aligned} G(\mathbf{s}) &:= \mathbb{E} \left[ (\xi_1 + \gamma)^{s_1} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] = \mathbb{E} \left[ \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{s_1}{2j} \gamma^{s_1-2j} \cdot \xi_1^{2j} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] \\ &= \begin{cases} 0, & \text{if some } s_i \text{ is odd for } i = 2, 3, \dots, d, \\ C(\mathbf{s}, \gamma) \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{2 \lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \cdot \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(s_1 \text{ odd})} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s-s_1}, & \text{otherwise,} \end{cases} \end{aligned}$$

*where* 1/5 ≤ C(s, γ) ≤ s s/2 *.*

*Proof of [Proposition I.8.](#page-77-1)* Note that

$$G(\mathbf{s}) = \mathbb{E} \left[ (\xi_1 + \gamma)^{s_1} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] = \mathbb{E} \left[ \sum_{j=0}^{s_1} \binom{s_1}{j} \xi_1^j \gamma^{s_1-j} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right].$$

Note that if there exists any odd degree s<sup>i</sup> for i ≥ 2, then G(s) = 0. For s2, s3, . . . , s<sup>d</sup> being even, we have

$$\begin{aligned} G(\mathbf{s}) &= \mathbb{E} \left[ \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{s_1}{2j} \gamma^{s_1-2j} \cdot \xi_1^{2j} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] \\ &= \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{s_1}{2j} \gamma^{s_1-2j} \cdot \frac{\Gamma(j+1/2)}{\Gamma(1/2)} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \frac{\Gamma((s_i+1)/2)}{\Gamma(1/2)} \cdot \frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{\Gamma((s-s_1+2j+d)/2)} \\ &= \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{s_1}{2j} \gamma^{s_1-2j} \cdot \frac{(2j-1)! \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d (s_i-1)!}{(s-s_1+2j+d-2) \cdot (s-s_1+2j+d-4) \cdots d}, \end{aligned}$$

where for the second identity, we use [Lemma I.5](#page-76-2) to compute the moments of the polynomial on the sphere. Now, we compute the lower and upper bounds of G(s).

Lower Bound. For the lower bound, we have

$$\begin{aligned} G(\mathbf{s}) &\geq \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{s_1}{2j} \gamma^{s_1-2j} \cdot d^{-j-(s_2+s_3+\cdots+s_d)/2} \\ &\geq \frac{1}{5} \cdot 5 \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor}{2j} \cdot \gamma^{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor-2j} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{2j} \cdot d^{-(s_2+s_3+\cdots+s_d)/2} \cdot \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(s_1 \text{ odd})} \\ &\geq \frac{1}{5} \cdot \sum_{j=0}^{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor}{j} \cdot \gamma^{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor-j} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^j \cdot d^{-(s_2+s_3+\cdots+s_d)/2} \cdot \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(s_1 \text{ odd})}. \end{aligned}$$

Here in the first inequality, we use the fact that a/b ≥ (a − 1)/(b − 1) for 2 ≤ a ≤ b and apply it recursively to the factorial (I) until it gives us d −j−(s2+s3+···+sd)/2 . For the second inequality, we first rearrange the terms in the summation and lower bound the binomial coefficients by changing s<sup>1</sup> to 2⌊s1/2⌋. For the last inequality, let us define

$$A_j = \binom{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor}{j} \cdot \gamma^{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor - j} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^j.$$

We invoke [Lemma J.4](#page-82-3) and have that for each odd j, we have that

$$A_j \leq 2(A_{j-1} + A_{j+1}), \quad j = 1, 3, \dots, 2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor - 1.$$

Therefore, the summation of all the odd terms is upper bounded by 4 times the summation of all the even terms, which gives us the last inequality. Therefore, the lower bound of G(s) is

$$G(\mathbf{s}) \geq \frac{1}{5} \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{2[s_1/2]} \cdot \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(s_1 \text{ odd})} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{(s-s_1)}.$$

Upper Bound. For the upper bound, we have

$$\begin{aligned} G(\mathbf{s}) &\leq \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{s_1}{2j} \gamma^{s_1-2j} \cdot d^{-j - (s_2+s_3+\cdots+s_d)/2} \cdot s^{s/2} \\ &\leq \sum_{j=0}^{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \binom{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor}{j} \cdot \gamma^{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor - j} \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^j \cdot d^{-(s_2+s_3+\cdots+s_d)/2} \cdot \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(s_1 \text{ odd})} \cdot s^{s/2} \\ &= s^{s/2} \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{2\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \cdot \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(s_1 \text{ odd})} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{-(s-s_1)}, \end{aligned}$$

where in the first line, we lower bound the denominator in the factorial (I) by d j+(s2+s3+···+sd)/2 and upper bound the numerator by s s/2 . For the second inequality, we use the nonnegativity of each terms and append the terms with j being odd to the summation.

Combining the lower and upper bounds, we have that

$$G(\mathbf{s}) = \begin{cases} 0, & \text{if some } s_i \text{ is odd for } i = 2, 3, \dots, d, \\ C(\mathbf{s}, \gamma) \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{\lfloor s_1/2 \rfloor} \cdot \gamma^{\mathbb{1}(s_1 \text{ odd})} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{-(s-s_1)}, & \text{otherwise,} \end{cases}$$

for 1/5 ≤ C(s, γ) ≤ s s/2 . Hence, the proof is complete.

## I . 3 TE C H N I C A L RE S U LT S O N P O L A R I Z E D R A N D O M V E C T O R S

Lemma I.9 (Moments of weakly polarized random vector). *Suppose* ξ = (ξ1, ξ2, . . . , ξd) ∼ *Unif*(S d−1 ) *and define the polarized vector* w *as*

$$w = \frac{\xi + \gamma e_1}{\|\xi + \gamma e_1\|_2},$$

*where* γ = o(1) > 0 *is a parameter that describes the polarization strength, and* e<sup>1</sup> = (1, 0, . . . , 0) *is the first standard basis vector. Take nonnegative integer degree* s = (s1, s2, . . . , sd) *with* ∥s∥<sup>1</sup> = <sup>s</sup> <sup>=</sup> <sup>O</sup>(1) <sup>&</sup>lt; (2√ eγ) <sup>−</sup><sup>1</sup> − 2*. Then we have*

$$\mathbb{E}_w \left[ \prod_{i=1}^d w_i^{s_i} \right] = \begin{cases} 0, & \text{if some } s_i \text{ is odd for } i = 2, 3, \dots, d, \\ C(\mathbf{s}, \gamma) \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s_1} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s-s_1}, & \text{if } s_1 \text{ is even, } s_2, \dots, s_d \text{ are even,} \\ C(\mathbf{s}, \gamma) \cdot \gamma \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s_1-1} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s-s_1}, & \text{if } s_1 \text{ is odd, } s_2, \dots, s_d \text{ are even,} \end{cases}$$

*where* 1/5 − O(γ) ≤ C(s, γ) ≤ s (s+1)/<sup>2</sup> + O(γ)*.*

*Proof of [Lemma I.9.](#page-78-1)* Let r = ∥ξ + γe1∥2. We reformulate the moment as

$$\mathbb{E} \left[ \prod_{i=1}^d w_i^{s_i} \right] = \mathbb{E} \left[ \frac{(\xi_1 + \gamma)^{s_1}}{r^s} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right].$$

By symmetry, we have the moment equal zero if some s<sup>i</sup> is odd for i = 2, 3, . . . , d. Hence, we only need to consider s2, s3, . . . , s<sup>d</sup> being even. In the following, we study the case for s<sup>1</sup> being even and odd separately.

Case 1: s<sup>1</sup> is even. We first look at the simpler case where s<sup>1</sup> is even. We note by weak polarization in the sense of ∥ξ∥<sup>2</sup> ≫ ∥γe1∥2, we can approximate 1/r<sup>s</sup> as 1 with approximation error

$$\begin{aligned} \left| \mathbb{E} \left[ \prod_{i=1}^d w_i^{s_i} \right] - \mathbb{E} \left[ (\xi_1 + \gamma)^{s_i} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] \right| &\leq \sqrt{\mathbb{E} [(1 - r^{-s})^2]} \cdot \sqrt{\mathbb{E} \left[ (\xi_1 + \gamma)^{2s_1} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{2s_i} \right]} \\ &\leq es\gamma \cdot \sqrt{\mathbb{E} \left[ (\xi_1 + \gamma)^{2s_1} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{2s_i} \right]}, \end{aligned} \quad (\text{I.4})$$

where we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the first line and for the second line, we use the fact

$$|1 - r^{-s}| \leq \frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^s} - 1 = \left(1 + \frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\right)^s - 1 \leq \exp\left(\frac{s\gamma}{1-\gamma}\right) - 1 \leq es\gamma \quad (1.5)$$

for s < (2√ eγ) −1 . Define

$$G(\mathbf{s}) := \mathbb{E} \left[ (\xi_1 + \gamma)^{s_i} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right].$$

By [Proposition I.8,](#page-77-1) we have that for even s1, s2, . . . , sd,

$$G(\mathbf{s}) = C'(\mathbf{s}, \gamma) \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s_1} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s-s_1},$$

with 1/5 ≤ C ′ (s, γ) ≤ s s/2 . Plugging the form of G(s) into [\(I.4\)](#page-79-0), we have that

$$\begin{aligned}\mathbb{E}_w \left[ \prod_{i=1}^d w_i^{s_i} \right] &= M(\mathbf{s}) \pm es\gamma \cdot \sqrt{M(\mathbf{2s})} \\ &= \left( C'(\mathbf{s}, \gamma) \pm es\gamma \sqrt{C'(2\mathbf{s}, \gamma)} \right) \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s_1} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s-s_1} \\ &= \left( C'(\mathbf{s}, \gamma) \pm es\gamma (2s)^{s/2} \right) \cdot \left( \gamma + \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s_1} \cdot \left( \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \right)^{s-s_1}.\end{aligned}$$

Case 2: s<sup>1</sup> is odd. We now consider the more complicated case where s<sup>1</sup> is odd. In the following proof, we will frequently invoke the following proposition, whose proof is deferred to [Proposition I.7](#page-77-0) of [Appendix H.](#page-66-1)

Proposition I.7 (Restated). *Suppose* ξ = (ξ1, ξ2, . . . , ξd) ∼ *Unif*(<sup>S</sup> d−1 )*. Let* f : <sup>R</sup> <sup>d</sup> → <sup>R</sup> *be a function such that* <sup>E</sup>[(f(ξ) −1)<sup>2</sup> ] ≤ ε 2 *. Take nonnegative degree* s = (s1, s2, . . . , sd) *with* ∥s∥<sup>1</sup> = s*, and suppose each* s<sup>i</sup> *is even for* i ∈ [d]*. We then have*

$$(1 - (2s)^{s/2}\varepsilon) \cdot \left(\frac{1}{d}\right)^{s/2} \leq \mathbb{E} \left[ f(\xi) \cdot \prod_{i=1}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] \leq (1 + 2^{s/2}\varepsilon) \cdot \left(\frac{s}{d}\right)^{s/2}.$$

We first rewrite the moment as

$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E} \left[ \prod_{i=1}^d w_i^{s_i} \right] &= \mathbb{E} \left[ \frac{(\xi_1 + \gamma)^{s_1}}{r^s} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] & (I) \text{ even terms} \\ &= \underbrace{\sum_{j=0}^{(s_1-1)/2} \binom{s_1}{2j} \gamma^{s_1-2j}}_{(I) \text{ even terms}} \cdot \mathbb{E} \left[ \frac{\xi_1^{2j}}{r^s} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right] + \underbrace{\sum_{j=0}^{(s_1-1)/2} \binom{s_1}{2j+1} \gamma^{s_1-2j-1}}_{(II) \text{ odd terms}} \cdot \mathbb{E} \left[ \frac{\xi_1^{2j+1}}{r^s} \cdot \prod_{i=2}^d \xi_i^{s_i} \right]. \end{aligned} \quad (I.6)$$

Let us look at the odd terms of [\(I.6\)](#page-80-0). Let r<sup>+</sup> = ∥ξ + γe1∥<sup>2</sup> and r<sup>−</sup> = ∥−ξ + γe1∥2. By symmetry, we have for the expectation within the odd terms that
